Manifold: the complete
engineering book

A single document that teaches one system completely — high-level design, low-level design, the geometry it runs on, its infrastructure, its economics, and the judgement that produced every decision. It is written so that a competent engineer who has never seen this repository can, by the end, defend every line of it in front of a hostile technical buyer — and can then go build something else the same way.

Part 0

Orientation

What this system is, who it is for, and the mental model you need before any of the detail can land. Read these three chapters even if you skip everything else — the rest of the book assumes them.

01
Chapter 01 · Orientation

How to read this book

This is not API documentation and it is not a tutorial. It is an attempt to write down the complete reasoning behind one real system, so that reading it transfers judgement and not just facts. Facts go stale in a quarter. The reasoning — why a boundary sits here rather than there, why a measurement beat an argument, why a "small" default is load-bearing — is what still pays rent in five years.

The three readers this is written for

The new engineerweek one

You have written CRUD APIs and you know TypeScript. You have never built a retrieval system, never thought about tenancy as a correctness property, never owned a production incident.

Read it front to back. Chapter 8 (geometry) and chapter 18 (retrieval on one page) are the two hills; everything after them is easier. Do the exercises in Appendix D.

The senior engineerjoining

You want the map, the invariants, and the landmines — in that order, in an hour.

Read 03, 06, 07, 18, 30, 38, then the defect register (67) and the war stories (68). That is enough to change code safely. Come back for the rest when you touch that surface.

The founder / CTOdeciding

You need to know what is real, what it costs, what it cannot yet do, and what the next three decisions are.

Read 02, 04, 28, 36, 51, 63, 65, 70, 73. Those nine chapters are the honest diligence pack. Chapter 67 is the list you would hand a buyer before they find it themselves.

The five questions asked of every component

Every non-trivial component in this book gets the same interrogation. The format is deliberate: the fifth question is the one most documentation omits, and it is the only one that transfers judgement. A design without a stated trade-off is a design nobody has stress-tested.

What
The mechanism, stated concretely enough to reimplement. Not "handles auth" — "parses mk_<prefix>_<secret> on the first two underscores, looks up by indexed prefix, compares SHA-256 in constant time".
Why
The forcing constraint. Usually a failure that already happened, a measurement, or a property the product sells. If the answer is "it seemed cleaner", that is recorded as preference, not law (see ch. 69).
When
The moment in a request, a job, or the product's life at which this runs — and the moment at which it stops being the right answer.
How
The code path, the data touched, the failure modes, the tests that hold it in place.
Trade-off
What was given up, what the alternative would have bought, and the observable signal that would mean the decision should be revisited. A trade-off with no revisit trigger is a bias with better grammar.

Conventions used throughout

ConventionMeaning
builtExists in the repository and is covered by a passing test or a verified runtime check.
partialExists, works, and has a stated gap that would matter at the next scale step.
absentNot built. Where the book describes it, that is a design proposal, clearly marked.
0 LLMThis path spends no language-model call. The core claim of the product; marked wherever it holds.
file.ts:42A real path in this repository. Everything quoted as code is quoted from the repository, not invented.
“War story”A bug that actually happened here. Each one is included because the class of bug recurs everywhere.
Verification status of this document Everything marked measured was measured on this repository. Test counts were re-run while writing, after the durable-queue work landed: 180 unit tests + 70 Postgres-backed tests = 250, all passing; tsc --noEmit clean. (The README still says "117 + 40" — it is stale, which is itself a lesson in chapter 69: docs rot unless changing them is part of changing behaviour.) Latency figures at 100k entities come from the repository's own bench:retrieval harness; the 1M-entity target is explicitly not proven and is labelled so every time it appears.
02
Chapter 02 · Orientation

What Manifold is, and why the shape follows

Almost every architectural choice in this system falls out of one observation about cost, and one observation about trust. Get those two right and the rest of the book reads as consequence rather than invention. Get them wrong and the system looks like an eccentric RAG pipeline.

The problem, stated precisely

An AI agent working over a large body of knowledge — a research corpus, an enterprise document store, the combined API surface of a hundred connected systems — spends most of its budget orienting itself. It burns tokens finding out what exists before it can spend tokens reasoning. Worse, the conventional architectures make that cost scale with the corpus:

The naive-RAG cost curvestuff the prompt

Embed the question, take the top-k chunks, paste them in. Cheap per query, but:

  • It cannot answer multi-hop questions at all. If the answer shares no vocabulary with the question and lives two relationships away, nearest-neighbour search never surfaces it. Measured here: 0% recall on the multi-hop family.
  • Redundant context. Ten chunks that say the same thing cost ten chunks of tokens.
  • No provenance beyond "this chunk", so a claim cannot be traced to a span.
The agentic-loop cost curveretrieve → reason → retrieve

Let the model decide what to fetch next, repeatedly. It does answer multi-hop questions, but:

  • Each hop is a model call. A three-hop question is 3–7 calls, each with full context.
  • Latency is the sum of those calls — seconds to tens of seconds, which is unusable inside another agent's loop.
  • It is non-deterministic: the same question can take different paths, which makes it impossible to regression-test and impossible to bill predictably.

The central bet

The one sentence the whole system is built on Multi-hop reasoning over a corpus is a geometry problem, not a language problem. If you build the graph once, at ingest time, then "what improves something that extends X?" is a walk — one linear-algebra computation over a bounded subgraph — and the language model is only needed at the very end, to turn the retrieved evidence into a sentence a human reads.

Everything distinctive in the codebase is that bet cashed out. Where a conventional stack reaches for a model, this one reaches for a geometric or algebraic operation with the same semantics and none of the cost:

JobConventionalHereLLM cost
Entity resolution
"3DGS" = "3D Gaussian Splatting"?		 An LLM call per chunk asking the model to canonicalise mentions. One batched embedding call; match by max(cosine, normalized-name) against in-domain nodes.
knowledge-field/resolve-embed.ts		 0
Relationship validation
is this edge plausible?		 An LLM call per chunk judging each candidate edge. A type-compatibility matrix + confidence floor + dedup. A dataset cannot extends anything.
knowledge-field/validate-rules.ts		 0
Multi-hop retrieval Repeated retrieve→reason→retrieve loops. One Personalized PageRank pass over a bounded subgraph seeded by ANN.
knowledge-field/ppr.ts		 0
Ranking Pick one signal (usually cosine) and hope. Three index-backed signals fused by Reciprocal Rank Fusion on positions.
knowledge-field/fuse.ts		 0
Context assembly Top-k raw chunks, redundancy and all. Maximal Marginal Relevance over atomic propositions under a hard character budget.
knowledge-field/compress.ts		 0
Hierarchy
"what generalises X?"		 Not modelled at all. Poincaré-ball embeddings: norm ≈ generality, distance ≈ relatedness.
knowledge-field/hyperbolic.ts		 0
Broad questions Re-read N chunks every time. Cached community summaries — one call per cluster, amortised over every future query. 1, amortised
Answer generation Many calls. One verbalization call over evidence retrieval already found. 1
Precision matters more than the headline "Zero LLM calls" is a claim that must be stated exactly or it reads as a trick. Retrieval spends one embedding call to turn the question into a vector. Embeddings are two to three orders of magnitude cheaper than generation, and the system accounts for them separately (services/metrics.ts tracks embedCalls apart from llmCalls) — but the honest phrasing is "no generative model call", and a caller who already holds the vector can skip even that via RetrieveOptions.queryVector.

The second observation: isolation is the product, not a feature

A single Manifold instance hosts several isolated knowledge domains — different research fields today, different customers or verticals tomorrow. It is tempting to file that under "multi-tenancy, we'll get to it". That would be the wrong call, and the codebase reflects the right one:

Why isolation is a P0 and not a feature In a knowledge product, a leak is not a privacy incident, it is a wrong answer. If the attention node from the NLP corpus merges with the attention node from the vision corpus, the graph is now lying — and it will keep lying, confidently, with citations, forever, because nothing recorded that the merge happened. There is no "we'll clean it up later": entity resolution is lossy and irreversible. That is why unknown domains fail closed (ch. 29), why traversal checks both endpoints of every edge (ch. 30), and why an out-of-scope node returns 404 rather than 403.

The five properties the system sells

1 · Cheaptoken budget

One generative call per answer, zero for evidence-only. Ingestion is one call per chunk, and zero for structured sources. This is the number a buyer models.

2 · Citableprovenance

Every edge is written in the same transaction as a sources row naming the paper, section and text span. An edge with no source is an unfalsifiable claim, so the schema does not permit one to exist alone.

3 · Isolatedcorrectness

Domains cannot bleed. Proven by 61 database-backed tests that drive the real HTTP app against real Postgres, because the defects worth catching live in the SQL, not above it.

4 · Honestno fabricated success

A failed extraction is recorded as a failure. A paper whose chunks all failed is failed, never completed. /health returns 503 and names the misconfiguration rather than reporting ok while the pipeline cannot extract anything.

5 · Fast enough to sit inside a looplatency

153 ms p50 / 193 ms p95 for evidence-only retrieval at 100k entities. That is the difference between a component another agent can call in a loop and one it calls once, reluctantly.

And the one it does not sellhonesty

Extraction quality. The graph is only as good as the model that built it, and there is still no metric for it. Chapter 51 is the list of things this system cannot currently prove. Publishing that list is the point.

The strategic reframe (and why the framing is an engineering decision)

The repository still describes itself as "a geometric knowledge field over research papers". That undersells it twice — it ties a general capability to one literature, and it describes the pipeline rather than the value. Nothing in the core is domain-specific: resolution is cosine similarity, validation is a type matrix, retrieval is PageRank over a typed graph. The 3D Gaussian Splatting corpus is a fixture the system was developed against, not a constraint it was designed for.

The honest framing is: a token-efficient, domain-isolated knowledge substrate that lets an agent retrieve grounded, cited evidence without spending a model call. And that framing is an engineering decision, not a marketing one, because it dictates where the seams go — the connector contract (ch. 9), the domain registry (ch. 29), and the MCP surface (ch. 39) all exist because the substrate has to be source-agnostic, tenant-isolated, and agent-callable. A "3DGS paper tool" needs none of those.

Where the framing still leaks into the code Fourteen source files still reference Gaussian Splatting, and several are load-bearing in ways a prospect would notice within a minute: /api/graph/queries/improves-3dgs is a vertical hardcoded into the public API surface, the benchmark's default questions are 3DGS-specific (so the benchmark is meaningless in any other domain), and the resolution prompt teaches "3DGS → 3D Gaussian Splatting" to every domain. Chapter 67 tracks these as open defects, because a demo-shaped API surface is technical debt with a customer-facing blast radius.
03
Chapter 03 · Orientation

The whole system on one page

One diagram, then the five things a senior engineer notices about it, then the contract each layer owes the next. If you only ever read one page of this book, read this one and chapter 06.

The whole of Manifold. Two paths (write, read), one datastore, three surfaces, one cross-cutting governance layer.

Five things a senior engineer notices immediately

1
There is exactly one stateful component. No Redis, no separate vector database, no graph database, no message broker. Postgres is the graph store, the vector index, the full-text index, the job queue itself, and the audit log. That is a deliberate anti-microservice stance and chapter 05 defends it — but notice what it buys: a single transaction can write an edge and its provenance together (invariant 7), or a batch and all its member jobs together, which a polyglot store makes impossible without a saga.
2
The expensive thing happens once, on the write path. One model call per chunk builds the graph — measured at ~34 s/chunk × ~26 chunks ≈ 15 minutes per paper on this hardware, so a 100-document batch is 20+ GPU-hours. Every subsequent query amortises against that. This is the whole economic argument, and it also names the risk: graph construction is the dominant cost at corpus scale. It is also why the queue is shaped the way it is (ch. 16): no design makes 20 GPU-hours faster, so what the architecture must buy is a run that survives restarts.
3
The read path has no unbounded operation. Seeds are LIMIT 8 off an HNSW index. Expansion is capped at maxSubgraphNodes with a per-hop fan-out cap. Candidates are capped before MMR — which matters specifically because MMR is quadratic in its candidate set. The response reports stats{seedCount, subgraphNodes, subgraphEdges, candidateEvidence, truncated} so the bound is observable, and the database tests assert on it.
4
Governance is a bar across the bottom, not a box on the side. Authentication runs on every /api route including reads. Authorization is not a step routes remember — it is fused into the helper they already had to call. That inversion (ch. 32) is the single highest-leverage security design in the codebase, because it makes the safe path the lazy path.
5
Both pipeline modes write identical output shapes. PIPELINE_MODE=field|legacy swaps the geometric resolver/validator for the original three-LLM-agent versions, and nothing downstream knows. That is what makes the A/B benchmark honest — you are comparing two implementations of one contract, not two systems.

The layer contracts

A layer boundary is only real if you can state what crossing it guarantees. These are the six that hold this system together; violating any of them is how you get a bug that looks like a mystery.

BoundaryThe contractWhat breaks if you violate it
connector → pipeline A connector emits IngestUnit[]: each unit has either text (extract it) or extraction (already a graph). If structured: true, every unit carries an extraction. A "structured" connector emitting text turns a free import into a per-operation model bill. The connector tests assert this explicitly.
pipeline → store Edge and sources row are written in one transaction, both stamped with the resolved domain id. An edge with no provenance is an unfalsifiable claim in a system whose entire value is traceability.
store → retrieval Every hot-path predicate has an index behind it, and every vector in the corpus is from one declared embedding space. Silent sequential scans (correct, unusably slow) or silent nonsense scores (fast, confidently wrong — see ch. 19).
retrieval → surfaces retrieveField() returns ranked evidence plus stats. Verbalization is opt-in and costs exactly one call. Callers that cannot get evidence without an answer cannot be used as a component by another agent — which is the primary use case.
route → domain Routes call requireDomain(c, raw, action), never resolveDomain. It resolves and authorises in one step. Every isolation defect ever found in this codebase came from a route that omitted a filter. The fix was to make omission impossible.
route → queue → handler Routes insert a row and return. queue/ claims rows and knows nothing about arXiv or extraction. jobs/ says what a claimed row does, classifying failures as transient or PermanentJobError. Work held in process memory dies with the process — fatal for a batch that runs 20+ GPU-hours. And a handler that decides its own disposition makes every transient network blip terminal.
anything → outside All outbound HTTP goes through services/http.ts: mandatory timeoutMs, streamed body under a byte cap. A bare fetch in a worker path is a permanently consumed concurrency slot. Two of them on the default config is a dead pipeline with no error anywhere.
Reading order from here The book now follows the data. Part I is the ground you stand on (drivers, technology, invariants, schema, the maths). Part II follows a document in; Part III follows a question out. Parts IV–VI are the cross-cutting concerns those two paths share. Part VII is how any of it is known to be true. Parts VIII–IX are what happens when you have to run it for someone else's money. Part X is the judgement layer — the part that is actually hard to hire for.
Part I

Foundations of the design

The ground everything else stands on: the priority order that settles arguments, every technology and the alternative it beat, the invariants that are law rather than preference, the schema, and the mathematics — taught from zero, because you cannot review what you cannot derive.

04
Chapter 04 · Foundations

Architectural drivers: how to resolve any argument

Most architecture disagreements are not disagreements about facts. They are two people optimising different quality attributes and neither saying which. A stated, ordered list of drivers converts an argument about taste into a lookup. This is Manifold's list, and the order is the whole content.

The order

1
Correctness of isolation. No path may leak, merge, or mis-scope data across domains. Beats everything, including availability. A system that refuses to answer is recoverable; a system that answered with someone else's data is not.
2
Honesty of failure. The system may fail; it may not lie about failing. A silent empty graph is worse than a loud outage, because the outage gets fixed and the empty graph gets queried.
3
Provenance. Every claim traces to a source span. An unfalsifiable claim in a knowledge product is not a feature with a caveat; it is the product not working.
4
Token efficiency at query time. The economic thesis. Sacrifice ingest cost, storage, and code complexity to protect it.
5
Query latency. Fast enough to sit inside another agent's loop — a p95 in the low hundreds of milliseconds, not seconds.
6
Operational simplicity. One stateful component. Every additional one is a new failure mode, a new backup story, and a new on-call page.
7
Ingestion throughput. Deliberately last. Ingestion is asynchronous, amortised, and retryable; the user is not waiting on it.

Watching the order do real work

Four decisions in this codebase are only explicable by that ordering. Each one looks wrong if you rank the drivers differently:

Unknown domain → 400, not a fallback1 > everything

A typo'd ?domain=nlpp returns an error naming the valid domains, rather than quietly serving the default domain. This reduces availability — some requests that used to "work" now fail.

Driver 1 beats driver 6 and beats convenience. Returning one field's graph to a caller who asked for another's is the precise failure the product exists to prevent. And on the write side it was worse: domain: "nlpp" stored the raw string on the paper while stamping every extracted node default, permanently and unfilterably contaminating a shared bucket.

A failed chunk is not a processed chunk2 > 7

chunksFailed is a separate counter, and a paper whose chunks all failed is marked failed with the reason. After LLM_UNAVAILABLE_ABORT_AFTER consecutive unreachable-model failures the whole paper is abandoned.

Driver 2 beats driver 7. Abandoning early throws away work. But grinding through forty more chunks re-discovers one outage forty times, holds a worker slot for minutes, and buries the real cause under identical errors.

Edge + source in one transaction3 > 7

Every accepted relationship costs a transaction rather than a batched insert. That is measurably slower at ingest.

Driver 3 beats driver 7. Separately, a failed sources insert left an edge with no evidence behind it. There is no repair for that: you cannot tell later which edges lost their provenance and which never had any.

Postgres for everything6 > 5

A dedicated vector database would likely beat pgvector on pure ANN latency, and a graph database would beat SQL on deep traversal.

Driver 6 beats a marginal driver 5. Two stores means two backup stories, two consistency models, and no transaction spanning an edge and its provenance. The measured p50 is 153 ms — comfortably inside the budget — so the extra latency was not worth buying.

Non-functional requirements, with honest labels

AttributeTargetStatusEvidence, or why not
Evidence-only retrieval p95< 150 ms @ 1M entitiespartial193 ms p95 measured at 100k. 1M unproven. Levers named in ch. 64.
Verbalized answer TTFT< 1.5 sunmeasuredNo streaming yet; the verbalize call is awaited whole. Ch. 41.
Domain isolationZero cross-domain readsproven61 DB-backed tests across seven domain-scoped surfaces.
AuthorizationPer-principal, per-domainenforcedApplication layer + a grep test. No RLS beneath it — ch. 36.
Durability of accepted workNo silent job losssolidThe jobs table is the queue (SKIP LOCKED claims). Queued work survives any restart; interrupted work is re-queued and retried. Proven by a kill-9-mid-extraction test. Ch. 16.
Horizontal scale of workersN instances drain one backlogreadyAny instance can claim any job in any lane, with no broker. Untested beyond two processes, but the mechanism is the standard one. Ch. 16.
AvailabilitySingle instanceno HANo load balancer, no rolling deploy, no health-gated traffic. Ch. 54.
RPO / RTOUndefinedabsentDocker volume, no automated backup, no restore drill. Ch. 61 — this is the highest-severity operational gap.
ObservabilityTraces + metricsabsentconsole.log and an in-process metrics singleton. Ch. 60.
Answer groundednessMeasured rateabsentRetrieval quality is measured; whether the generated sentence is supported by it is not. Ch. 51.

What this design explicitly does not support

Naming non-goals is not modesty; it is how you stop a roadmap from being negotiated one exception at a time.

  • Real-time ingestion. Ingest is minutes of model calls, asynchronous by construction. There is no "index this document and query it in the same second".
  • Cross-domain entities. A concept studied in two fields is two nodes, deliberately. A bridge concept would reintroduce exactly the contamination the isolation model exists to prevent, and it needs a design (a shared namespace, or domains text[]) before it needs code.
  • Conflict resolution. When two sources disagree, both become edges with confidence scores. For a system meant to prevent errors, silently averaging contradictions is the wrong behaviour, and it is a known open problem (ch. 67).
  • Temporal / "as of" queries. The graph is a current-state snapshot. Nothing records that an edge was true in 2023 and superseded in 2024.
  • Arbitrary SQL / Cypher for callers. The API surface is deliberately narrow. Handing out query languages hands out the ability to bypass the domain filter.
  • Priorities and per-tenant fairness in the queue. Strict FIFO by created_at, so an interactive single-document ingest queues behind a 100-document batch, and one tenant's batch blocks everyone. Both are known and both become urgent with the second customer (ch. 16).
05
Chapter 05 · Foundations

Every technology, and why it won

A stack is a set of bets. This chapter states each bet, the alternative it beat, and — the part that matters — the observation that would mean the bet has gone bad. A technology choice with no failure condition attached is a preference that has been promoted to a decision without an election.

Runtime and API layer

Node.js 18+ / TypeScript, ESMruntime
Why
The workload is I/O-bound almost end to end: HTTP to model providers, HTTP to arXiv, SQL to Postgres. Node's event loop is the right shape, and TypeScript gives the domain model (open entity types, discriminated errors) real teeth.
Trade-off
The pieces that are not I/O-bound — PPR iterations, MMR's quadratic cosine loop, Poincaré training — run on one thread in a garbage-collected language. Today they operate on a working set of ~1,100 nodes and cost single-digit milliseconds.
Revisit when: PPR or MMR appears in a latency profile above ~15% of p95. The fix is a worker thread or moving PPR into Postgres via a recursive CTE, not rewriting the service.
HonoHTTP framework
Why
Tiny, Web-standard Request/Response, first-class middleware, runtime-portable (Node today, an edge runtime later without a rewrite). Sub-router composition (app.route('/api/graph', graphRouter)) matches how the domain decomposes.
Beat
Express (callback-era types, no Web-standard primitives), Fastify (heavier, schema-first in a way that fights open entity types), NestJS (DI and decorators for a service with ~25 endpoints is ceremony without payoff).
Trade-off
A smaller ecosystem — there is no off-the-shelf rate limiter or auth plugin, so both are hand-written here. That turned out to be a benefit twice: the hand-written limiter is correct about X-Forwarded-For (ch. 34) in a way most defaults are not.

The data plane — the decision that actually defines this system

PostgreSQL 16 + pgvector, as the only stateful componentthe load-bearing bet

This system stores a graph, vectors, full-text, job state, and an audit log — five workloads that the industry has five specialised products for. It puts all five in one Postgres.

Why
Transactions across concern boundaries. Invariant 7 says an edge and its provenance are written together. With Neo4j for the graph and Postgres for provenance, that guarantee needs a saga, a compensating action, and a reconciliation job — three new things that can be wrong, to replace one BEGIN. Same for invariant 8's idempotent clear-and-reingest, which deletes across propositions, sources and edges atomically.
How the graph works without a graph DB
Composite indexes on (source_id, type) and (target_id, type), and traversal as one round trip per hop with WHERE source_id = ANY($frontier) OR target_id = ANY($frontier). Depth is capped at 3. This is fine precisely because the depth is bounded — the case where a graph database wins decisively is unbounded variable-length traversal, which this design never performs.
How vectors work
vector(768) columns with HNSW indexes on vector_cosine_ops. Nearest-neighbour search happens in the database, returning tens of rows — instead of pulling the corpus into Node to score it, which is what made retrieval unusable past demo scale (ch. 28).
How lexical search works
An expression GIN index on to_tsvector('english', text), queried with plainto_tsquery and ranked by ts_rank. It is not BM25, but it is a genuinely strong baseline — strong enough that measurement showed it beating the graph pipeline on direct lookups, which is why it became a fused signal rather than a benchmark arm (ch. 23).
Trade-off
One database is one blast radius, one scaling axis, and one thing to tune. pgvector's HNSW is not as fast as a purpose-built ANN engine at very high recall, and Postgres full-text is not as good as a real search engine.
Revisit when: (a) ANN alone exceeds ~40% of retrieval p95 at target corpus size, or (b) write throughput on nodes/edges saturates a single primary, or (c) a single tenant's corpus makes per-domain partitioning insufficient. The first move in every one of those cases is still Postgres-shaped (read replicas, halfvec, partitioning) — see ch. 64.

What the alternatives would actually have cost:

AlternativeWhat it buysWhat it costs here
Neo4j / MemgraphNative traversal, Cypher, path algorithms out of the box.Loses the edge+provenance transaction. Adds a second backup/restore story and a second consistency model. Cypher becomes a new surface that can bypass the domain filter.
Pinecone / Qdrant / WeaviateFaster ANN at scale, managed sharding, hybrid search built in.Vectors and their nodes now live apart, so "is this vector's node in my domain?" becomes a cross-store join done in application code — precisely the class of check that has already leaked here once.
ElasticsearchReal BM25, better analyzers, faceting.A third store, and a re-indexing pipeline that can drift from the source of truth.
Redis (queue + rate limit)Shared queue and shared rate-limit windows across instances.This one is genuinely needed at N>1 instances and is the first store to add. It is deferred, not rejected — ch. 64.
Drizzle ORMdata access
Why
A typed SQL builder, not an object-relational mapper. The query you write is the query that runs, which matters enormously in a system where index usage is the architecture. alias(nodes, 'source_nodes') lets one statement join nodes twice for source and target — a self-join the retrieval path depends on. Raw sql escapes cleanly for pgvector operators and jsonb_exists_any.
Beat
Prisma (its own query engine binary, and an abstraction that would hide exactly the index behaviour that must stay visible), TypeORM (heavier, active-record patterns), raw pg (no compile-time column typing — and this schema has enough domain-stamped columns that a missing filter must be a type-adjacent mistake, not a runtime surprise).
Trade-off
Drizzle is younger; drizzle-kit's generator does not emit CREATE EXTENSION, so migration 0000 has that line prepended by hand with a comment saying it must survive regeneration. That is a real ongoing hazard, mitigated by db/vector-check.ts failing startup if the extension is absent.
Migrations, not push drizzle-kit push diffs live schema against the models and applies whatever it infers. It is convenient during exploration and unsafe afterwards, because the plan is never reviewed, never versioned, and can drop a column it believes was renamed. Anything holding data uses pnpm db:migrate. Migration 0001 shows what a careful destructive migration looks like: it refuses to drop the JSONB embedding columns unless every row already has a populated embedding_vec, raising an exception naming the counts otherwise. A migration that can silently discard the only copy of the data is not a migration.

Model providers and external services

Ollama (default) | OpenAI, over native HTTPthe anti-SDK decision

Both providers are called with two direct fetch calls, not through a model-abstraction SDK. This is the most counter-intuitive choice in the stack and it was bought with a production failure:

War story · the version-drift outage services/llm.ts previously routed Ollama through ollama-ai-provider@1.2.0, which implements AI SDK specification v1, while the installed ai@5 only accepts v2 models. Every Ollama completion threw UnsupportedModelVersionError before a single byte hit the network — and because the callers swallowed that into an empty result, papers ingested on the documented default configuration were marked "completed" with an empty graph.

Two separate defects compounded: an abstraction with a hidden version contract, and error swallowing. Removing the abstraction removed the version-drift surface entirely; removing the swallowing is invariant #5. Direct fetch also yields real token counts from both providers (prompt_eval_count/eval_count on Ollama), which is what makes the benchmark's token columns meaningful.

Revisit when: a third provider is needed and the two fetch functions have visibly diverged in behaviour. Until then, ~120 lines beats a dependency with a compatibility matrix.

Local-first by defaultproduct decision

LLM_PROVIDER=ollama is the default so the whole system runs end to end with no API key and no cost. That is a product decision expressed in a default: evaluation friction is the largest cause of tools never being tried.

It has a sharp edge, and the code knows it: EMBED_PROVIDER defaults to openai independently of LLM_PROVIDER. So "I configured Ollama and it still wants an API key" is the single most likely setup failure — which is why checkEmbedHealth() returns that exact sentence, and /health returns 503 naming it.

There is also EMBED_PROVIDER=local: a deterministic hashed-character-n-gram embedder with no model server, no key, no network. It is opt-in only and never a fallback — "a knowledge product quietly answering from lexical hashes would be worse than refusing to answer" — but it is what makes the evaluation harness and the DB tests runnable anywhere, including CI. See ch. 47.

Everything else, briefly

TechnologyRoleWhy it won, and the sharp edge
pnpm workspacesmonorepoContent-addressed store, strict by default (no phantom dependencies), fast. --filter scoping makes every script unambiguous about which package it runs in. Sharp edge: the API's package.json pins several deps to "latest", which is a reproducibility hazard — flagged in ch. 67.
tsxdev + scriptsRuns TypeScript directly with no build step, so every harness (eval, seed:load, bench:retrieval, auth:bootstrap, mcp) is a first-class entry point rather than something requiring a compile. tsx watch is the dev server.
node:testtestingNo test framework dependency at all. Node's built-in runner via tsx. 250 tests. The trade-off is no snapshot testing, no built-in mocking, weaker watch ergonomics — accepted because the valuable tests here drive the real Hono app against real Postgres, where a mocking library would only help you test the mock.
pdf-parsetext extractionPure JS, no native binaries, no system dependency. Sharp edge: it is a text extractor, not a document-understanding model — tables, figures, and two-column math survive poorly, and the cleanup regexes in services/pdf.ts (de-hyphenation, ASCII coercion) are a blunt instrument. Extraction quality caps answer quality; ch. 51.
@modelcontextprotocol/sdkagent surfaceThe emerging standard for exposing tools to agents. stdio transport so any desktop/CLI client can launch it. Sharp edge handled well: console.log is reassigned to console.error at the top of the file, because stdout belongs to the JSON-RPC protocol and one stray library log corrupts the transport.
React 18 + ViteconsoleFast dev loop, standard ecosystem. Honest status: three pages, ~1,400 lines, 2-second polling. It shows the graph; it is not an operator console (ch. 40–41).
reactflowgraph canvasPan/zoom/selection for node-edge canvases. Layout is not delegated: lib/layout.ts is a dependency-free force simulation, deterministic (circle init, no Math.random) so the same data lays out identically across renders.
TailwindstylingColocated styles, no naming debate, no dead CSS. Fine at this size.
zodvalidationPresent as a dependency but barely used — validation is largely hand-rolled (ARXIV_ID_PATTERN, isUuid, pageParams). That inconsistency is a real smell, noted in ch. 67.
Docker Composelocal infraOne command to a correct Postgres. Two settings in it are load-bearing and were found by measurement, not by copying a template: shared_buffers=1GB and shm_size: 1gb. See ch. 28.
The meta-lesson of this chapter Three of these choices — no model SDK, no test framework, no force-layout library — are subtractions. Each one removed a dependency whose abstraction was hiding something that had to stay visible: a version contract, the real HTTP app, determinism. That is not minimalism as aesthetic. The rule is narrower and more useful: do not abstract over a boundary whose failure modes you need to see. Abstract over boring, stable, well-understood things (Postgres wire protocol, HTTP routing, JSX). Own the thin layer over anything whose breakage would be silent.
06
Chapter 06 · Foundations

The invariants: a constitution

An invariant is a property that must hold after every change, by anyone, forever. It is distinct from a convention (which you may break with a reason) and from a preference (which you may break with a shrug). Manifold keeps its invariants in CLAUDE.md at the repository root, and nearly every one is a scar — a statement of a bug that already happened, written so it cannot happen twice. That is the correct way to build this list: you do not get to add an invariant because it sounds wise.

The full list, with enforcement

#InvariantWhy it exists (the scar)How it is enforced
1Domains fail closed. resolveDomain() throws on an unregistered id; resolveStoredDomain() does the same for a DB value. The lenient getDomain() is for prompt context only — never to decide what to read, write, or filter. A silent fallback to default returned one field's graph to a caller asking for another's, and stamped entities into a shared bucket where they merged permanently. Type-level separation (three functions, three contracts) + unit tests + a grep test on src/routes/.
2A subgraph never spans domains. Traversal is pinned to the center node's domain. Filter on both endpoint nodes, not just edges.domain. A boundary-crossing edge carries one side's stamp, so an edge-only filter passes it and drags the far node — and its whole neighbourhood — in. A test caught exactly this. DB test: "subgraph traversal refuses to follow a cross-domain edge", applied to /graph/subgraph, /graph/nodes/:id, /field/hierarchy, MCP get_subgraph.
3Out-of-scope must 404, not 403. A 403 confirms the entity exists. The endpoint would become an oracle for enumerating other domains' entities. DB test: "requesting a node under the wrong domain returns 404, not 403".
4Persist resolved ids only. papers.domain stores the canonical registry id, never the raw request string. A paper claimed nlpp while its entities were stamped default. The two disagreed permanently and nothing recorded it. requireDomain(...).id at every write entry point; DB test "no row in the database carries an unregistered domain".
5No fabricated success. The LLM layer throws (LLMUnavailableError, LLMStructuredOutputError) and never returns empty-but-successful. A failed chunk increments chunksFailed. A paper whose chunks all failed is failed. A job whose pipeline threw is failed. The version-drift outage (ch. 05) marked papers "completed" with empty graphs on the default configuration. Typed errors; DB tests "a paper whose chunks all fail is marked failed, never completed" and "an unreachable model aborts the paper early".
6Every outbound request has a deadline. Go through services/http.ts. fetch has no default timeout. A hung socket permanently consumes one of JOB_CONCURRENCY worker slots; two of them on the default config is a dead pipeline with no error anywhere. FetchOptions.timeoutMs is required — there is no safe default, so the type system asks. 8 unit tests in http.test.ts.
7Every edge has provenance. Edge + sources row in one transaction. A failed sources insert left an edge with no evidence — an unfalsifiable claim in a graph whose entire point is traceability. db.transaction() in processor.ts; schema FK sources.edge_id → edges.id ON DELETE CASCADE.
8Processing is idempotent. A re-run calls clearPaperContributions() first. Nodes survive; edges another paper also asserts survive. Nodes dedup by normalized name, but every run inserted fresh edges/sources/propositions — so re-processing doubled a paper's PPR mass and repeated its evidence in every retrieval. One transaction that deletes propositions, captures affected edge ids, deletes this paper's sources, then deletes only edges with not exists (select 1 from sources ...).
9The jobs table IS the queue. Enqueue = insert a row (status='queued', no owner). Execute = claim it with FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED. Never hold queued work in process memory. Claiming sets owner/lease and increments attempts; recovery re-queues interrupted work until MAX_JOB_ATTEMPTS, then fails honestly. Handlers signal dead ends with PermanentJobError; everything else is presumed transient. Batch progress is always computed from member jobs, never from counters. Two scars, one on top of the other. First: a global "fail all non-terminal jobs" startup sweep meant a second process — another instance, a test run, tsx watch reloading — silently killed live work. Second, and worse at batch scale: queued work lived only as closures in one process's memory, so any restart during a 20-hour batch lost every job not yet started. 10 DB tests across durable-queue.test.ts and queue-recovery.test.ts, including a kill-9-mid-extraction run that re-queues, reclaims and completes with zero duplicated edges. The "never touch another instance's live-leased job" case is still asserted — that regression shipped once.
10An outage is decided once. LLMUnavailableError for LLM_UNAVAILABLE_ABORT_AFTER consecutive chunks abandons the paper. Content failures stay per-chunk. Availability is a property of the deployment, not the chunk. If the model is unreachable now it is unreachable for chunk 47 too. The distinction between transport and content failure is the whole design. Two error classes; consecutiveUnavailable counter reset by any non-availability failure.
11Retrieval fuses three signals; never silently drop one. RRF on rank positions only. Weights graph=2, vector=1, lexical=1, chosen by sweep. Removing the graph signal costs multi-hop recall, which no other signal provides. Removing lexical costs 37 points of hub nDCG. Both measured. The graph-only arm is kept permanently in the eval harness so fusion's contribution stays attributable rather than assumed.
12Nothing in the retrieval hot path scans a table. Retrieval used to read every node, every vector in the database with no WHERE clause at all, every edge and every proposition, then score in JS. 21 s p50 at 100k entities. RetrieveResult.stats reports the working set precisely; DB test "the working set is reported, and is far smaller than the corpus".
13One embedding space per deployment. Enforced on write, on comparison, and at startup. Changing the model is a migration plus a re-embed, never a config edit. cosine() iterated Math.min(a.length,b.length) — so a 1536-d OpenAI vector scored 0.71 against a 768-d Ollama vector. A confident number derived from comparing the first 768 coordinates of unrelated spaces. Every metric still looked healthy. assertVectorShape / assertComparable / checkVectorStorage(); 17 unit tests.
14Schema changes ship as migrations. db:migrate, never db:push, on anything holding data. Destructive steps guard themselves. push infers a plan nobody reviews and can drop a column it believes was renamed. Migration 0001 raises an exception with row counts rather than dropping the only copy of the data.
16Authorization goes through the chokepoint. Routes call requireDomain(c, raw, action), never resolveDomain. Mutations also call requireScopeOn. Every isolation defect in this codebase came from a route that omitted a filter. The fix was to make omission impossible, not to review harder. A unit test greps src/routes/ and fails if any route reaches for the unauthenticated resolver.
17Deny quietly, log loudly. Every authentication failure returns identical text; the reason goes to the audit log. Distinguishable errors ("no such key" vs "revoked" vs "expired") are a probe oracle. DB test "every authentication failure returns the same message"; recordAudit on every denial.
19Entity and relationship types are open. Any non-empty string is stored as written. A normaliser folded unknown types into concept/uses. It silently contradicted documented behaviour and discarded the entire taxonomy of the first structured source that used it — an OpenAPI import produced api, endpoint, capability, schema, auth and stored all five as concept. text columns, not pgEnum. toEdgeType() passes identifier-shaped predicates through verbatim; connector tests assert the domain types survive.
20Structured connectors must not fall back to text extraction. If structured: true, every unit carries an extraction. A unit arriving with only text turns a free import into a per-operation model bill — silently. Connector test: "every unit carries a pre-built extraction and no text".
22Model choice per role is decided by models:compare, not by intuition. Spend on extraction; economise on verbalization. The measured answer is the opposite of the instinct. Extraction recall is permanent: a sparse graph cannot be queried and fixing it means re-ingesting. Routing table printed at startup and served from /health; models:compare reruns the comparison on your hardware.
23Retrieval tuning is decided by the harness, not by argument. Run pnpm --filter api eval before and after any change to PPR, MMR, fusion weights, seed count, or hop limits. Two tuning decisions have already been reversed by measurement (ch. 49). Both sounded correct. --sweep-alpha and --sweep-weights are built in; the reasoning is recorded in ppr.ts for the next person with the same idea.
24Entity resolution reads the index, never a window. Candidates come from one batched equality probe on nodes_normalized_name_idx and one batched k-NN lateral over node_vectors_embedding_hnsw, both domain-scoped. Filtered ANN asks for hnsw.iterative_scan. A "most recent N nodes" preload made identity depend on creation order: past N, a domain forked entities it already contained and only logged about it. And ilike where eq was meant was a Seq Scan discarding all 100,000 rows (cost 3039) against an index scan at cost 12. DB suite seeds 2,500 nodes — more than the old window — and demands the oldest entity still resolve, in-domain only.
25A signal is a shutdown, not a crash. SIGTERM/SIGINT close the server, stop claiming, drain for SHUTDOWN_GRACE_MS, then close the pool — and never release still-running claims. Releasing a live claim lets another instance run the same extraction concurrently. Nothing handled SIGTERM at all, so every deploy was a crash. Handlers installed at boot; verified live (clean drain, port released). Expiry is the safe handover, and it is bounded.
26A pause is not a failure. Pausing stops at the next chunk boundary — never mid-chunk. The job takes a paused state: terminal for the queue, no retry consumed. Recording a deliberate pause as failed is the same category of lie as showing a failed paper as "Pending" — a state the system knows, reported as something else. Own enum value in both processing_status and job_status; DB tests assert a paused job is never claimed or owned.
27A state the operator must clear belongs in the operations view. GET /api/papers/processing returns failed and paused, not only in-flight papers. Excluding them put the Retry and Resume controls in a list that could never contain a paper needing them. The buttons existed and were unreachable. A failure with no control is an outage. DB tests assert the view distinguishes queued / running / unscheduled and reports lane width.
28Proximity is not identity — a merge must be justifiable. merge-guard.ts approves every embedding merge: papers by exact title only, numbers must match, the unshared part must be generic rather than distinguishing, initialisms exempt. Audited on the live graph, every same-type pair above 0.82 was two different things — two papers at 0.870, English-to-German vs -French at 0.904, a model vs its ensemble at 0.917. No threshold separates those from synonyms: the embedding measures topic, and identity is not topic. 26 unit tests using the real observed names, both directions. Never widen GENERIC_TOKENS without re-running the live audit.
29An entity with no edge is not knowledge. Every entity a chunk names gets paper -[mentions]-> entity, chunk-attributed like any other claim. The extractor returns entities and relationships as separate lists, so anything it did not put in a relationship became a node with no edges — measured at 62% of the graph. Present in the corpus, absent from the knowledge. backfill:mentions repairs older corpora: 132 → 552 edges, isolation 62.4% → 3.6%, retrieval quality unchanged.
30A corpus split across domains looks sparse when it is severed. Check select domain, count(*) from papers group by 1 before blaming the extractor. Six papers landed in default and one in nlp. All were about attention; none could relate to another, and shared concepts existed once per domain, unable to merge. This is isolation working — and invisible. POST /api/papers/:id/domain moves a paper and rebuilds its contribution: every identity decision it made was made against the wrong neighbourhood.
31Entity identity is enforced by the database. A unique index on (coalesce(domain,''), normalized_name) where the name is not null, and node creation is an ON CONFLICT … DO UPDATE … RETURNING upsert so a losing writer reads back the winner. Resolution's lookups decide which node a mention belongs to and cannot prevent a race. The write path ended in a SELECT that missed followed by an INSERT — two concurrent transactions produced two nodes with the same name in one domain. Reachable in the shipped config: PROCESS_CONCURRENCY is a knob and the queue is multi-instance. DB suite races eight writers at one entity: one row, and all eight receive the same id. Also asserts the constraint does not collapse the same name across two domains.
32One live process job per paper. Partial unique index over non-terminal, non-paused process jobs; createJob raises AlreadyScheduledError. Two routes could schedule a paper and only /resume checked first. Four papers ended up with two queued jobs, one already processing. Both run resumePaper, which clears unfinished contribution before rebuilding — so with concurrency each clears what the other is writing. A DB test bypasses the route guard and calls createJob directly; the database refuses. paused is terminal so a parked paper stays resumable.
33Progress must describe work that still exists. Every path that clears contribution resets processing_progress in the same statement. A rebuild left the old percentage, so a paper holding zero chunks displayed 100%. Same class of lie as a failed paper shown as "Pending". Reset is in the same UPDATE as the status change, so the two cannot drift.
34A paper's nodes cannot stay behind when it moves. A domain move removes nodes it owns in the old domain that nothing else references. clearPaperContributions keeps nodes on purpose — shared within a domain — and that reasoning inverts across a move. One paper in nlp still owned 255 nodes stamped default: invariant 4 broken by the operation meant to preserve it. repair:domain-drift audits any corpus for the disagreement; dry run unless --apply.
35type='paper' is not "an ingested document". Offering a document to open additionally requires p.id = n.paper_id AND lower(p.title) = n.normalized_name. The extractor creates a paper node for every cited work — seven papers produced eighty-five paper nodes, seventy-four of them citation targets with no text to study. Only a document's own node satisfies both halves; a citation target carries the citing paper's id.
36An activity signal counts work, not rows. The dashboard's poll interval reads workers.running + workers.queued. It keyed on list length until invariant 27 made that list include failed and paused papers — after which it polled every 2 s forever with nothing running. A change three files away re-created the bug the interval existed to fix. DB test: parked papers appear in the list and contribute zero to workers. That test is the contract the first fix lacked.
37A control that fails silently is worse than no control. Mutation errors render in the UI, not only in console.error. A broken button and a slow one looked identical, leaving the operator no way to tell which they were looking at. Errors surface in a dismissible banner beside the controls.
How to read a gap in the numbering 15, 18, 21 are missing. That is not sloppiness — invariants were removed when the thing they protected was redesigned away, and the numbers were not reused so that older commit messages and comments referring to "invariant 18" do not silently point at something else. 24–30 were added later and are appended rather than interleaved, for the same reason: an invariant's number is an identifier, not a ranking. Stable identifiers for rules are worth more than tidy sequences. The same reasoning is why revoking a principal is a timestamp and not a delete (ch. 31), and why the audit log denormalises the actor label.

The pattern behind the pattern

Read the enforcement column again and a shape appears. Every invariant is held by one of five mechanisms, in descending order of strength:

1
Impossible by construction. FetchOptions.timeoutMs is required, so you cannot write a fetch without a deadline. text columns instead of enums make open types cost nothing. This is the strongest form and always worth reaching for first.
2
Impossible unless you go out of your way. requireDomain is the ordinary helper; resolveDomain is the exotic one. The safe path is the lazy path. This is what makes the chokepoint work where a checklist would not.
3
Caught by a test that encodes the failure. Not "auth works" but "leaves a live job owned by ANOTHER instance untouched". A test named after the bug it prevents survives refactors; a test named after the function does not.
4
Caught by a meta-test. The grep over src/routes/ is unusual and worth stealing: it tests a property of the codebase rather than of a function. Cheap, and it catches the class rather than the instance.
5
Written down and hoped for. Invariants 22 and 23 live here — they are process rules, and process rules decay. Both are mitigated by making the measurement one command away, which is the best available substitute for enforcement.

When you propose a new invariant anywhere, ask which rung it lands on. If the honest answer is 5, the design is not finished — ask what would have to change for it to be 1 or 2. That question is most of what "architecture" means in practice.

07
Chapter 07 · Foundations

Data model: six kinds of truth

Fourteen tables, grouped into six concerns. Read the schema and you have read most of the architecture — the domain stamp on four tables is the isolation model, the sources table is the provenance guarantee, and the index list is the performance contract.

The six concerns

Fourteen tables. The amber column is the product's central correctness claim, expressed as data.

Table by table: the decisions worth knowing

papers — the document, plus its lifecycle

Note what is not here: no separate documents table for non-paper sources. The OpenAPI connector writes into papers too, with connector = 'openapi' and structured_units holding the pre-built extraction. The name is now wrong — it should be documents — and that is honest debt, not a defence. But the shape is right, and the reason is subtle and worth internalising:

Why structured_units is persisted rather than passed in memory The connector could hand its units straight to the pipeline. Persisting them instead means reprocessing a structured document behaves exactly like reprocessing a text one — the pipeline reads its input from the row either way, and neither path needs the original source to still be reachable. That is the invariant-8 idempotency guarantee extended to a source type that did not exist when it was written. Generalising a guarantee costs less than special-casing around it.

processing_status is a pgEnum with nine values (pending … completed / failed). Enums are used here and rejected for type columns, and the distinction is exactly right: a lifecycle is closed and owned by the code; a taxonomy is open and owned by the data. Adding a status is a deliberate state-machine change that should require a migration. Adding an entity type must not.

nodes / edges — the graph, and six indexes that are the architecture

IndexServesWithout it
edges_source_type_idx (source_id, type)"outgoing edges of this node of this type"Seq scan on every traversal hop.
edges_target_type_idx (target_id, type)"incoming edges" — the reverse directionNode detail views degrade to full scans.
edges_domain_type_idx (domain, type)Domain-scoped type queries, hyperbolic training edge selectiontrain-hyperbolic scans all edges per domain.
nodes_domain_type_idx (domain, type)The ANN seed query's domain predicate; type-filtered node listsThe HNSW search's filter becomes a post-filter, wrecking recall at low ef_search.
nodes_normalized_name_idxExact-name resolution before the embedding matchThe cheapest, most certain resolution path becomes the most expensive.
papers_arxiv_id (UNIQUE)Duplicate ingest detectionThe pre-flight check in the route is advisory only — two concurrent ingests of the same id both pass it. The unique index is the real guard, and the route catches SQLSTATE 23505 to report the race as the duplicate it is rather than a 500.
A pattern worth stealing: the pre-flight check is advisory, the constraint is authoritative ingest.ts checks for an existing arxivId before creating a job — and then also catches the unique violation, walking up to five levels of cause because Drizzle wraps the driver error so the SQLSTATE is not on the object thrown. Checking only the top level meant every duplicate surfaced as an unhandled DrizzleQueryError with the whole INSERT in the log. Any check-then-act you write is a race; the question is only whether the database will catch you.

sources — provenance as a first-class table

One row per (edge, paper) assertion, carrying section, extracted_text, and a span. Two properties fall out of it that are hard to get any other way:

  • Co-assertion survives deletion. When paper A is reprocessed, only edges whose sole remaining provenance was A are removed. An edge that paper B also asserts keeps its other citation. That is the not exists (select 1 from sources where edge_id = edges.id) clause in clearPaperContributions, and it is the difference between a graph you can maintain and one you can only rebuild.
  • Confidence is auditable. An edge's confidence is a number; its sources rows are the argument for that number. GET /api/graph/queries/provenance/:edgeId returns the paper title, arXiv id, section and extracted span — a claim you can go read.

node_vectors / propositions — the geometry, and four indexes

A proposition is the unit of compressed retrieval: one atomic factual sentence (the evidence text of one accepted edge, capped at 500 chars) with its own embedding and the node ids it mentions. This is the single most under-appreciated design choice in the system, so it is worth stating plainly:

Why propositions, not chunks Naive RAG retrieves 2,000-character chunks — mostly padding around the one sentence that mattered. Propositions are ~200 characters and each maps to exactly one asserted relationship. That gives you (a) a ~10× better signal-to-token ratio, (b) a natural join key (node_ids) so the graph signal can say "give me evidence about these entities", and (c) a redundancy measure MMR can actually use, because two propositions saying the same thing really are near-duplicates in embedding space, whereas two chunks containing the same sentence are not.

propositions carries four indexes and each one serves a different retrieval signal — this table is where the three-signal fusion is physically realised:

IndexSignalQuery it serves
propositions_embedding_hnswvectorORDER BY embedding_vec <=> $q LIMIT n — "what reads like the question".
propositions_node_ids_gingraphjsonb_exists_any(node_ids, ARRAY[...]) — "what do we know about these entities". Without GIN this is a sequential scan that decodes every JSONB array in the corpus.
propositions_text_search_ginlexicalExpression index on to_tsvector('english', text). Without it, every query recomputes to_tsvector over the whole corpus — and this is in the hot path, not just a benchmark arm.
propositions_domain_idxisolationEvery one of the above is AND-ed with a domain predicate.

node_vectors.space stores provider:model:dimensions alongside every vector. It is never read in the hot path. It exists so that a mixed-space corpus — the failure mode of invariant 13 — is detectable after the fact rather than merely preventable going forward. Recording enough to diagnose a failure you have already prevented is cheap insurance and almost nobody does it.

jobs / batches — the queue, not a mirror of one

The id is text, not a UUID: job-{timestamp}-{counter}-{random}. The counter is there because Date.now() has millisecond resolution and bulk ingest creates rows in a tight loop — two jobs sharing an id would overwrite each other's status rows. A generated UUID would have been fine too; what matters is that the collision was considered.

The load-bearing columns are owner, lease_expires_at and attempts. A row with status='queued' and owner IS NULL is available work; claiming it is a single UPDATE … WHERE id = (SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED). Chapter 16 is entirely about that statement and what it buys.

batches: an entity with no counters, on purpose "100 documents in one request" needs an entity the caller can hold on to. The row carries note, domain, and total — fixed at creation — and nothing else.

Progress and completion are always COMPUTED from the member jobs at read time, never denormalised onto this row. The reasoning is in the schema comment: "counters that are written twice drift, and a batch that claims 100/100 while a job row says failed is worse than no batch tracking at all." A derived value that can disagree with its source is a bug waiting for a deadline. The cost is one GROUP BY per read, which the jobs_batch_idx index makes trivial.

paper_chunks — the checkpoint that makes a retry cheap

One row per chunk a paper has completed, with a content hash. Extraction costs ~34 s per chunk over ~26 chunks, so a failure at chunk 24 used to discard fifteen minutes of correct work — the only provably-safe undo was "undo everything", because an edge could not be attributed to a chunk. sources.chunk_index makes it attributable, so the undo can be per-chunk and the retry can resume.

Why the hash, and why the write order Resume assumes chunk N still means the text it meant last time. Chunking is deterministic given the source, so a hash settles it: a mismatch means the boundaries moved and every checkpoint is stale. Resuming onto different content would silently mis-attribute evidence — worse than redoing the work.

The checkpoint is written after the chunk's rows land. A crash between the two costs a redo of that chunk; recording one that did not happen would skip it forever. Losing a checkpoint is recoverable; inventing one is not.

graph_findings — proposals, not changes

What the background audit believes about a domain: a node (and for a merge, the node that should survive), a detector name, a verdict, a reason in the operator's terms, and a confidence. The audit writes here and changes nothing else.

Why reason is a column

A proposal nobody can evaluate is noise. "Distinguished by ensemble" is actionable; a confidence score alone is not. The reason is what makes a human able to overrule the tool, which is the point of storing rather than applying.

Why dismissed rows are kept

Without them the next audit re-proposes what a human already rejected. A tool that keeps asking the same question gets ignored — which is how a quality system stops working without anyone deciding to switch it off. Same reasoning as revoking a principal by timestamp rather than by delete.

Proposals for a domain are replaced on each run rather than accumulated: a finding is a statement about the graph as it is now, and a stale one would have an operator applying a merge whose target was rebuilt an hour ago.

principals / audit_log — identity with an afterlife

Three decisions in these two tables that are worth copying wholesale:

  • key_prefix is stored in the clear and indexed. It is a lookup handle, not a secret. Without it, verifying a presented key means fetching every principal and comparing hashes — a table scan per request and a growing timing surface.
  • domains is jsonb and the wildcard must be spelled out as ['*']. An empty or malformed grant list authorises nothing. A grant list that failed to load cannot be mistaken for permission to read everything — that is default-deny expressed in a data representation rather than in a code branch.
  • audit_log.actor is a denormalised text label beside the FK, which is ON DELETE SET NULL. Deleting a principal must not erase its history. Normalisation is the right default; an audit trail is one of the few places it is actively wrong.

Where the same concept lives in more than one place

ConceptHomesWhy the duplication is deliberate
A paperpapers row and a nodes row of type paperThe row is the document; the node is its position in the graph, so edges can point at it. getOrCreatePaperNode scopes the lookup to the domain being processed — reusing a paper node stamped with a different domain would place this run's entities on the wrong side of the boundary.
Evidence textsources.extracted_text and propositions.textDifferent jobs: sources is the audit record attached to one edge; propositions is the retrievable, embedded, deduplicated unit. Merging them would force retrieval to carry provenance columns it never reads, and force provenance to be embedded.
Domain membershipFive tablesDenormalised on purpose. Joining to papers to learn a node's domain would put a join in every hot-path predicate and make the isolation filter something you can forget. A column you must name is harder to omit than a join you must add.
Entity typenodes.type, DomainConfig.entityTypes, KNOWN_NODE_TYPESThe column is truth; the config is the domain's preference (prompt context); the shared constant is a UI seed default. Three roles, three homes — but note packages/shared/src/types.ts still declares a closed union for NodeType, which contradicts invariant 19. Tracked in ch. 67.
08
Chapter 08 · Foundations

A geometry primer, from zero

Six pieces of mathematics carry this system: cosine similarity, PageRank, Reciprocal Rank Fusion, Maximal Marginal Relevance, hyperbolic embeddings, and label propagation. None is hard. All are taught here from nothing, because you cannot review a ranking change you cannot derive, and a system whose core is a black box to its own team will be tuned by superstition.

1 · Embeddings and cosine similarity

An embedding is a function from text to a fixed-length list of numbers, trained so that texts with similar meaning land near each other. nomic-embed-text gives 768 numbers; text-embedding-3-small gives 1,536. Each number is a coordinate; the text is a point, or equivalently an arrow from the origin.

To compare two of them we care about direction, not length — a long document and a short sentence about the same thing should score as similar. That is cosine similarity: the cosine of the angle between the two arrows. 

// services/embeddings.ts
export function dot(a: number[], b: number[]): number {
  assertComparable(a, b, 'dot');          // ← invariant 13, see below
  let sum = 0;
  for (let i = 0; i < a.length; i++) sum += a[i] * b[i];
  return sum;
}

export function cosine(a: number[], b: number[]): number {
  assertComparable(a, b, 'cosine');
  const denom = norm(a) * norm(b);
  return denom === 0 ? 0 : dot(a, b) / denom;   // 1 = same direction, 0 = orthogonal, -1 = opposite
}
War story · the confident wrong number This function used to loop Math.min(a.length, b.length) instead of asserting equality. Compare a 1,536-dimension OpenAI vector to a 768-dimension Ollama vector and you got 0.71 — a plausible, confident similarity derived from the first 768 coordinates of two completely unrelated coordinate systems.

Flip EMBED_PROVIDER on a populated corpus and retrieval quietly becomes noise while every metric still looks healthy. This is the archetypal geometry bug: not a crash, not an exception — a number of the right type, in the right range, that means nothing. The fix is invariant 13, enforced at three points: on write (assertVectorShape), on comparison (assertComparable), and at startup (checkVectorStorage reads pg_attribute.atttypmod to confirm the column's declared width matches the configured space).

In Postgres, pgvector's <=> operator is cosine distance = 1 − cosine similarity. Distance, not similarity, because ORDER BY … ASC LIMIT k is what an index can accelerate. The code converts back: score: 1 - Number(r.distance).

2 · PageRank, and then Personalized PageRank

Imagine a random walker on the graph. At each step it either follows a random edge from where it stands, or — with probability α — teleports. Run it forever and the fraction of time spent at each node is that node's score. Nodes that many important nodes point to accumulate mass. That is PageRank.

Classic PageRank teleports to a uniform random node, which yields a global notion of importance. Personalized PageRank teleports back to a fixed set of seed nodes. The stationary distribution then answers a much better question: "starting from what the question is about, where does importance flow?" Nodes two and three hops away get real mass, because the walk reaches them — and that is precisely the multi-hop capability that nearest-neighbour search cannot have. 

// knowledge-field/ppr.ts — 30 iterations of power iteration
for (let iter = 0; iter < iterations; iter++) {
  const next = new Array<number>(n).fill(0);

  // Teleport component: alpha of all mass returns to the seed distribution.
  for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) next[i] = alpha * s[i];

  // Propagation: each node spreads (1-alpha) of its mass over its edges,
  // split in proportion to edge weight (= confidence).
  for (let j = 0; j < n; j++) {
    const rj = r[j];
    if (rj === 0) continue;
    if (rowSum[j] === 0) {                 // dangling node: no edges at all
      for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) next[i] += (1 - alpha) * rj * s[i];
      continue;                            // send its mass back to the seeds, or it leaks
    }
    const share = ((1 - alpha) * rj) / rowSum[j];
    for (const [i, w] of adj[j]) next[i] += share * w;
  }
  r = next;
}

Four implementation details in that loop are each a decision:

  • Edges are treated as undirected for propagation (each edge is pushed into adj[i] and adj[j]). "A extends B" should make B relevant to a question about A and vice versa. Directionality lives in the edge type, which retrieval reads separately.
  • Edge weight is confidence, floored at 0.01 so a zero-confidence edge still conducts a trickle rather than silently disconnecting the graph.
  • Dangling nodes return their mass to the seeds. Without this the distribution leaks and no longer sums to 1, which quietly changes what every score means.
  • Thirty iterations, no convergence check. On a working set of ~1,100 nodes the distribution is stable long before that, and a fixed count is deterministic — the same input always produces the same ranking, which is what makes the eval harness meaningful.
α = 0.15, and the story is chapter 49 The restart probability was briefly raised to 0.5 on an argument that was correct in its own terms and still made retrieval worse. That war story is the best single lesson in this book about proxy metrics; it is told in full in chapter 49 and recorded in a 20-line comment inside ppr.ts for the next person who has the same idea.

3 · Reciprocal Rank Fusion

You have three ranked lists of the same documents from three different rankers. How do you merge them?

The tempting answer — normalise the scores and add them — is wrong, and wrong in a way that hides. A cosine similarity of 0.83, a ts_rank of 0.09, and a PageRank mass of 0.004 are numbers from three unrelated scales with different distributions. Mapping them onto a common scale means inventing a conversion no data supports, and that invented constant then silently decides every ranking you ever serve.

RRF sidesteps it entirely by using only positions, which are comparable by construction:

score(d) = Σ over rankers   weight_r / (k + rank_r(d))          // k = 60 by convention
Why k exists

With k=60, rank 1 contributes 1/61 = 0.0164 and rank 2 contributes 1/62 = 0.0161 — nearly identical. Without k, rank 1 would contribute 1.0 and rank 2 0.5, letting a single over-confident ranker dominate.

Large k encodes a belief: agreement across rankers matters more than any one ranker's certainty. That is exactly right when your rankers have complementary blind spots, which measurement showed these do.

Why weights are still needed

Equal weights cost multi-hop recall here. The graph list is the only signal that can surface an answer sharing no vocabulary with the question; diluting it one-to-one against two lexical/semantic signals pushed such answers out of the top k — recall 100% → 95%, nDCG 63.1% → 54.8%.

A sweep showed graph=2 restores multi-hop completely at no cost elsewhere, and that 3 and 4 measured identically to 2. So 2 is chosen — the smallest value that buys the gain. Picking the largest value that also works is how you accumulate parameters nobody can justify later.

Fused scores are then normalised to [0,1] by dividing by the maximum, because MMR's λ · relevance − (1−λ) · redundancy trades two quantities on one scale. RRF scores are unbounded above and depend on how many rankers ran — so without normalisation the trade-off would silently shift whenever a ranker returned nothing.

4 · Maximal Marginal Relevance

You have 400 candidate propositions and a 2,000-character budget. Taking the top 10 by relevance gives you ten near-identical sentences. MMR greedily picks the item that maximises: 

MMR(d) = λ · relevance(d)  −  (1 − λ) · maxs ∈ selected similarity(d, s)

At λ = 0.7 (this system's setting) relevance dominates but a candidate that closely resembles something already chosen is penalised. The second pick is the best thing that says something new.

The subtle bit: relevance is injected, redundancy is computed MmrCandidate accepts a pre-computed relevance, and retrieval supplies the fused score. If MMR recomputed cosine(query, vector) itself it would throw away the graph and lexical signals and rank purely by embedding distance again — undoing the entire fusion stage one line before the end.

The vector is still required, because MMR needs it to measure redundancy between selected items. Relevance-to-query and similarity-to-selected are different questions and must be allowed to come from different sources. That distinction is documented in the type itself, which is where a subtlety like this survives.

MMR is O(n·k) cosine computations — for each of k selections it scans all n remaining candidates and compares each against every already-selected vector. That is why maxCandidates: 400 exists and why the fused set is capped before MMR rather than after. This is the one place in the read path where an unbounded input would be quadratically expensive rather than linearly.

5 · Hyperbolic space, and why trees break Euclidean geometry

Here is the problem, stated concretely. Take a binary tree of depth d. It has ~2d leaves, and every leaf should be roughly equidistant from the root. In flat (Euclidean) space, the amount of room at radius r grows polynomially — like rn for n dimensions. The number of tree nodes grows exponentially. So you run out of space, and the embedding is forced to place unrelated cousins close together. Distortion is unavoidable and gets worse with depth.

Hyperbolic space has exponentially growing volume — the room available at radius r grows like er. It is, in a precise sense, the natural geometry of trees. The Poincaré ball model puts all of it inside the unit ball, with distance defined as: 

d(u, v) = arcosh( 1 + 2·‖u − v‖² / ((1 − ‖u‖²)(1 − ‖v‖²)) )

Note the denominator: as either point approaches the boundary (‖u‖ → 1), that term goes to zero and the distance blows up. The edge of the ball is infinitely far away. Two consequences that the system uses directly:

norm ≈ generality

General concepts settle near the origin, where they are "close to everything". Specific ones are pushed outward. So a node's distance from the origin is a learned abstraction level — no one labelled it.

distance ≈ relatedness

hierarchyNeighbors() takes the 3k nearest nodes, then splits them: smaller norm than the target ⇒ generalizations, larger ⇒ specializations. Two lines of code, one genuinely novel query.

Training is Riemannian SGD: sample a positive edge and 5 random negatives, softmax over negative distances, then take a gradient step rescaled by (1 − ‖θ‖²)² / 4. That rescaling is the essential part — near the boundary a Euclidean-sized step would be an enormous hyperbolic move, so the metric tensor shrinks it. Every update is followed by project(), which clips back inside the ball. A burn-in of 10 epochs at 1/10th the learning rate stabilises the early chaotic phase.

6 · Label propagation

The cheapest respectable community detection there is. Every node starts as its own community. Repeatedly, in random order, each node adopts the most common label among its neighbours. Densely connected groups converge on a shared label within a few passes; the loop stops early when nothing changed.

War story · .sort(() => Math.random() - 0.5) is not a shuffle The shuffle here is Fisher–Yates. It used to be the one-liner above, which is a well-known trap: an inconsistent comparator is not a uniform permutation, and the resulting bias is implementation-defined — V8's sort switches algorithms by array length. So which labels won depended on the engine's sort internals, and communities changed shape for reasons entirely unrelated to the graph. If a result depends on randomness, the randomness has to be correct randomness, or the result is not reproducible and cannot be debugged.

The migrations, and what each one was written to prevent

Schema changes ship as versioned migrations, never db:push, on anything holding data (invariant 14). Read as a sequence they are a fair record of what this system got wrong and when.

MigrationWhat it exists for
0000_add_pgvector_indexesHNSW on node and proposition vectors — the change that took retrieval from 21 s to 153 ms.
0001_drop_jsonb_embeddingsStoring vectors twice was a latency bug: 1.8× p50 from cache eviction. Guards itself — refuses to drop the column unless its replacement is populated.
0002_propositions_text_searchGIN index for the lexical signal, worth 37 points of hub nDCG.
0003_identity_and_auditTenants, principals, keys, audit log.
0004_connector_documentsconnector and structured_units: one substrate, many sources.
0005_batches_and_durable_queuebatches, jobs.attempts, jobs.batch_id — the queue becomes the table.
