0006_separate_failures_from_claimsjobs.failures, because an interruption was spending the retry budget meant for real errors. Also repairs the damage: re-queues jobs failed by that accounting, since a schema change that leaves destroyed work destroyed is half a fix.
0007_chunk_checkpointspaper_chunks, sources.chunk_index, and the paused statuses. Retries resume instead of restarting.
0008_node_identity_uniqueThe unique identity index — and it merges pre-existing duplicates first, repointing edges, provenance, propositions and vectors, and deleting the self-edges the merge creates.
0009_one_active_job_per_paperPartial unique index; collapses existing duplicates, preferring a claimed job over an unclaimed one because it may be mid-extraction.
0010_graph_findingsThe audit's proposals table.
A pattern worth stealing: migrations that repair, not just alter 0006, 0008 and 0009 all do two jobs — add the constraint and fix the rows that violate it. A constraint cannot be added to a table that already breaks it, so the repair is not optional; the choice is whether it happens in the migration where the reasoning is written down, or in a hurried manual query at 2 am. The migration is the only place the intent and the fix stay together.

Operational commands

CommandWhat it answers or repairs
pnpm --filter api evalRetrieval quality. Run before and after any change to PPR, MMR, fusion, seeds or hops (invariant 23).
pnpm --filter api models:comparePer-role model trade-offs on this hardware (invariant 22).
pnpm --filter api bench:retrievalLatency A/B against the pre-index path.
pnpm --filter api bench:lane-widthShould PROCESS_CONCURRENCY be higher? Alternates arm order so a drifting machine cannot fake a result, refuses to run while a live lease holds the GPU, and calls a result inconclusive when it is smaller than the run-to-run spread.
pnpm --filter api seed:loadSynthetic corpus. Clears its own prior fixture — it used to be additive, which produced 100,000 rows over 50,000 names and a corpus the system could not legally hold.
pnpm --filter api backfill:mentionsAttach entities to the paper that named them, for corpora ingested before that was automatic. Dry run by default.
pnpm --filter api repair:domain-driftFind and fix nodes whose domain disagrees with their paper's. Dry run by default.
POST /api/graph/auditQueue the quality audit (ch. 17c). Writes proposals; changes nothing.
Every repair tool is a dry run until told otherwise backfill:mentions writes 420 rows on a corpus this size; repair:domain-drift deleted 535 nodes on its first real invocation. A tool that does that on first run — before its operator has seen what it intends — is a trap, however correct its logic. Reporting first is not politeness; it is the only way the operator can catch the case the tool's author did not think of.

Putting the six together

MathematicsAnswersCostWhere
Cosine similarity"Do these two texts mean the same thing?"O(d)Resolution, MMR redundancy, ANN (in Postgres)
Personalized PageRank"Given what this question is about, what else matters?"O(iters · edges)Ranking the working subgraph
Reciprocal Rank Fusion"Three rankers disagree — now what?"O(total ranks)Merging graph + vector + lexical
MMR"Which 10 of these 400 say the most, in 2,000 characters?"O(n · k · d)Context compression
Poincaré embeddings"What generalises / specialises this?"O(epochs · edges · neg)Hierarchy queries (batch-trained)
Label propagation"What are the natural clusters?"O(iters · edges)Community summaries (batch)

Notice the column that is not there: none of them costs a model call. That table is the product thesis expressed as a cost model — and it is why the read path can be a component another agent calls in a loop rather than a service it calls once, reluctantly.

Part II

The write path — from a source to a graph

Follow one document all the way in: through a connector, a queue, a PDF parser, one language-model call, resolution in embedding space, validation in rule space, and finally into Postgres with its provenance attached. This is where the expensive work happens, exactly once.

09
Chapter 09 · Write path

Connectors: one substrate, many sources

Manifold's pitch is that it is a knowledge substrate and research papers are one source among many. Until the connector contract existed, papers were the only source and "universal" was an assertion with nothing behind it. The contract makes the claim testable — and its central distinction is worth more than the code.

The distinction that is the whole point

Unstructured1 LLM call per chunk

A PDF, a wiki page, a transcript, a Slack export. The connector produces text; the extractor spends one model call per chunk finding the entities and relationships inside it.

Cost scales with corpus size × chunk count. For a 26-chunk paper on a local 7B model, that is roughly 15 minutes of wall time and the dominant cost of the whole system.

Structured0 LLM calls

An OpenAPI document, a database schema, a dependency manifest, a Terraform state file. The graph is already there — paths, operations, tags, schemas, foreign keys, security requirements. Asking a language model to rediscover them would be slower, cost money per import, and hallucinate edges the file states exactly.

These connectors emit the ExtractorOutput directly. Cost: parsing and inserts.

Both kinds converge on the same ExtractorOutput shape, so resolution, validation, provenance, embedding and retrieval are identical downstream. That convergence is what makes the substrate claim real rather than aspirational: nothing after this boundary knows or cares where a fact came from.

The contract

// connectors/types.ts
export interface IngestUnit {
  section: string;                // carried into provenance
  text?: string;                  // extract from this…
  extraction?: ExtractorOutput;   // …or this is already the graph. Mutually exclusive.
}

export interface Connector {
  readonly id: string;
  readonly name: string;
  readonly description: string;
  readonly inputSchema: string;   // human-readable, surfaced by GET /api/ingest/connectors
  readonly structured: boolean;  // ← reported, not inferred

  collect(input: Record<string, unknown>, ctx: ConnectorContext): Promise<ConnectorDocument[]>;
}
Why structured is declared rather than inferred You could compute it: "did every unit come back with an extraction?" But then an operator planning a 100,000-operation import cannot find out what it will cost before starting it. Declaring it makes GET /api/ingest/connectors answer llmCallsPerDocument: 0 | "one per chunk" up front. Cost is part of an interface, not an implementation detail — and invariant 20 turns the declaration into a promise the connector tests enforce.

The OpenAPI connector, in detail

This is the connector that makes the positioning concrete. When an agent can reach hundreds of connected systems, no context window holds every tool schema — so choosing the right endpoint is a retrieval problem over a typed graph, which is exactly what this system does.

The traversal is entirely structural. From one spec it emits:

Spec elementBecomesEdges
info.title + versionnode type api—
each operationnode type endpoint
label = operationId, else METHOD /path		api --exposes--> endpoint
tags[]node type capabilityendpoint --belongs_to--> capability
components.securitySchemes + per-operation securitynode type authapi --requires--> auth, endpoint --requires--> auth
$refs under requestBodynode type schemaendpoint --accepts--> schema
$refs under responsesnode type schemaendpoint --returns--> schema

A four-operation spec produces a typed graph at llmCalls: 0:

nodes: endpoint 4 · capability 3 · schema 3 · auth 1
edges: exposes 4 · belongs_to 4 · returns 4 · accepts 3 · requires 3
The clever part: where the evidence text comes from Retrieval embeds propositions.text. For a structured connector, that text is composed from the author's own prose — the summary and description fields:

"createUser (POST /users) in Acme API: Creates a new customer record and sends a welcome email."

That is why the natural-language question "which endpoint creates a user" finds POST /users even though the path string never contains the word "create". The structural graph gives you exact answers to structural questions (which endpoints require bearerAuth); the human prose gives you semantic reach over the same nodes. You get both from one free import.

Details that separate a demo connector from a real one

  • collectSchemaRefs walks arbitrary sub-trees. A $ref can be nested under content → media type → schema → items → allOf[2]. A shallow lookup would find perhaps 60% of them.
  • confidence: 1 and spanStart/End: 0. These are stated facts, not inferences, and spans are meaningless for a parsed document. Fabricating a span would corrupt the provenance contract with a number that looks like evidence.
  • Per-unit deduplication. The same schema referenced in three responses becomes one entity.
  • An empty security: [] array is the spec's way of saying "explicitly public" and correctly produces no edge — an absence with meaning, which is the kind of thing shallow parsers get wrong.
  • JSON only, and it says so. YAML would need a parser dependency. A YAML document is detected (/^\s*(openapi|swagger)\s*:/m) and gets a specific error telling you to convert it — rather than "not valid JSON", which sends you looking in the wrong place. The limitation is reported, not silently mishandled.
  • Bounded fetch. MAX_SPEC_BYTES = 12 MB via readBodyWithLimit, http(s) only, with the timeout every outbound call must have (invariant 6).

Structured connectors run inline; unstructured ones queue

POST /api/ingest/connector/:id processes structured documents synchronously and returns the result, while arXiv ingestion returns 202 and a job id. That asymmetry is correct and worth stating as a rule: a caller who just imported an API surface would rather have the graph than a job id to poll, and there is no model call to wait for. The queue exists to protect the event loop and the inference budget from minutes-long work — not as a reflex.

Adding a connector: the whole recipe

1
New file in connectors/ implementing Connector.
2
Register it in connectors/index.ts — one line in a Map. Deliberately the same shape as the domain registry, for the same reason: the extension point should be obvious and boring.
3
Throw ConnectorInputError for bad caller input (→ 400) and ConnectorSourceError for an unreadable source (→ 422). The distinction matters: one is the caller's fault, one is the world's.
4
If structured: true, ensure every unit carries an extraction (invariant 20) and write the test that asserts it.
5
Nothing else. No route, no schema migration, no pipeline change. getConnector() is strict — an unknown id is an error naming the valid ones, never a silent fallback, same contract as domain resolution.

The connectors that should exist next

SourceKindWhy it earns its place
Postgres/MySQL schemastructuredTables, columns, foreign keys, indexes are a graph stated exactly. Answers "what depends on this table" with zero inference — the single most requested question in any migration.
Git repositorystructuredFiles, imports, exports, package manifests. A dependency graph with real provenance (commit + line).
MCP server manifeststructuredDirectly the tool-selection use case: import every connected server's tool list, rank by graph proximity to the task.
Confluence / NotionunstructuredThe institutional-memory use case. Needs PII redaction first (ch. 36).
Jira / LinearhybridIssue links, epics and blockers are structured; descriptions are not. The first connector that would need both paths in one document — a genuinely useful stress test of the contract.
10
Chapter 10 · Write path

Ingestion, end to end

One POST /api/ingest/arxiv, followed all the way to a graph. Every branch in this flow is a decision about trust, cost, or honesty — and several of them are branches that only exist because their absence caused a bug.

Ten gates before a single entity is extracted. Seven return a distinct, actionable error. Note where the HTTP request ends: at an INSERT.
The shape that matters: the route runs nothing POST /api/ingest/arxiv validates, authorises, admits, inserts one row, and returns. It does not fetch, parse, extract, or hold a closure. Everything below the dashed line happens because a worker — this instance or another, now or after a restart, possibly on attempt three — claimed that row.

That separation is what makes the pipeline restart-safe, and it is also a clean layering result: routes insert rows, jobs/ says what a claimed row does, and queue/ knows nothing about arXiv or extraction. Three modules, three concerns, no shared knowledge.

Two stages, two lanes, two durable rows

An arXiv ingest is not one job. It is an ingest-lane job (network + CPU: metadata, PDF download, parse) that, on success with autoProcess, chains a second process-lane job (GPU: extraction) carrying the same batch_id.

Why chain instead of continue

The ingest worker is immediately free to fetch the next document while extraction queues for the GPU. A whole 100-document batch is fetched and parsed to disk within minutes, even though extraction then grinds for twenty hours.

And a restart between the two stages loses nothing: the fetched text is a row, and the process job is a row.

Retry idempotency, designed in

The handler checks for an existing paper before fetching, and if job.attempts > 1 treats it as its own earlier success rather than a duplicate. That closes the window where a crash between the insert and the terminal status update would make the retry fail on a unique-key violation forever.

Any handler that may be retried must be able to recognise its own partial work.

Admission control: the cost of never losing work

async function admit(c, incoming: number) {
  const pending = await pendingJobCount();
  if (pending + incoming > MAX_PENDING_JOBS) {
    return c.json({ error: 'Queue is full', pending, limit: MAX_PENDING_JOBS, message: '…' }, 429);
  }
  return null;
}
// bulk admits at up to 2x size, because each document may chain a process job
const full = await admit(c, unique.length * (autoProcess ? 2 : 1));
A durability guarantee creates an obligation "The queue is durable, which cuts both ways: nothing is ever silently lost, so nothing stops a caller from piling up work either." Without a ceiling, a scripted loop of bulk requests builds a backlog measured in GPU-days, and every later caller's batch quietly lands behind it.

The response carries the current depth and the limit, so a client can back off intelligently rather than guessing. Refusing loudly at a bound is the honest alternative to accepting silently — exactly the same instinct as invariant 5 applied to capacity rather than correctness.

The branches worth studying

Input validation is a security boundary, not a nicety

// routes/ingest.ts
const ARXIV_ID_PATTERN = /^(\d{4}\.\d{4,5}(v\d+)?|[a-z-]+(\.[A-Z]{2})?\/\d{7}(v\d+)?)$/;

export function normalizeArxivId(raw: unknown): string | null {
  if (typeof raw !== 'string') return null;
  // Trim BEFORE stripping the prefix: `^arxiv:` cannot match through leading
  // whitespace, so " arXiv:2308.04079" would otherwise survive as an invalid id.
  const cleaned = raw.trim().replace(/^arxiv:\s*/i, '').trim();
  return ARXIV_ID_PATTERN.test(cleaned) ? cleaned : null;
}

The id is interpolated into two upstream URLs, so it is validated as a format rather than merely trimmed — an unchecked value could inject query parameters into the metadata request or path segments into the PDF URL. (Both are additionally encodeURIComponent-wrapped; belt and braces, because the pattern is the thing a future refactor might weaken.) It also turns a typo into a 400 at the edge instead of a confusing failure three network calls deep. The double-trim is a one-character-class bug that a test now holds in place.

Being a good citizen of someone else's API

Bulk ingest runs JOB_CONCURRENCY papers at once and each opens with a metadata lookup. Firing those in parallel at arXiv earns a 429 — and because the original fetch had no retry, every one of those jobs failed outright. The user queued five papers and got five failures caused entirely by our own impatience. services/polite-fetch.ts fixes it with three mechanisms, and the third is the one most implementations miss:

1 · Throttle

Per-host promise chain plus a minimum gap (ARXIV_MIN_INTERVAL_MS, default 3 s). Concurrent callers take turns. This prevents the 429 rather than reacting to it — which matters because a rejected request still consumed the remote's budget.

2 · Backoff

On 429/5xx, wait and retry — honouring Retry-After when sent, because a server that tells you when to come back has given you better information than any formula. 429 gets a linear 15 s·attempt curve, not exponential: measured, four exponential attempts spanning ~14 s were all rejected because the window had not moved.

3 · Host-wide cooldown

The one people forget. Per-request backoff alone means five queued jobs each independently discover the 429 and independently retry — so the host receives traffic through the entire backoff and never gets a chance to forgive you. A shared cooldownUntil deadline makes one rejection pause everybody.

Also: the metadata call now sends a User-Agent. arXiv's guidelines ask clients to identify themselves and throttle anonymous callers far more aggressively. The PDF fetch always sent one; the metadata call did not — a one-line omission that was a large part of why bulk ingest kept earning 429s.

Deliberately not retried: 4xx other than 429. A malformed id returns the same answer however many times you ask; retrying just moves the failure later while holding a worker slot.

The upstream that answers 200 with an error

// arXiv answers an unknown id with HTTP 200 and an entry literally titled "Error".
if (/^error$/i.test(title)) {
  throw new Error(`arXiv has no paper "${arxivId}"...`);
}

Without this check the corpus silently gains a paper called "Error" whose abstract is the API's complaint — and that paper gets chunked, extracted, and turned into nodes. Never trust a status code as the sole signal of success from an upstream you do not control. Validate the shape of what came back.

Failure classification is the handler's job; failure disposition is the runner's

This is the single most transferable idea in the new ingestion design. The handler never decides whether a job is dead — it decides only whether retrying could change the answer:

Handler throwsRunner doesCases
Error (plain)re-queue until MAX_JOB_ATTEMPTSNetwork failure; arXiv still throttling after politeFetch's patience; a model outage.
PermanentJobErrorfail now, no attempts burnedarXiv has no such paper; a concurrent request already ingested it; the PDF has no text; the stored domain is unregistered; the paper no longer exists.
Why the distinction is worth a whole error class "The old inline version caught everything and marked the job failed itself, which made every transient network blip terminal." A dropped connection on document 47 of 100 ended that document permanently.

And the inverse matters more in the process lane: a retry there costs fifteen minutes of GPU time. Burning three attempts to rediscover that an arXiv id does not exist is 45 minutes of a scarce resource spent reaching the same dead end. PermanentJobError is not defensive programming — it is capacity management expressed as a type.

Note the ordering subtlety in the "saved but unprocessable" case: the handler calls setJobStatus(job.id, { paperId }) first, then throws PermanentJobError — so the batch view can still link to what was saved even though the job failed. Recording what did happen before reporting what did not is a small courtesy to whoever reads the batch later.

The rate-limit lesson: budget by cost, not by prefix

War story · the limit that stopped you watching your own work Ingest was limited to 10/min across the whole /api/ingest/* prefix — which included GET /api/ingest/status/:jobId. Starting five ingests and then polling their status, exactly what the UI does, exhausted the budget on the polling and returned 429 for the status of jobs the caller had just created.

A limit that stops you observing the work you started is not protecting anything — the expensive thing already happened. Limits are now per method: writes get 10/min, reads get 300/min, and observability endpoints get the read budget. The general rule: shape a rate limit like the cost curve of the operation, never like the URL tree.

Bulk ingest: validate the batch, then enqueue the batch

POST /api/ingest/bulk takes up to 100 ids. It normalises and validates all of them before enqueueing any of them, returning a 400 that lists exactly which entries were invalid. A single typo cannot leave half a batch queued and half rejected — an all-or-nothing admission decision, which is the only kind a caller can reason about. It also de-duplicates via [...new Set(normalized)], because the same id twice in one batch would race itself into the unique-violation path.

11
Chapter 11 · Write path

PDF → text → chunks → sections

The least glamorous stage and one of the most consequential: everything downstream can only be as good as the text that comes out here. It is also where the system's most honest known weakness lives.

Download, bounded

// services/pdf.ts → services/http.ts
const buffer = await readBodyWithLimit(response, maxPdfBytes(), 'PDF download');
if (buffer.length < 1000) throw new Error('PDF file too small, likely an error page');

response.arrayBuffer() will happily allocate however many bytes the remote sends; an attacker-controlled or merely broken URL can OOM the process. readBodyWithLimit streams and bails the moment the cap is crossed, so the ceiling is real rather than advisory. It trusts a declared Content-Length only to fail early — never to skip the streaming check, since the header is remote-controlled and may be absent or a lie. And it cancels the reader in a finally, so the socket is released whether the read finished or bailed.

The retry loop rethrows HttpTooLargeError immediately rather than retrying: an oversized document is a deterministic property of the URL, so retrying just re-downloads the same bytes and burns the worker slot again. Distinguishing deterministic from transient failures is most of what a good retry policy is.

Parsing off the event loop

Measured: a 400 ms parse froze the entire API Parsing a 2.2 MB arXiv PDF takes ~400 ms of synchronous CPU, during which the Node event loop services zero timer ticks. Every concurrent request, every health probe, and every job lease heartbeat stalls behind it. At batch scale — 100 documents — that is roughly 40 seconds of accumulated API freeze scattered through the run.

This is the classic single-threaded-runtime trap, and it is invisible in development: one PDF at a time on an idle laptop looks fine. It only appears when something else needs the loop during the parse — which is exactly what a lease heartbeat does, and a missed heartbeat is how a healthy job loses its lease.

services/pdf-worker.ts moves the parse into a worker_threads worker. Two constraints shaped an unusual implementation, and both are worth understanding because they generalise:

No new dependencies

No piscina, no tinypool. A single persistent worker with a task map is enough: "PDFs arrive at most once per document, throughput is not the problem, the blocked loop is."

Diagnose what is actually scarce before reaching for a pool. A worker pool solves throughput; here the resource under contention was latency on the main thread, and one worker frees it completely.

A data: URL module
new Worker(new URL('data:text/javascript,' +
  encodeURIComponent(WORKER_SOURCE)), {
  workerData: { pdfParseUrl: import.meta.resolve('pdf-parse') },
});

The codebase runs as TypeScript under tsx in dev and compiled JS in prod, which makes a worker file awkward to path correctly in both. So the worker is an inline JS module, with pdf-parse located via import.meta.resolve on the main thread and handed over in workerData.

Verified live: identical parse output, and the loop stayed responsive — 20 timer ticks during the parse versus 0 on-thread. That measurement is the whole justification for the file, and it is the right kind: a number, not an argument.

The failure handling, which is where most worker code goes wrong

FailureHandling
Document cannot be parsedWorker survives and posts {ok: false} → rejects with PdfParseError. Never falls back to the main thread — "a document the worker could not parse fails identically on the main thread, and retrying it there just blocks the loop for nothing."
Parse hangs (malformed document looping the parser)PDF_PARSE_TIMEOUT_MS (90 s) fires, the worker is terminate()d, and the next task gets a fresh one. A wedged worker must be killed, not waited on.
Worker dies (spawn failure, non-zero exit)failAllPending() rejects every outstanding task with a clear reason, and worker is nulled so the next call respawns.
Worker infrastructure unavailableOnly then falls back to on-thread parsing — and logs loudly: "the event loop WILL stall for the duration." A degraded mode that hides its own degradation is how a temporary fallback becomes permanent.
Process exitw.unref() — "the worker must never hold the process open." Same discipline as the queue's heartbeat timers.

The distinction in row 1 is the one to internalise: separating "broken input" from "broken infrastructure" determines whether a retry is useful. It is the same split as PermanentJobError vs plain Error in the queue, and the same split as LLMStructuredOutputError vs LLMUnavailableError in the model layer. Three layers, one idea, expressed with three type pairs — that consistency is not decoration, it is what lets a reader predict how an unfamiliar part of the system behaves.

Extraction, and its honest limits

pdf-parse gives raw text. cleanExtractedText then applies four transformations, and the fourth is a real trade-off worth naming:

TransformationPurposeWhat it costs
\r\n | \r → \nLine-ending normalisationNothing.
([a-z])-\n([a-z]) → $1$2Re-join words hyphenated across a line breakMerges genuinely hyphenated compounds that happen to break at the hyphen.
\n{3,} → \n\nCollapse vertical whitespaceLoses some section-boundary signal.
[^\x20-\x7E\n] → ' 'Strip non-ASCIIDestroys every mathematical symbol, Greek letter, and accented author name. "α-blending" becomes " -blending"; "Müller" becomes "M ller".
Open defect · the ASCII coercion The last rule is defensible for a first pass — non-ASCII noise from PDF encodings genuinely confuses extraction prompts — but it is lossy in a domain-relevant way for scientific papers. The right fix is a Unicode-aware cleaner that strips control and private-use ranges while keeping Greek, mathematical operators, and Latin-1 supplement. Tracked in chapter 67; it is cheap and nobody has done it.

Chunking: 2,000 characters, 200 overlap

chunkText splits on paragraph boundaries first and only falls back to word-splitting when a single paragraph exceeds the budget. Then it prepends the last 200 characters of the previous chunk to each subsequent one. The overlap exists for one reason: a relationship stated across a chunk boundary is invisible to an extractor that sees only one side. Overlap buys recall at the cost of some duplicate extraction — which is safe here, because resolution deduplicates entities by name and the rule validator deduplicates identical triples.

ParameterValueThe reasoning, and when to change it
Chunk size2,000Roughly 500 tokens: large enough to contain a claim with its context, small enough that a 1B–7B local model does not lose the middle. Larger chunks mean fewer LLM calls (cheaper) but measurably worse recall on small models. This is a cost/recall dial and it has never been swept — a genuine gap.
Overlap20010%. Enough for one sentence to survive a boundary. Higher overlap is pure duplicated cost.
Inter-chunk delay500 msA fixed setTimeout between chunks in the processor. It smooths load on a local Ollama, but it is a blunt instrument: on a 26-chunk paper it adds 13 seconds for no benefit when the provider is a hosted API. Should be provider-aware.

detectSection: heuristics, honestly labelled

Section labels feed sources.section (provenance) and propositions.section. The detector looks for keywords in the first 500 characters — "introduction", "related work", "method", "experiment", "conclusion", "references" — and falls back to positional guessing: first 20% is introduction, next 40% methods, next 25% results, remainder conclusion.

Why a crude heuristic is the right call here — and where it bit Section is metadata, not truth. A wrong label degrades a provenance display slightly; it does not corrupt the graph. Spending an LLM call per chunk to classify sections would double the dominant cost of the entire system for a cosmetic gain. That is the correct trade.

But it is not free: a real run produced a relationship extracted from the acknowledgments section, labelled as something else. The genuinely valuable use of section detection would be filtering — never extract from references or acknowledgments, because they contain author names and institutions that become plausible-looking, entirely spurious entities. That filter does not exist yet and would improve graph quality more cheaply than any model upgrade.
12
Chapter 12 · Write path

Extraction: the one LLM call

In field mode this is the only generative model call in the entire ingestion pipeline. Everything about how it is invoked, retried, and failed is shaped by that fact: it is the cost-dominant path, and it is the one place where a silent failure would poison the corpus permanently.

The contract

// agents/extractor.ts
export interface ExtractorOutput {
  entities:      Array<{ mention, type, spanStart, spanEnd, confidence }>;
  relationships: Array<{ subject, predicate, object, evidenceText, confidence }>;
}

Note type is string, not a union: open by construction (invariant 19). The domain config supplies preferred types as prompt context, and the model may invent task, model, loss, regularizes — all stored as written, with no migration. evidenceText is the most important field in the whole shape: it becomes both the sources row (provenance) and the propositions row (retrievable, embedded evidence). An extraction without evidence text produces an edge nobody can check and nothing can retrieve.

Domain-aware prompting

The system prompt is assembled from the DomainConfig: domainContext (one sentence of expertise framing), entityTypes and relationshipTypes (preferred vocabulary), plus entityExamples and relationshipExamples (few-shot grounding). So the same extractor, unchanged, becomes an NLP extractor or a graphics extractor by which config it is handed.

That is why getDomain() — the lenient resolver — is permitted here and nowhere else. Getting prompt context slightly wrong degrades extraction; getting a scope filter wrong leaks data. Invariant 1 draws the line exactly there, and the three-function split makes the line visible in the type signature rather than in a comment.

Structured output, and retrying only the right failures

// services/llm.ts — generateStructuredCompletion
for (let attempt = 1; attempt <= totalAttempts; attempt++) {
  let result: LLMResponse;
  try {
    result = await generateCompletion(enhancedSystemPrompt, userPrompt, temperature, operation);
  } catch (err) {
    // Transport/availability failures are not fixed by asking again with the
    // same prompt — surface them immediately with their original type.
    throw err;
  }
  try { return parseJSONResponse<T>(result.text); }
  catch (err) { /* retry — this one IS worth asking again */ }
}
throw new LLMStructuredOutputError(...);
The distinction that makes retry policy correct Availability failures are not retried; content failures are. If Ollama is not running, asking again in one second changes nothing — it wastes a second, holds the worker slot, and buries the real cause. If the model produced prose around its JSON, asking again genuinely might work, because sampling is stochastic.

Two error classes carry the distinction all the way up: LLMUnavailableError (transport) and LLMStructuredOutputError (content). The processor then uses that same distinction to decide whether to abandon the whole paper (invariant 10). One taxonomy, three consumers, no re-derivation.

parseJSONResponse: defensive parsing, and its cost

Small models wrap JSON in prose, fence it in markdown, or emit trailing commas. The parser tries, in order:

  1. Extract from a ```json fence, then a bare ``` fence, then the first {...}, then the first [...].
  2. Strip trailing commas before } and ]; flatten newlines/tabs; strip control characters.
  3. JSON.parse. On failure, one more attempt against a balanced-brace regex.
  4. Throw with the first 200 characters of the response in the message — so the log tells you what the model actually said, which is the only thing that helps at 2 a.m.
Honest assessment This is regex-based repair of malformed JSON, and it can in principle change semantics — \n → ' ' corrupts any string value that legitimately contained a newline, which for evidenceText is plausible. It exists because it makes small local models usable, which is a real product requirement.

The correct fix is provider-side constrained decoding: Ollama's format: "json" and OpenAI's response_format: {type: "json_schema"} both guarantee well-formed output at the sampler. Neither is used. That is the highest-value 20-line change available in this file, and it is tracked in chapter 67.

The structured shortcut

// pipeline/processor.ts — the branch that makes a free import free
const extractorOutput = unit.extraction
  ? unit.extraction                              // ← 0 LLM calls
  : await extractEntitiesAndRelationships({ paperId, chunkIndex: i, text: unit.text ?? '', section, domain });

Two lines. The entire difference between a structured import and an unstructured one, in the pipeline. Everything after this point — resolution, validation, provenance, embedding, retrieval — is identical. That is what a well-placed seam looks like: the variation is expressed once, at the point where it actually varies, and the rest of the system never learns about it.

13
Chapter 13 · Write path

Resolution in embedding space

"3DGS", "3D-GS", "3D Gaussian Splatting" and "Gaussian splatting" are one entity. Deciding that is entity resolution, and it is the single most consequential operation in the write path: resolve too eagerly and you merge two real things forever; too conservatively and the graph fragments into unconnected synonyms that no traversal can bridge. Conventional stacks spend an LLM call per chunk on it. This spends one batched embedding call.

The algorithm

1
Gather every mention that needs an identity. Not just extracted entities — also both endpoints of every relationship. Without that, an edge can reference a name that never got a node, and the processor drops it. A subtle, easy-to-miss completeness requirement.
2
One batched embed() call for all mentions in the chunk. One network round trip per chunk, not one per mention.
3
Exact normalized-name match first. byNormName maps both normalizedName and lower(name). Cheap, unambiguous, and it should win — an embedding match that disagrees with an exact name match is an embedding match that is wrong.
4
Otherwise, cosine against same-type existing nodes that have a cached vector. Best score above SIM_THRESHOLD = 0.82 wins.
5
No match ⇒ new entity, isNew: true, confidence 0.7 (versus 0.9 for a match). The processor then does a second exact-name check against the database before inserting, because the candidate window may not have contained it.
6
Relationships are rewritten to canonical endpoint names, and self-edges (source === target after canonicalisation) are dropped here rather than surviving to validation.

The vectors are reused, not recomputed

// resolve-embed.ts returns the mention vectors alongside the resolution…
export interface EmbedResolverOutput extends ResolverOutput {
  vectorsByName: Map<string, number[]>;
}
// …and processor.ts persists them for new nodes with no extra call:
const vec = vectorsByName.get(entity.canonicalName.toLowerCase());
if (vec) await db.insert(nodeVectors).values({ nodeId, embeddingVec: vec, model, space }).onConflictDoNothing();

A small thing with a large consequence: the embedding computed to resolve a mention is the same embedding needed to make that node retrievable. Recomputing it would double the embedding bill of ingestion. Threading it through the return type — rather than re-deriving it — is the difference between a pipeline that costs one embedding call per chunk and one that costs two.

Threshold 0.82: audited, and found insufficient on its own

The threshold was chosen by reasoning, never measured. When it finally was measured — by running every same-type pair in the live graph through the comparison that resolution uses — the result inverted the design. Every single pair above 0.82 was a pair of genuinely different things:

cosinePairWhy they are not the same
0.917GNMT + RL [38] vs GNMT + RL Ensemble [38]A model and its ensemble
0.913Deep-Att + PosUnk Ensemble [39] vs Deep-Att + PosUnk [39]Same, reversed
0.904WMT 2014 English-to-German vs WMT 2014 English-to-FrenchTwo different tasks
0.888recurrent neural networks vs gated recurrent neural networksA qualifier that changes the architecture
0.870Convolutional Sequence to Sequence Learning vs Sequence to Sequence Learning…Two different papers
0.833Neural Machine Translation by Jointly Learning… vs Effective Approaches…Two different papers
0.839averaging the last 20 checkpoints vs …the last 5 checkpointsDifferent numbers, different claims
The finding that killed the threshold-only design Two distinct papers score 0.87. Two distinct language pairs score 0.90. There is no value of the threshold that separates these from genuine synonyms, because the embedding measures topic and identity is not topic. Raising the bar to 0.93 would keep these apart and would also refuse 3DGS ↔ 3D Gaussian Splatting, which is the entire reason the embedding path exists. A single scalar cannot carry this decision.

The merge guard: cosine gets a vote, not a veto

knowledge-field/merge-guard.ts must approve every embedding-based merge, on both the graph-lookup path and the intra-batch path. It is a set of small, precise rules that replace one blunt number. 

// merge-guard.ts — the four rules, in the order they fire
export function mayMerge(a, b): MergeVerdict {
  // 1. A paper's identity is its title, not its subject.
  if (IDENTITY_BY_NAME_ONLY.has(typeA) || IDENTITY_BY_NAME_ONLY.has(typeB))
    return { ok: false, reason: 'papers are identified by title, not by topical similarity' };

  // 2. Numbers must match exactly: "last 20" and "last 5" are different claims.
  if (numbers(tokensA).join() !== numbers(tokensB).join())
    return { ok: false, reason: `different numbers (…)` };

  // 3. The abbreviation case — the reason embeddings are here at all.
  if (isInitialism(a.name, tokensB) || isInitialism(b.name, tokensA)) return { ok: true };

  // 4. Whatever they do NOT share must be generic, not distinguishing.
  const distinguishing = [...onlyA, ...onlyB].filter(t => !GENERIC_TOKENS.has(t));
  if (distinguishing.length > 0)
    return { ok: false, reason: `distinguished by "ensemble"` };
  return { ok: true };
}

Rule 4 is the interesting one. Helios and Helios system differ by a category word and are one thing; GNMT + RL and GNMT + RL Ensemble differ by a technical qualifier and are two. GENERIC_TOKENS is deliberately short — every addition widens what may merge, and the cost of a wrong entry is a silent, permanent conflation.

Verified against the live graph, not against intuition All 7 above-threshold pairs in the corpus are refused, each with a logged reason (distinguished by "ensemble", different numbers (20 vs 5), papers are identified by title…). The merges the system exists to make still happen: 3DGS ↔ 3D Gaussian Splatting, PPR ↔ personalized page rank, residual connection ↔ residual connection technique. Twenty-six unit tests pin both directions using the real observed names, and retrieval quality is unchanged (nDCG 87.7%).
The asymmetry is the whole argument A false split leaves two nodes that anyone can see and merge later; the evidence survives on both. A false merge destroys the distinction permanently and invisibly — nothing downstream can recover that two things were ever different. So the guard refuses unless it can state a reason to proceed, and RESOLUTION_MERGE=exact disables embedding merges entirely for deployments that want zero risk. vector restores the audited-unsafe behaviour and exists only to reproduce the audit above.

The cross-type rule, and the dead code that hid inside it

// The old gate. The inner `if` is unreachable — the outer one already proved it.
if (nodeType !== cand.type && cand.type !== 'paper' && nodeType !== 'paper') {
  if (nodeType !== cand.type) continue;   // ← always true: dead condition
}
War story · a correct outcome reached by incorrect code The outer if had already established nodeType !== cand.type, so the inner check did nothing and the block reduced to continue. The behaviour happened to match the stated intent — which is the most dangerous kind of defect, because tests pass and nobody looks again. The next person to "simplify" it had a coin-flip chance of inverting the semantics.

Resolved, and the rule it encoded was then reversed by the audit. The old intent was that a paper could match across types on proximity, because the extractor names papers both as entities and as citation targets. Measurement says otherwise: two genuinely different papers score 0.87, so proximity cannot decide which paper something is. Papers now merge on an exact title match and nothing else — and an exact title still resolves, because that path never goes through the embedding comparison at all.

The candidate window, and why it had to die built

Resolution used to load the RESOLUTION_CANDIDATE_LIMIT (2,000) most recently created in-domain nodes and compare every mention against every one of them in JavaScript. Two separate defects lived there.

War story · the silent duplication engine The first version was .limit(500) with no ORDER BY. Past 500 nodes Postgres returned an arbitrary subset — different on every call, at the planner's discretion — and every mention outside it silently became a duplicate. Any LIMIT without an ORDER BY is a non-deterministic result set masquerading as a bounded one.

Ordering made it deterministic, which made it predictably wrong rather than randomly wrong. Past 2,000 nodes, identity depended on creation order: an entity introduced by paper #1 was invisible to paper #40, so the graph forked an entity it already contained — and logged "Older entities may be duplicated" while carrying on.

The window is gone. Candidates now come from knowledge-field/resolve-candidates.ts: two bounded, domain-scoped index reads whose cost is proportional to the number of mentions, not to the size of the graph. 

// One batched k-NN query answers every mention in one round trip.
with mentions(idx, vec, mtype) as (values (0, '[…]'::vector, 'method'), …)
select m.idx, c.id, c.name, c.type, c.distance
from mentions m
cross join lateral (
  select n.id, n.name, n.type, (nv.embedding_vec <=> m.vec) as distance
  from node_vectors nv join nodes n on n.id = nv.node_id
  where nv.embedding_vec is not null
    and n.domain = $domain                       -- isolation, inside the scan
    and (lower(n.type) = m.mtype or …)          -- the type rule, pushed down
  order by nv.embedding_vec <=> m.vec
  limit 8
) c
Exact namebtree

One batched equality probe on nodes_normalized_name_idx, whole domain, no window. The old path used ilike — measured on 100k nodes it was a Seq Scan discarding all 100,000 rows at plan cost 3039, against an index scan at cost 12, for a question it was already semantically equivalent to since both sides are pre-lowercased.

Nearest neighboursHNSW

A lateral join over node_vectors_embedding_hnsw, so N mentions cost one round trip and each gets its own index search rather than sharing a trimmed result set.

The plan is worth reading honestly EXPLAIN confirms Index Scan using node_vectors_embedding_hnsw per outer row — but it also shows the domain and type filters applied above the index scan, as the executor discards rows that arrive. With hnsw.iterative_scan off, that walk stops after ef_search candidates whether or not K of them passed, so a domain holding a small slice of the corpus can come back with fewer than K eligible neighbours — or none. In retrieval that is a quality wobble; here it would mean minting a duplicate entity, which is permanent. The query therefore asks for strict_order iterative scan, with a capability probe and a loud fallback for pgvector < 0.8. Measured on 100k nodes it was not needed — a 20-node minority domain returned 8 of 8 either way — but "it happened to work on this corpus" is not a property.
Proven by a test that seeds more than the old window The regression suite inserts 2,500 nodes — more than the limit that used to exist — buries a decade-old entity underneath them, and demands it still resolve, in-domain only. Any reintroduced window fails it. A second test gives 200 of the filler nodes vectors so the k-NN query has a real field to discriminate within, rather than one candidate it cannot help but return.

The layer none of the above provides: concurrency built

Everything so far decides which node a mention belongs to. None of it prevents two workers from creating the same node at the same time, because the write path ended in a check-then-insert: 

-- Two workers, two different papers, both mentioning "Transformer"
select id from nodes where normalized_name = $1 and domain = $2;  -- both miss
insert into nodes …;                                              -- both create
Demonstrated, not hypothesised Two concurrent transactions with a 0.4 s pause between the check and the insert produced two nodes with the same normalized_name in the same domain. This is reachable in the shipped configuration: PROCESS_CONCURRENCY is a knob, and the queue is deliberately multi-instance, so two papers extracting in parallel is the design working as intended.

Only the database can serialise this, so it does. Migration 0008 merges any pre-existing duplicates — repointing edges, provenance, propositions and vectors, deleting self-edges the merge creates — and then adds the constraint: 

create unique index nodes_identity_unique
  on nodes ((coalesce(domain, '')), normalized_name)
  where normalized_name is not null;

Node creation became an upsert, so the loser of the race reads back the winner and carries on attaching its edges rather than failing the chunk. coalesce because NULL domains do not conflict with each other in Postgres — including them would silently weaken the constraint rather than widen it. Partial, because a node without a normalized name has no identity to compare.

Eight concurrent writers, one node, eight identical ids The DB suite races eight writers at the same entity and asserts both properties that matter: one row exists, and every writer came away with the same id. It also asserts the two things the constraint must not do — collapse the same name across two domains (isolation is not deduplication), or collapse two genuinely different names.
A fixture that could not exist in production was measuring production The constraint would not build on the 100k load corpus: 50,000 duplicate groups. The seeder was additive and restarted its counter each run, so two invocations produced 100,000 rows over 50,000 distinct names. The latency figures stand — HNSW indexes 100,000 distinct vectors either way — but the corpus did not describe what it claimed to, and it was not a corpus the system could ever hold. seed:load now clears its own prior fixture first.

The other half of the same defect: one chunk naming a thing twice

Fixing cross-document fragmentation left the intra-batch case untouched, and a live run exposed it immediately: a single chunk named Helios and Helios system, neither existed yet, so no lookup could relate them — two nodes for one thing, in one document. Mentions that are new to the graph are now compared against each other before identities are minted, under the same threshold, the same type rule and the same merge guard. First occurrence wins the canonical name: deterministic, and the first mention of a thing is usually the definitional one.

Verified end to end Two nodes before, one after, with the real embedder (nomic-embed-text scores that pair 0.881). The unit tests deliberately use lexically-near pairs — adaptive batching / adaptive batching method, 0.87 under the deterministic local embedder — rather than semantically near ones, because they test the collapse mechanism, not the embedder's judgement. The embedder is a swappable deployment choice; this logic is not.

Where legacy mode fits

PIPELINE_MODE=legacy swaps in agents/resolver.ts, an LLM call per chunk. It exists for exactly one reason: to be the control arm. Both implementations satisfy the same ResolverOutput interface, so GET /api/field/benchmark compares two implementations of one contract rather than two systems. Keeping a slower, more expensive predecessor alive as a measurable baseline is a discipline most teams skip — and then they cannot answer "how much did the clever thing actually buy?"

14
Chapter 14 · Write path

Validation in rule space

The validator's real job turns out to be mechanical: check that an edge type is compatible with its endpoint types, drop degenerate and low-confidence edges, and deduplicate. None of that needs a language model. A type-compatibility matrix and a confidence floor do it deterministically, in microseconds, at 0 LLM calls.

The compatibility matrix

Edge typeVerdictRule, and the reasoning
evaluates_onrejectTarget must be a dataset or metric when its type is known. "Method evaluates on Method" is a mis-extraction, not a fact.
extends
improves
compares_to		rejectNeither endpoint may be a dataset or metric. A dataset cannot extend anything. Papers are allowed as proxies for methods, because "Kerbl et al. improves NeRF" is how papers actually write.
authored_bysoftenShould involve a paper. If both endpoints are known and neither is a paper, subtract 0.2 from confidence rather than rejecting — the extraction is suspicious, not impossible.
cites · introduces · uses
and anything unknown		acceptPermissive by default. Invariant 19: unknown types never hard-reject.
Three verdicts, not two — and the unknown rule 'ok' | 'soft' | 'reject' is the design worth stealing. A binary validator forces every uncertain case into one of two wrong answers. The middle verdict lets the system express "this is probably wrong" as a number that flows into edge confidence, into PPR edge weight, and ultimately into ranking — where a weak edge conducts less mass than a strong one instead of being present-or-absent.

Equally important: unknown endpoint types never hard-reject. If the validator does not know what a node is, it lacks the information to condemn the edge — and rejecting on ignorance is how an open type system quietly becomes closed. This is what lets the OpenAPI connector's endpoint --requires--> auth pass through untouched, despite the matrix never having heard of either type.

The other three checks

  • Degenerate edges. Missing endpoint, or source === target after normalisation. A self-loop adds nothing to the graph and inflates PPR mass on one node.
  • Duplicate triples. source|type|target seen already in this chunk ⇒ rejected. Directly the counterpart of chunk overlap (ch. 11): overlap deliberately re-extracts boundary claims, and this is what makes that safe.
  • Confidence floor 0.4. Below it, the edge is dropped. A low-confidence edge is not free — it conducts PPR mass, appears in subgraph views, and adds a row to edges forever. The floor is applied after softening, so a soft verdict can push a marginal edge below the line. Ordering matters and is correct here.

Rejections are returned, not discarded

export interface ValidationOutput {
  accepted: ResolvedRelationship[];
  rejected: Array<{ relationship, reason: string }>;      // ← with a reason
  confidenceAdjustments: Array<{ relationshipId, originalConfidence, adjustedConfidence, reason }>;
}

stats.relationshipsRejected surfaces the count all the way to the job's progress message. That number is a quality signal: a paper with 40 extractions and 35 rejections says the extractor is producing garbage for this document, which is exactly what you want to know when tuning a prompt or choosing a model. Throwing rejections away would make the pipeline look clean and leave you blind.

Closing the loop later: agentic graph repair

The rule validator checks relationships at insertion time. But a graph drifts: confidence stays low, later papers create temporal impossibilities, two methods end up each "extending" the other. agents/repair.ts is an auditor that periodically re-examines suspect edges against their recorded provenance and an LLM judge, then keeps, re-weights, or retracts them.

Detection reasonLLM callsHow it is found
low_confidence0Confidence ≤ confidenceThreshold (default 0.5).
temporal_contradiction0Pure date logic over sources → papers.publicationDate. A paper cannot extend something published after it.
mutual_contradiction0Pure graph logic: A extends B and B extends A.
verdict per flagged edge1, cappedAn LLM judge returns keep | adjust | retract with a rationale, bounded by maxEdges (default 25).
Two design choices worth copying Detection is free; judgement is metered. Deciding which edges are suspect is pure graph and date arithmetic at zero model cost — exactly the same instinct as the rest of the field layer. Only the per-edge verdict spends a call, and the cap makes the bill knowable before the run.

It is read-only by default (apply: false), returning a full RepairReport of proposed actions with rationales. A tool that mutates a knowledge graph on its first invocation is a tool nobody will run twice. Dry-run-by-default is the correct posture for any destructive automation — the same instinct as POST /api/domains/backfill's dryRun.

Status: the module exists and is not yet wired to a route or a schedule. It is the first concrete answer to the contradiction gap in ch. 51, and it needs an endpoint plus a cron before it counts.

The known weakness

The matrix is global; ontologies are per-domain validate-rules.ts hardcodes a matrix built for the research-paper ontology (method, concept, dataset, metric, paper). Its normalizeType maps everything else to unknown — which is safe, because unknown never hard-rejects, but it means the API-surface domain gets no type validation at all. Nothing stops schema --requires--> endpoint, which is backwards.

The fix has an obvious home: DomainConfig already carries entityTypes and relationshipTypes. Add an optional typeRules — a list of {edgeType, allowedSource[], allowedTarget[]} — and have validateRelationshipsRules consult the domain's rules first, falling back to the global matrix. Per-domain type rules are named as a natural next step in the README and remain unbuilt. It is a half-day of work with a real quality payoff.
15
Chapter 15 · Write path

Persistence, provenance, idempotency

Where the graph actually gets written. Three properties are established here that nothing downstream can recover if they are missed: every edge has a source, every row carries the resolved domain, and running the same paper twice converges instead of accumulating.

The transaction that defines the product

// pipeline/processor.ts
await db.transaction(async (tx) => {
  const [edge] = await tx.insert(edges).values({
    sourceId, targetId, type: relationship.type,
    domain: domain.id,                                  // ← the RESOLVED id, invariant 4
    confidence: String(relationship.confidence || 0.5),
  }).returning();

  await tx.insert(sources).values({
    edgeId: edge.id, paperId, section,
    extractedText: relationship.evidence?.slice(0, 1000),
  });
});

Two inserts, one transaction. Written separately, a failed sources insert left an edge with no evidence behind it — an unfalsifiable claim in a graph whose whole point is that every claim is traceable to a paper. And there is no repair path: later you cannot distinguish edges that lost their provenance from edges that never had any.

The general rule Any two writes where the second is what makes the first meaningful belong in one transaction. Not "related data" — data whose absence changes the interpretation of the other. That test is sharper than "are these logically together?" and it is what makes the single-datastore choice (ch. 05) load-bearing rather than merely convenient.

Idempotency: clearPaperContributions

Processing is not idempotent on its own. Nodes dedup by normalized name, but every run inserts fresh edges, sources and propositions. Re-running POST /api/papers/:id/process — which the route explicitly permits — therefore duplicated the paper's entire contribution, silently doubling its weight in PPR and repeating the same evidence in every retrieval. 

return db.transaction(async (tx) => {
  // 1. this paper's propositions — no other paper can own them
  await tx.delete(propositions).where(eq(propositions.paperId, paperId));

  // 2. capture affected edges BEFORE dropping the provenance that identifies them
  const touchedEdgeIds = [...new Set((await tx.select({ edgeId: sources.edgeId })
    .from(sources).where(eq(sources.paperId, paperId))).map(r => r.edgeId))];

  await tx.delete(sources).where(eq(sources.paperId, paperId));

  // 3. delete ONLY edges whose sole remaining provenance was this paper
  await tx.delete(edges).where(and(
    inArray(edges.id, touchedEdgeIds),
    sql`not exists (select 1 from ${sources} where ${sources.edgeId} = ${edges.id})`
  ));
});
What it deliberately does not delete
  • Nodes. They are canonical and shared. An entity first seen in this paper may be referenced by twenty others, and dropping it would cascade away their edges (ON DELETE CASCADE on edges.source_id).
  • Edges another paper also asserts. The not exists clause is the whole point: co-asserted facts survive with their other citations intact.
  • Node vectors. They belong to the node, which survives.
Why step 2 must precede step 3

The set of "edges this paper contributed to" is defined by the sources rows. Delete those first without capturing the ids and the information is gone — you would have to scan every edge in the domain to find orphans.

Ordering inside a transaction is not just about atomicity. It is about not destroying the index you need to do the next step.

From whole-paper undo to per-chunk undo built

Clearing everything is correct, and it was also the only provably-safe option while an edge could not be attributed to a chunk. The cost was severe: extraction runs ~34 s per chunk over ~26 chunks, so a failure at chunk 24 discarded fifteen minutes of correct GPU work and began again at chunk 0. Retries are common — a deploy, a crash, a transient model error — so this was paid often.

sources.chunk_index makes every claim attributable and paper_chunks records what completed, with a content hash. Idempotency then holds at a finer grain: the chunks about to run have their previous claims cleared first, exactly as a full re-run does for the whole paper, while completed chunks keep theirs.

resumePaperdefault

Keeps every chunk that completed against unchanged text, clears and redoes the rest. What a retry, a resume and a restart all call.

reprocessPaper?rebuild=true

Clear everything, start at chunk 0. The right call when the goal is genuinely to rebuild — a changed extractor, a new model, a corrected document — and never what a crash silently triggers.

The content hash is the guard that makes resume sound Resume assumes chunk N still means the text it meant last time. Chunking is deterministic given the source, so a hash settles it: matching means the checkpoint describes this exact text, mismatching means the boundaries moved and every checkpoint is stale. Resuming onto different content would silently mis-attribute evidence — worse than redoing the work — so stale checkpoints are discarded, loudly.

The checkpoint is written after the chunk's rows land, never before. A crash between the two costs a redo of that chunk; recording a checkpoint that did not happen would skip it forever.
Proven live Ran a paper, killed the process, dropped one checkpoint, called resume. The processor logged resuming: 1/2 chunk(s) already extracted, skipping them, ran only chunk 2, and produced zero duplicated edges. Nine DB tests pin the rest, including the two properties most likely to rot silently: an edge another kept chunk still asserts survives, and rows written before chunk_index existed (null attribution) are redone rather than guessed at.
Honest limit Propositions carry no chunk attribution, so a partial resume keeps the ones from completed chunks rather than rewriting them. Correct today, but it means proposition-level dedup leans entirely on the chunk-level undo being right.

reprocessPaper() composes it: clear, reset status to pending, then processPaper(). The route decides which to call by checking paper.processed || processingStatus === 'completed' — so a first run never pays the clear cost, and a re-run always does.

Propositions: written per chunk, in one batched embed

if (mode === 'field' && propsToWrite.length > 0) {
  const embs = await embed(propsToWrite.map(p => p.text), 'proposition');
  await db.insert(propositions).values(propsToWrite.map((p, idx) => ({
    paperId, text: p.text, embeddingVec: embs[idx], nodeIds: p.nodeIds,
    section, domain: domain.id, space: embedSpaceId(),
  })));
}

One embed call per chunk, not per proposition. And it is outside the per-edge try/catch — deliberately: a failure here loses the retrieval evidence for edges that were just written, so it counts against the chunk rather than being logged and forgotten. An edge whose proposition failed to write is an edge that exists in the graph but can never be retrieved as evidence: present, invisible, and worse than absent.

A positional contract worth defending embs[idx] pairs with propsToWrite[idx] by index. A short or reordered batch would silently attach the wrong vector to the wrong text — no error, just permanently wrong geometry. That is why embed() checks vectors.length !== safe.length and throws "refusing to misalign them", and why the OpenAI path re-sorts by index defensively even though the API returns in order. Any index-paired API needs its contract asserted at the boundary, because violations of it are invisible downstream.

Entity name → id: the fuzzy fallback, and why it is a smell

function findEntityId(entityMap: Map<string,string>, name: string): string | null {
  const normalized = name.toLowerCase();
  if (entityMap.has(normalized)) return entityMap.get(normalized)!;
  for (const [key, value] of entityMap.entries()) {
    if (key.includes(normalized) || normalized.includes(key)) return value;   // ← substring match
  }
  return null;
}
Open defect · substring matching is a second, worse resolver Resolution already ran and produced canonical names. This substring fallback is a second entity resolution pass with far weaker semantics, running after the careful one. "GS" matches "3D Gaussian Splatting". "NeRF" matches "Mip-NeRF360". It has no threshold, no type check, no confidence, and it is first-match-wins over an unordered Map, so which node an edge attaches to depends on insertion order.

It exists to paper over cases where the validator returned a name that resolution did not canonicalise — the right fix is to make that impossible upstream (resolution already gathers relationship endpoints, so the gap should be closable) and delete this function. Tracked in chapter 67; it is the most likely source of silent graph corruption remaining in the write path.
16
Chapter 16 · Write path

The queue: the table is the queue

450 lines, no broker, and a design that is the direct consequence of one piece of arithmetic. Understanding why the arithmetic forced this shape — rather than the shape being chosen and then justified — is the point of the chapter, and the reasoning transfers to any long-running job system.

The arithmetic that decided the architecture

Start here, before any design Extraction measures ~34 s per chunk × ~26 chunks ≈ 15 minutes per paper on this hardware. So "process 100 documents in one request" is 20+ GPU-hours.

No queue design makes that faster. Not BullMQ, not Temporal, not a thread pool, not Kubernetes. The GPU is the constraint and it is fully occupied.

So the question is not "how do we go faster?" It is: what must the architecture buy, given that the run takes a day? And the answer follows immediately — a run that survives. Over 20 hours, a deploy, a crash, an OOM kill, or tsx watch reloading on a file save is not a risk, it is a certainty.

That reframing is the whole chapter. Once you accept that the job takes a day, every property of the design falls out: durability of queued work, resumability of interrupted work, retry with a cap, idempotent handlers, stage separation, admission control, and observable depth.

What the previous design got wrong

The earlier version was a perfectly respectable in-process bounded-concurrency runner: enqueue() pushed a closure onto an array, and the jobs table mirrored its status. Ownership and leases were added to stop instances destroying each other's work. All of that was correct as far as it went.

The hard ceiling A queued job existed only as a closure in one process's memory. The row said queued; the thing that would actually run it was a JavaScript function on a heap.

So a restart in the middle of a 100-document batch lost every job not yet started and failed the ones mid-flight with "Interrupted by server restart. Please retry." A twenty-hour run, killed at hour nineteen, left the operator a table full of failures and no way to resume.

The status was durable. The work was not. That distinction is easy to miss precisely because the table looks like a queue from the outside.

The new shape, in one statement

// queue/index.ts — claimNextJob(). This is the entire coordination mechanism.
update jobs
set owner = ${INSTANCE_ID},
    lease_expires_at = ${leaseDeadline()}::timestamptz,
    attempts = attempts + 1,
    updated_at = now()
where id = (
  select id from jobs
  where status = 'queued' and owner is null and type = ${type}
  order by created_at
  limit 1
  for update skip locked
)
returning id
FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, and why it is the whole answer The inner SELECT takes a row lock on the oldest available candidate. SKIP LOCKED tells Postgres: if a row is already locked by another transaction, do not wait — move to the next one.

So with N concurrent claimers, each one locks a different row or skips past locked ones. Two instances can never take the same job, and neither ever blocks on the other. No broker, no distributed lock, no leader election — one SQL clause, in a database that was already there.

This is a battle-standard pattern (it is how Que, GoodJob, Solid Queue and River all work) and it is dramatically under-used, because most teams reach for Redis before checking whether their existing database already solves the problem.

The four state transitions

Four states. The only interesting edge is the amber one — and it is the one the previous design did not have.

Two lanes, because the stages consume different resources

LaneDefaultEnvResource, and why the number
ingest2FETCH_CONCURRENCYNetwork + CPU: arXiv metadata (throttled per host), PDF download, parse (worker thread). Cheap and parallelisable — the constraint is arXiv's patience, not ours.
process1PROCESS_CONCURRENCYGPU: extraction. Measured to serialise inside Ollama anyway, so raising it on one GPU buys queueing and memory pressure, not speed. On a hosted extraction provider, raise it — the bottleneck moves to the network.
What the split actually buys Document N+1 fetches while document N extracts. A whole batch is fetched and parsed — restart-safe on disk — within minutes, even though extraction then grinds for hours.

That is not a throughput gain (the GPU is still the ceiling). It is a risk gain: the fragile, externally-dependent, rate-limited part of the pipeline completes early and never has to be repeated. If arXiv goes down at hour six, the batch keeps extracting.

The general rule: split lanes by the resource they contend for, not by the step they represent. Two steps that both use the GPU belong in one lane; two steps that use the GPU and the network belong in two.

Retries: two counters, because there are two questions

try {
  await handler(job);
} catch (err) {
  const permanent = err instanceof PermanentJobError;

  // The handler ran and threw. THAT is evidence about the work, so it counts.
  const [row] = await db.update(jobs)
    .set({ failures: sql`${jobs.failures} + 1` })
    .where(eq(jobs.id, job.id)).returning({ failures: jobs.failures });

  if (permanent || row.failures >= MAX_JOB_ATTEMPTS) {
    await setJobStatus(job.id, { status: 'failed', error: /* … */ });
  } else {
    await requeueJob(job.id, message);      // owner → NULL. Anyone may take it.
  }
}
War story · the retry budget that a deploy could spend The original design used one counter. attempts was incremented by the claim, and the reasoning for that was genuinely sound: a job claimed and then SIGKILLed never reaches a catch block, so a counter that lived there would let such a job be re-queued forever. Count what happened to the job, not what the code managed to observe.

That reasoning is correct and it still produced a bug, because the same counter also gated the give-up decision. An interruption therefore spent the budget meant for real errors. In development, tsx watch reloads the process on every file save — three saves during a fifteen-minute extraction and two papers were permanently marked failed having never once thrown. The recorded reason admitted it: "Interrupted and out of retry attempts (3)", with no handler error stored anywhere.

In production the trigger is the same with a different name: three deploys during a long batch.
failuresMAX_JOB_ATTEMPTS = 3

Handler errors only — evidence that the work is bad. This is what the give-up decision reads.

attemptsMAX_JOB_CLAIMS = 25

Claims, including interruptions. A crash-loop backstop for a job that kills its worker every time — the role the single counter was accidentally serving, and serving badly, because it could not tell a poisoned job from an unlucky one.

The generalisable rule Never gate a give-up decision on a counter that a deploy increments. An interruption is evidence about the process, not about the work. Migration 0006 both adds the column and repairs the damage: it finds jobs failed with that exact message and returns them to the queue, because a schema change that leaves destroyed work destroyed is only half a fix.

Note also the third re-queue path: a job whose type has no registered handler is re-queued, not failed. During a rolling deploy an old instance may claim a job type only the new build knows how to run — "a row we cannot run must not be swallowed into limbo." It is released leaseless so the reaper collects it if nobody ever can.

One live job per paper, and why the route check was not enough

Two routes could schedule a paper for processing. POST /:id/resume looked for an existing non-terminal job first; POST /:id/domain did not. That asymmetry is the same shape of bug the authorization chokepoint exists to prevent elsewhere — a rule held by remembering to check it, in one of the two places that needed it.

Observed live, on the operator's dashboard Four papers with two queued process jobs each, and one with a job already processing while another sat queued behind it. The visible symptom was mild — a paper would simply be extracted twice — but the real cost is not the wasted fifteen minutes. Both jobs run resumePaper, which clears the paper's unfinished contribution before rebuilding it. With PROCESS_CONCURRENCY > 1 or a second instance, the two overlap and each clears work the other is writing. 
create unique index jobs_one_active_per_paper
  on jobs (paper_id)
  where type = 'process'
    and paper_id is not null
    and status not in ('completed', 'failed', 'paused');

createJob translates the violation into a typed AlreadyScheduledError, so both routes report "already scheduled" rather than crashing, and a third route added later is safe by construction rather than by diligence. paused is deliberately treated as terminal here: a parked paper is not scheduled, and resuming it must be able to create a fresh job.

The same lesson, twice in one layer This is the second constraint in two chapters that replaces a check-then-act with a database guarantee — the first being entity identity (ch. 13). The pattern is worth naming: when two independent code paths must agree on a uniqueness rule, the rule belongs to the schema. A check in one caller protects that caller; an index protects the invariant.

Progress must describe work that still exists

A paper reporting 100% while holding zero chunks A domain move clears the paper's contribution and queues a rebuild — but it reset the status and left processing_progress alone, so the dashboard showed a completed bar for a paper whose extraction no longer existed. Every path that clears contribution now resets progress in the same statement: ?rebuild=true, and the domain move.

A queued paper showing 60% is not the same bug — that figure describes chunks already checkpointed, which resume will keep, and it is genuinely useful. The distinction is whether the work the number refers to still exists.

Shutdown is a signal, not a crash built

Nothing handled SIGTERM, which made every deploy indistinguishable from a crash: connections cut mid-response, the Postgres pool dropped without closing, and in-flight jobs went to lease-expiry recovery even when two seconds from finishing. A deploy is the most frequent "failure" a service experiences, so it is the one most worth handling properly. 

// Order matters, and each step has a reason.
server.close()            // 1. stop accepting, so nothing new arrives to be abandoned
await stopWorkers()      // 2. stop claiming; drain in-flight for SHUTDOWN_GRACE_MS
await closeDb()          // 3. close the pool last, once nothing else needs it
What shutdown deliberately does NOT do It does not release still-running claims. That would look like a faster handover and would be a correctness bug: the job is still executing in this process, so another instance could claim it and both would extract the same paper into the same graph concurrently. The lease exists precisely to make that impossible, so interrupted work is left leased and recovered the safe way — by expiry, bounded by JOB_LEASE_TTL_MS. The grace period is short by design: extraction takes ~15 minutes and no supervisor waits that long before SIGKILL, so waiting for it would only convert a clean shutdown into a killed one. Anything still in flight is named in the shutdown log, not summarised — an operator reading it after a bad deploy needs to know exactly which work is coming back.

Pause: cooperative, and never mid-chunk built

An operator can park a paper between chunks. The processor checks before each chunk and stops cleanly — never mid-chunk, which would discard the ~34 s already spent and leave a partial extraction to reconcile. The job moves to a paused state that is terminal for the queue (nothing claims it), releases its lease, and consumes no retry.

A pause recorded as a failure is the same lie as a failure shown as "Pending" Both are states the system knows and reports as something else. paused earned its own enum value in both processing_status and job_status rather than borrowing failed, because the difference is exactly what an operator needs to see. Resume and retry are then the same endpoint, because they are the same operation: put it back on the queue and keep the checkpoint.

The reaper, and startup recovery

// reapExpiredJobs() — runs every 60 s, and once at startup with ownerFilter = INSTANCE_ID
requeued = update jobs set status='queued', owner=null, lease_expires_at=null
           where <orphaned> and attempts < MAX_JOB_ATTEMPTS;

failed   = update jobs set status='failed', owner=null
           where <orphaned> and attempts >= MAX_JOB_ATTEMPTS;

papers   = update papers set processing_status='failed'
           where <stuck mid-processing> and not exists (<a live or queued job for it>);
ModeOrphan predicateWhy it is safe
Periodic (every 60 s)owner IS NOT NULL AND lease_expires_at < now()Only jobs whose owner stopped renewing. A live instance renews every LEASE_TTL/4, so it takes four consecutive missed heartbeats to be reaped — a transient DB blip cannot orphan a healthy job.
Startup (ownerFilter)owner = INSTANCE_ID OR lease expiredOur own leftovers are dead by definition — we just started, so our in-memory running set is empty. Anything we own that is non-terminal was interrupted by the previous process exiting.
The one-word difference from the old design The old startup recovery failed interrupted jobs with "please retry". The new one re-queues them. That difference is the entire point of the durable queue — and note what needs no recovery at all: a queued, unowned row. It is simply still in the queue.

The papers sweep is ordered after the requeue deliberately: by the time it runs, resumable papers have a non-terminal job again, so they are excluded by the not exists clause. Only papers with nothing behind them get marked failed. Ordering inside a recovery transaction is load-bearing logic, not sequencing.

Retry safety: why the process handler always reprocesses

// jobs/process-paper.ts — always reprocessPaper, never processPaper
const stats = await reprocessPaper(job.paperId);   // clears prior contribution FIRST

Attempt one may have died after writing edges for chunks 0–11. Re-running the plain path would duplicate every one of them — silently doubling the paper's weight in PPR and repeating its evidence in every retrieval. Clearing first is a no-op on a fresh paper and a correctness requirement on a resumed one.

So invariant 8 (idempotent processing), which was written to make manual re-runs converge, turns out to be the precondition that makes automatic retry possible at all. That is not luck — it is what happens when a guarantee is stated generally rather than scoped to the case that motivated it. The kill-9 test proves it end to end: interrupt mid-extraction, restart, and the job re-queues, is reclaimed as attempt 2, completes, and produces zero duplicated edges.

Polling, jitter, and the small details

DetailReasoning
POLL_MS + random jitter"So multiple instances do not synchronise their polls." Without jitter, N instances started by the same deploy poll in lockstep forever, turning a spread-out load into a thundering herd every second.
Drain-until-empty per tickThe lane claims in a loop up to its limit and breaks on empty, so an idle instance costs one query per poll rather than one per slot.
All timers unref()'dPoll, heartbeat and reaper never hold the process open. A test run or CLI exits normally.
