stopWorkers()Exists and is used by tests. It is also half of the graceful-shutdown story that is still missing (ch. 61) — stop claiming, then wait for in-flight jobs.
Handler registryregisterHandler(type, fn), wired once in jobs/index.ts before startWorkers(). The queue knows nothing about arXiv or extraction, and the routes know nothing about execution.

Observability: depth in /health

"queue": { "queued": 47, "running": 3,
           "byType": { "ingest": { "queued": 12, "running": 2 },
                       "process": { "queued": 35, "running": 1 } } }

One GROUP BY with FILTER clauses, surfaced on the health endpoint. It is the autoscaling signal (ch. 54 scales the worker service on queue depth), the admission-control gauge, and the single most useful number for answering "why is my batch slow?" — a deep process queue with running: 1 is not a bug, it is the GPU, and the numbers say so without anyone having to explain it.

Why not BullMQ + Redis

The decision, stated with its revisit trigger Considered and rejected: "it adds an infrastructure dependency to move hundreds of jobs a day through a system that already has Postgres. SKIP LOCKED is the boring, battle-standard answer at this volume."

Redis buys you: sub-millisecond claims, built-in delayed/scheduled jobs, rate-limited queues, a mature dashboard, and priorities. At hundreds of jobs per day where each takes fifteen minutes, none of those are constraints — and each one is a new thing to operate, back up, and page someone about.

Revisit when: claim contention becomes visible in query latency (thousands of jobs/minute), or you need delayed/scheduled execution, or priorities. The enqueue/claim contract is small enough that the swap stays local to queue/index.ts — which is the correct way to defer a decision: name the trigger, and keep the seam narrow enough that acting on it is a day's work.

What is still missing

  • No graceful shutdown. stopWorkers() exists but nothing calls it on SIGTERM. On a deploy, in-flight jobs are cut mid-transaction — recoverable now (the lease lapses and the reaper re-queues them within 60 s), but a clean drain would avoid the wasted attempt entirely. Ch. 61.
  • No priorities. Strict ORDER BY created_at, so an interactive single-document ingest queues behind a 100-document batch submitted a minute earlier. A priority column in the ORDER BY is a one-line change and an obvious next step.
  • No dead-letter inspection. Failed jobs are rows with an error, but there is no "retry this failed job" endpoint — an operator must re-submit the document.
  • No per-tenant fairness. One tenant's 100-document batch blocks everyone. Fair scheduling means claiming round-robin by tenant, which the single ORDER BY created_at cannot express. This becomes urgent with the second customer.
  • Rate limiting is still per-instance (ch. 34) — so the queue is now genuinely multi-instance while the limiter is not. That asymmetry is worth fixing before scaling out.
17
Chapter 17 · Write path

Failure honesty as a feature

Every system claims to handle errors. Very few take a position on what an error means and defend it consistently across every layer. This one does, and it is the second-highest architectural driver — above provenance, above cost, above latency. This chapter is the whole doctrine in one place.

The doctrine

The rule An empty result and a failed call are different facts about the corpus. The first says "this chunk asserts nothing." The second says "we do not know what this chunk asserts." Collapsing them is how a total model outage produced papers marked fully processed with an empty graph — and those papers then answered future questions with "no evidence found", permanently, with no record that they had never been read.

That single distinction generates the entire error taxonomy. Trace it through every layer:

LayerThe dishonest versionWhat it does instead
LLM transportReturn {entities: [], relationships: []} on failure.Throws LLMUnavailableError (transport) or LLMStructuredOutputError (content). The module never fabricates a successful-looking empty result.
ExtractorCatch and return empty.Propagates. "The processor decides the policy" — the layer that knows the consequence makes the call.
Chunk loopchunksProcessed++ regardless.chunksFailed++, first five messages retained, consecutiveUnavailable tracked.
Papercompleted with an empty graph.All chunks failed ⇒ failed with the reason. Some failed ⇒ completed with the partial-failure note in processingError.
Jobcompleted with a note in progress.failed with the error and the attempt count. Previously the dishonest version meant a failed pipeline reported success to every poller and to the UI.
Job, interruptedfailed — "please retry", pushing the work onto the operator.Re-queued and retried, up to MAX_JOB_ATTEMPTS. Only then failed, saying so: "Interrupted and out of retry attempts (3)". Honesty is not only about admitting failure — it is about not declaring failure for work that can still succeed.
Queue, fullAccept everything; discover the 20-GPU-day backlog later.429 naming the current depth and the limit. Refusing loudly beats accepting silently.
Health{status: "ok"} unconditionally.503 naming the specific misconfiguration — including that the configured Ollama model was never pulled.
HTTP500 for everything.Nine distinct statuses mapped from typed errors (ch. 38).
Community summaryCount it as summarized.Only counts if completion.text.trim() is non-empty; otherwise the mechanical member-list fallback stays and the counter does not move.

The abort threshold, and why it is 3

const UNAVAILABLE_ABORT_THRESHOLD = max(1, env('LLM_UNAVAILABLE_ABORT_AFTER') ?? 3);

"Small but not 1: a single blip — a restarting Ollama, one dropped connection — should not discard a paper that would otherwise process. Three in a row is an outage, not a blip."

And note what resets it: consecutiveUnavailable = 0 on any non-availability failure. A paper where every third chunk produces unparseable JSON will never trip the abort, correctly — that is a content problem, and the other two-thirds of the paper still yields a graph. The counter measures "the deployment is broken", not "this paper is difficult".

Two error-handling patterns worth stealing

Best-effort writes that never mask the original error
async function markPaperFailed(paperId, reason) {
  try { await db.update(papers).set({...}); }
  catch (err) {
    console.error(`Failed to record failure state...`, err);
  }
}

If the database is what failed, the original error must still propagate to the caller rather than being replaced by this write's error. Recording a failure must never be able to hide the failure. The same reasoning makes recordAudit fire-and-forget (ch. 33) and lastUsedAt a void-ed promise.

Clearing stale state on success
processingError: stats.chunksFailed > 0
  ? `Completed with ${failed}/${total} chunk(s) failed: ...`
  : null,   // ← cleared

Without the null, a message from an earlier failed run lingers next to a completed status forever, and the operator reading it cannot tell whether it describes this run or a previous one. Every state transition must consider what it leaves behind, not only what it sets.

Honest health

GET /health → 503
{
  "status": "degraded",
  "services": {
    "database":   { "ok": true },
    "vectors":    { "ok": false, "problems": ["Index propositions_embedding_hnsw is missing. Queries will still
                                return correct results, but by sequential scan."] },
    "llm":        { "ok": false, "detail": "Model \"qwen2.5:7b\" not pulled. Available: llama3.2:3b.
                                Fix: ollama pull qwen2.5:7b" },
    "embeddings": { "ok": false, "detail": "OPENAI_API_KEY is not set. EMBED_PROVIDER defaults to \"openai\"
                                — set EMBED_PROVIDER=ollama for a key-free local run." }
  },
  "models": { "routes": [...], "warnings": [...] },
  "embeddingSpace": "ollama:nomic-embed-text:768",
  "authEnabled": false
}

Three properties of that payload are worth copying into any health endpoint you ever write:

  • It checks what the pipeline actually needs, not what is easy to check. "Ollama responds" is not the same as "the configured model is pulled" — and the second is the failure that actually happens.
  • Every failure names its fix. ollama pull qwen2.5:7b. Not a status code, not a stack trace — the command. A health endpoint's audience is a human at 3 a.m. or an alert that will page one.
  • It reports the silent failures. checkVectorStorage exists mostly for the missing-index case, which produces correct answers at unusable latency with no error anywhere. That class — right output, wrong performance — is invisible to every conventional health check.

And the startup banner, which is a design artefact

✗ Auth: DISABLED — every request runs as an anonymous admin,
    including /api/admin/*. Do not expose this beyond localhost.
  → To enable: pnpm --filter api auth:bootstrap   (issues the first key)
    then restart with AUTH_MODE=required

Auth is off by default so a first run works. That is a defensible product decision — but a dangerous default that is silent is negligence, whereas a dangerous default that is loud, named, and accompanied by the two commands that fix it is a considered trade-off. The same state is reported in /health as authEnabled: false and attributed in every audit row as "anonymous (auth disabled)". "No auth" is a visible state rather than an absence — which is the sentence to remember from this whole chapter.

Part III

The read path — from a question to grounded evidence

Seven stages, one embedding call, at most one generative call, and not a single table scan. This is the differentiated part of the system and the part most worth understanding line by line — including the two parameter changes that measurement reversed.

17b
Chapter 17b · Write path

Graph density: when the graph is not the problem

A knowledge graph can satisfy every invariant in chapter 06 and still be useless, and that is what happened here. The corpus reported 441 nodes and 132 edges — 0.30 edges per node. The Explorer, freshly redesigned, showed entities with one relationship each. The obvious diagnosis was that extraction was weak. The obvious diagnosis was a third right, and the two thirds it missed were both structural.

MeasurementBeforeAfterWhat it was
Edges132552Entities with no relationship had no edge at all
Edges per node0.301.25—
Isolated nodes62.4%3.6%In the corpus, absent from the knowledge
Cross-paper bridges0rebuildingThe corpus was split across two domains

Cause 1 · Entities and relationships are two separate lists

The extractor returns { entities[], relationships[] }. Any entity the model named but did not happen to place in a relationship became a node with no edges — unreachable by traversal, invisible to PPR, and useless to a reader. That was 275 of 441 nodes. It is not a tuning problem; it is what the output shape guarantees. 

// pipeline/processor.ts — every entity a chunk names is attached to the paper
const mentionTargets = new Set<string>();
for (const entity of resolverOutput.resolvedEntities) {
  const id = entity.canonicalId ?? entityMap.get(…);
  if (id && id !== paperNode) mentionTargets.add(id);   // never a self-edge
}
// one `mentions` edge per (paper, entity), with a chunk-attributed source row
A mentions edge is a record, not an inference We know this paper named this entity — that is the extraction record itself. It is also exactly the structure the two real questions need: "what does this paper cover" is the paper's own edges, and "what do two papers share" is a two-hop path through an entity both mention. Neither is traversable unless the entity is attached to both papers in the first place.

It is chunk-attributed like any other claim, so a partial resume undoes it exactly as it undoes everything else (ch. 15), and it carries provenance, so invariant 7 holds. backfill:mentions repairs a corpus ingested before this — dry-run by default, because a backfill that writes 420 rows on first invocation is a trap.
The measurement that had to be taken before shipping it This adds a hub node with ~60 edges per paper, and hub topology drives PPR. Invariant 23 applies: the eval harness was run before and after. Overall nDCG 87.7%, multi-hop 63.1%, hub 100% — unchanged. Had it regressed, the correct action was to revert, not to argue.

Cause 2 · The corpus was severed, and severance looks like sparsity

Even with every entity attached to its paper, zero entities were shared between papers. Five concepts existed twice — self-attention, machine translation, RNNs, attention-based models, learning rate schedule — once in each copy, unable to merge.

War story · six papers in default, one in nlp Papers ingested without an explicit domain land in default. Resolution is domain-scoped by design (invariants 1, 2, 4), so those six papers could not relate to the seventh, and a concept appearing in both halves was two nodes by construction.

This is isolation working, not failing. The system was refusing to do the thing it promises never to do. But the symptom — a sparse, disconnected graph — is indistinguishable from bad extraction, and nothing surfaced the split. Days of work could be spent tuning an extractor that was not the problem.
The diagnostic that should be reflexive select domain, count(*) from papers group by 1;

Before concluding that extraction is weak, confirm the corpus is in one place. A correctness guarantee that partitions data will, when misconfigured, produce a result that looks exactly like a quality problem. POST /api/papers/:id/domain moves a paper and queues a rebuild, not a resume: the chunk texts are unchanged, but every identity decision the paper made was made against the wrong neighbourhood, and carrying those checkpoints over would leave the paper and its entities disagreeing about where they live.

The move itself broke the invariant it was meant to preserve

War story · a paper in one domain owning 255 nodes in another clearPaperContributions keeps nodes on purpose: within one domain they are canonical and shared, so deleting them would cascade away other papers' edges. Across a domain move that reasoning inverts — the nodes carry the old stamp and the paper has left. After moving six papers into nlp, 535 nodes were still stamped default, 193 of them still connected, and a domain with zero papers held more nodes than the one with all seven.

Invariant 4 — a paper and its extracted entities must never disagree about which domain they are in — was broken by the operation written to preserve it. The symptom reached the UI as the same document listed twice, which is how it was noticed at all.

The move now also removes nodes it owns in the old domain that nothing else references — a node another paper there still asserts an edge to is still that domain's and survives. repair:domain-drift audits any corpus for the same disagreement and fixes it; it is a dry run unless asked otherwise, because a repair that deletes 535 rows on first invocation is a trap.

type = 'paper' is not "an ingested document"

War story · seven papers, eighty-five paper nodes The extractor creates a paper node for every work a document cites, which is correct — a citation is an entity. But the Explorer's paper list filtered on type = 'paper' and therefore offered seventy-four citation targets like "arXiv preprint arXiv:1601.06733" as documents to study. They have no text, no chunks, and nothing to show.

The fix is exact rather than heuristic. getOrCreatePaperNode stamps a document's own node with paper_id pointing at itself and normalized_name equal to its title; a citation target carries the citing paper's id, so it cannot satisfy both halves.

Cause 3 · Extraction, which is now the binding constraint partial

With the plumbing fixed, what remains is genuinely extraction quality — and the lens view makes it visible rather than hiding it inside a drawing. Two problems, both measurable:

Few semantic relationships~24%

101 of 422 edges are anything other than mentions. The extractor names many entities and states few relationships between them, so a paper's lens shows one "Introduces" and ninety-seven "Also covers".

Fragments as entity namesunmergeable

"model achieves a BLEU score of 41.0, out", "English-to-German translation on the dev". These are sentence spans, not entities. They can never match anything, so they inflate the node count permanently and pollute every list.

And there is no harness for it Retrieval quality has an eval harness and two reversed decisions to show for it (ch. 49). Extraction quality has neither — no labelled set, no metric, no sweep. Until it does, any change to the extraction prompt is the same kind of guess the PPR alpha change was, and should be treated with the same suspicion. This is the highest-value unbuilt harness in the system.
17c
Chapter 17c · Write path

The graph-quality audit: a cleaner that proposes

Extraction produces things that are not entities. Read straight off the live graph: 

  "dmodel = 512"                                                   ← a formula
  "FFN(x) = max(0, xW1 + b1)W2 + b2 (2)"                           ← a formula
  "a model architecture eschewing recurrence and instead relying"  ← a clause
  "input-feeding approach in which attentional vectors ht are con" ← truncated
  "our input-feeding approach"  /  "input-feeding approach"        ← one entity, twice

These can never merge with anything, they inflate every count, and they fill the entry-point lists a reader chooses from. The extractor learning not to emit them is the real fix and is not yet measurable; naming them afterwards is cheap and available now.

Why it cannot live inside extraction

Not on the critical path

Extraction is GPU-bound and already the bottleneck. Adding graph-wide analysis to it slows the thing that matters to improve something that can wait.

It needs the finished graphthe real reason

Whether "our input-feeding approach" and "input-feeding approach" are one entity is a question about the corpus, not the chunk. At write time resolution has only the mention in front of it. A cleaner running per-chunk would ask resolution's question with resolution's information and get resolution's answer.

So it is a third lane on the durable queue — audit×1, alongside ingest and process — and inherits retries, leases and restart survival for free. Concurrency one, because two audits of the same domain would each delete the other's proposals. It is CPU work, so it must not queue behind a fifteen-minute extraction: an operator asking "what is wrong with my graph" should not wait for the GPU.

It proposes; it does not clean

The asymmetry, sharpened The doctrine governing merges (invariant 28) governs cleaning with more force. A false merge destroys a distinction. A false drop destroys the distinction and the evidence, and leaves no record the node ever existed. So every finding carries a verdict, a stated reason and a confidence, and applying is a separate, explicit act by someone who can read the reason.

That is not caution for its own sake. Real data is full of pairs that look like duplicates and are not:

PairVerdictWhy
Base + reverse / Base + reverse + dropoutreviewTwo ablation configurations, not one entity
WMT / WMT translation tasks between…reviewTwo granularities
Bahdanau et al., 2015 / Bahdanau et al., 2015; Jean et al., 2015reviewA citation list and one of its members
our input-feeding approach / input-feeding approachmergeIdentical once a leading determiner is dropped

The detectors, and what each is allowed to conclude

Length alone is a bad detector — WMT 2014 English-to-German translation task is 43 characters and perfectly good. What distinguishes a fragment is structure:

SignalVerdictRationale
Formula notationdrop high=, ∑, ∈, arithmetic — an equation, not a name
Finite clause, >45 charsdrop high"we", "which", "that" inside a long string is running prose
Ends on a prepositiondrop highThe extracted span was cut
Ends mid-infinitivedrop high"…through a task to predict"
Opens with a bare articledrop highEntity names rarely do; sentences lifted from a paragraph almost always do
Citation markerreviewMay be a genuine referenced work the graph should keep
No edges at allreviewUsually a real entity whose paper has not been rebuilt yet
>80 chars, no structural tellreview lowSuspicious, not condemned
Every length rule is gated, for one specific reason 3D Gaussian Splatting ends in a participle and is a perfectly good entity. Each truncation rule therefore fires only above 45 characters. A rule that is right about the corpus and wrong about the flagship example is not right.

Two bugs the tool found in itself

War story · the survivor chosen by length The first version proposed merging previous state-of-the-art models into previous state-of-the-art  — keeping a truncated fragment and discarding the good name. "Shorter is better" is the usual case, because the longer name is longer where the extractor swept in surrounding words. It fails exactly where it matters. The survivor is now chosen by quality first: a name the detectors would reject can never win.
War story · two truncated clauses that scored as clean "a model architecture eschewing recurrence and instead relying" and "…through a task to predict" both passed the first detector set — no clause marker from the list, no trailing preposition. The tests caught them, which is the point of writing the tests from real names rather than from imagined ones. Both tells (opening article, trailing infinitive) are now encoded.

The result on this corpus

Scanned 579 node(s) in "nlp": 310 finding(s)
  23 drop  · high confidence   — formulae and sentence fragments
  23 merge · 1 high, 22 medium — determiner-identical and generic-word variants
 264 review                    — citations, orphans, and nested names
Nothing was changed.
Dismissals persist, deliberately A rejected finding is marked dismissed rather than deleted, so the next audit does not re-propose what a human already refused. A tool that keeps asking the same question gets ignored — which is how a quality system stops working without anyone deciding to switch it off.

Surfaces

EndpointScopeNotes
POST /api/graph/auditwriteQueues an audit job. Returns 202 immediately.
GET /api/graph/findingsidentityOrdered by confidence, so an operator working top-down is not asked to adjudicate the hard cases while warming up.
POST /api/graph/findings/applywriteapply[] / dismiss[]. Every id is checked to belong to the caller's domain first — a finding id is otherwise a way to delete a node in a domain you cannot read.
18
Chapter 18 · Read path

Retrieval on one page

The whole pipeline, the bound on every stage, and the proof that the bound holds. If you read one technical page of this book, make it this one.

Seven stages. Every one bounded by a named parameter, never by corpus size — and the bound is reported.

The defaults, and what each one trades

ParameterDefaultRaising it buys / costs
seedK8More entry points into the graph ⇒ better recall on ambiguous questions; more subgraph to expand and more diluted restart mass. The cheapest dial to sweep.
expandHops3Defines what "multi-hop" means here. Three hops covers "what improves something that extends X". Four risks pulling in most of a well-connected domain; two loses the capability that justifies the graph.
maxSubgraphNodes4000Ceiling on the PPR working set. PPR is O(iters × edges), so this is the direct latency dial for stage 4.
topNodes25How many PPR-ranked nodes seed the graph evidence signal. Also the only nodes whose names get fetched.
maxCandidates400The one that matters most for latency. MMR is quadratic-ish; doubling this roughly quadruples stage 6.
maxEvidence10Items returned. Directly the token bill of the verbalize call.
maxChars2000Hard character budget for the assembled context. The eval harness sets this to MAX_SAFE_INTEGER so that ranking is judged rather than the budget.
weights2/1/1graph/vector/lexical. Chosen by sweep; see ch. 49.
pprAlpha0.15Chosen by sweep; see ch. 49. Exposed specifically so the harness can sweep it.

Why the bound survives multi-hop reasoning

The obvious objection to bounding expansion is that it must break the thing the graph was for. It does not, and the argument is worth having explicitly:

The argument Three hops from the seeds is the same reachable set PPR would have explored — minus the mass it would have spent on unreachable regions of the graph. PPR's own damping means a node k hops away receives on the order of (1−α)k of the seed mass; at α=0.15 that is 0.61 at two hops, 0.52 at three, and it keeps shrinking. Nodes beyond the horizon were never going to rank. Expanding the whole domain would compute mass for tens of thousands of nodes in order to discard all but 25 of them.

The measurement backs it: the working set stayed essentially flat as the corpus doubled from 50k to 100k entities (1,044 → 1,122 subgraph nodes), while multi-hop recall stayed at 100%. Bounded and complete are not in tension here.

The empty-corpus path

If annSeeds returns nothing, retrieval returns a fully-formed empty result with stats zeroed — it does not throw, and it does not proceed to expand an empty frontier. A DB test asserts it ("an empty domain returns nothing rather than throwing"). Small, but it is the difference between a new tenant's first query returning evidence: [] and returning a 500.

19
Chapter 19 · Read path

Query embedding & space integrity

Stage one is a single line — embedOne(question, 'query') — followed by an assertion that is worth more than the line. This chapter is about why a system with vectors in it needs a declared, enforced coordinate system, and what happens when it does not have one.

The failure this prevents

Two vectors from different models are not comparable. That sounds obvious and is universally understood in the abstract. The problem is that nothing about the failure looks like a failure: you get a float in [-1, 1], distributed plausibly, ranked confidently, with every dashboard green. Flip EMBED_PROVIDER on a populated corpus and retrieval quietly degrades to noise while every metric keeps reporting health.

Manifold declares the space once and enforces it at three separate points:

On writeassertVectorShape

Every vector returned by embed() is checked for exact width and for non-finite components. Failing at the call that produced it — where the model and operation are still in scope — beats failing three layers deeper in a similarity loop.

On comparisonassertComparable

dot, cosine and therefore topK and MMR refuse mismatched operands with a message naming both widths and the remedy: "Re-embed the corpus after changing EMBED_PROVIDER."

At startupcheckVectorStorage

Reads pg_attribute.atttypmod to learn the column's declared dimension and compares it against the configured space. A mismatch means inserts would fail one row at a time under load while reads returned nothing.

The space id, and why it is stored with every vector

export const EMBEDDING_SPACE = {
  provider, model,
  get dimensions() { return resolveDimensions(model); },
  get id() { return `${provider}:${model}:${resolveDimensions(model)}`; },   // "ollama:nomic-embed-text:768"
};

node_vectors.space and propositions.space record that string on every row. It is never read in the hot path. It exists so that a mixed-space corpus — the failure the other three checks prevent — is detectable after the fact, by one GROUP BY space. Recording enough to diagnose a failure you have already prevented is cheap insurance, and almost nobody does it.

KNOWN_DIMENSIONS: refusing to guess

const KNOWN_DIMENSIONS = {
  'text-embedding-3-small': 1536, 'text-embedding-3-large': 3072, 'text-embedding-ada-002': 1536,
  'nomic-embed-text': 768, 'mxbai-embed-large': 1024, 'all-minilm': 384, 'snowflake-arctic-embed': 1024,
  'local-hashed-ngram': 768,
};
// Unknown model with no EMBED_DIMENSIONS override → throws at module load, naming both fixes.

An unknown model does not get a default. The vector column and its HNSW index are fixed-width — pgvector cannot build an index over a column of mixed dimension — so a guess would produce a deployment that appears to start and then fails on the first insert. The error names both remedies: set EMBED_DIMENSIONS, or add the model to the table.

One subtlety: VECTOR_DIMENSIONS (the constant Drizzle needs literally, at table-definition time) falls back to 1536 if resolution throws, so the module can still be imported for tooling — migration generation, tests — while the startup check reports the real problem. Separating "can this module load?" from "is this deployment correct?" is what lets tooling work on a misconfigured checkout.

Validating caller-supplied vectors

const qvec = opts.queryVector ?? (await embedOne(question, 'query'));
assertVectorShape(qvec, 'query vector');   // ← runs for BOTH branches

queryVector exists so callers that already hold a vector — caches, benchmarks, tests — can skip the provider round trip. That makes it unvalidated input. Checking it here fails with a message naming the deployment's space, instead of surfacing Postgres's "expected 768 dimensions, not 1536" from three calls deeper with no context about which space is correct. A DB test covers it: "a query vector from another space is rejected, not silently truncated."

Changing the embedding model: the actual procedure

1
Add the model to KNOWN_DIMENSIONS (or set EMBED_DIMENSIONS).
2
Generate a migration altering both vector(n) columns and rebuilding both HNSW indexes — an index is bound to the column's dimension.
3
Re-embed every node and proposition: POST /api/field/backfill. On a large corpus, do it per domain ({"domain": "..."}) to keep the embedding spend predictable.
4
Re-train hyperbolic coordinates and rebuild communities — both derive from the old geometry.
5
Only then flip EMBED_PROVIDER/OPENAI_EMBED_MODEL and restart.
The gap in that procedure Steps 2–5 involve a window where the column is one width and the live corpus is another. There is no zero-downtime story for this today. The real design is a second column (embedding_vec_v2), dual-write during the transition, backfill in the background, then a cutover flag and a drop — the standard expand/contract migration. Nobody has needed it yet, which is why it does not exist; it should be built before the first customer corpus, not during it.
20
Chapter 20 · Read path

ANN seeds, and HNSW properly

Stage two picks eight entry points into the graph. It is one SQL statement, and understanding why it is fast — and where it silently stops being fast — is the difference between operating this system and hoping it keeps working.

The query

// knowledge-field/retrieve.ts — annSeeds()
const domainPredicate = domainId === DEFAULT_DOMAIN_ID
  ? sql`(${nodes.domain} = ${domainId} or ${nodes.domain} is null)`   // legacy rows
  : sql`${nodes.domain} = ${domainId}`;

db.select({ id: nodes.id, name: nodes.name, type: nodes.type,
           distance: sql`${nodeVectors.embeddingVec} <=> ${literal}::vector` })
  .from(nodeVectors)
  .innerJoin(nodes, eq(nodeVectors.nodeId, nodes.id))
  .where(and(isNotNull(nodeVectors.embeddingVec), domainPredicate))
  .orderBy(sql`${nodeVectors.embeddingVec} <=> ${literal}::vector`)
  .limit(limit);                                  // 8

// score = 1 - distance, because <=> is cosine DISTANCE

The domain predicate is inside the same statement. That is not stylistic: it means the index search itself is filtered rather than the results being trimmed afterwards. Post-filtering an ANN result is the classic vector-search bug — you ask for 8 nearest, get 8 from the wrong tenant, filter them all out, and return nothing while the index reports it did its job.

How HNSW actually works

Hierarchical Navigable Small World graphs. Build a multi-layer graph where each layer is a sparser random subset of the one below. The top layer has a handful of nodes with long-range links; the bottom contains everything with short local links.

1
Enter at the top layer at a fixed entry point. Greedily walk to the neighbour closest to the query. Long edges cover distance fast.
2
Drop a layer and repeat from where you landed. Each descent refines the region.
3
At the bottom layer, run a best-first search keeping a candidate list of size ef_search, returning the top k.

Search is roughly O(log n) instead of O(n). At 100,000 vectors that is a few hundred distance computations rather than 100,000 — and, crucially, the vectors never leave Postgres.

The three parameters, and what each costs

ParameterDefaultWhen it appliesEffect
m16buildMax connections per node per layer. Higher ⇒ better recall, larger index, slower build. The dominant term in index size.
ef_construction64buildCandidate list size while building. Higher ⇒ better graph quality, much slower build, no query-time cost. Cheap to raise if you can afford the build.
hnsw.ef_search40queryThe live latency/recall dial. Set per session: SET hnsw.ef_search = 20;. Must be ≥ k.

Manifold uses the defaults — index('…').using('hnsw', table.embeddingVec.op('vector_cosine_ops')) with no parameter overrides. That is a reasonable starting point and an unexploited lever: with seedK = 8, ef_search = 40 is generous, and lowering it is explicitly named as a cheap step toward the 1M-entity latency target (ch. 64).

The failure mode that is invisible

A missing index does not break anything — it just makes it unusable Drop propositions_embedding_hnsw and every query still returns correct, identical results. Postgres falls back to a sequential scan and computes exact distances. The answers are, if anything, better (exact rather than approximate). Latency goes from 150 ms to 20 seconds.

No error. No log line. No failed test. Correctness monitoring catches nothing, because nothing is incorrect. This is why db/vector-check.ts queries pg_indexes by name at startup and reports it in /health: "Queries will still return correct results, but by sequential scan — the O(corpus) behaviour this layer exists to avoid."

The general principle: any performance-critical invariant that produces correct-but-slow behaviour when violated must be asserted explicitly, because no functional test will ever notice.

Where seeds come from, and what that implies

Seeds are nodes, not propositions — entities like "3D Gaussian Splatting" or "createUser". So a node embedding is computed from name + description, which is short. Two consequences:

  • Seeding is name-similarity. A question phrased entirely differently from any entity name will seed poorly, and the graph signal starts from the wrong place. This is precisely why the vector and lexical signals over propositions exist alongside the graph one — they do not depend on the seeding being right.
  • Seed scores become restart weights. seeds.set(s.id, s.score) where score is 1 − distance, and PPR normalises. A seed matching at 0.9 gets nearly twice the restart mass of one matching at 0.5. The ANN ranking is not discarded after selection; it shapes the walk.

Note the guard: if (s.score > 0). A negative cosine (angle beyond 90°) would otherwise be a negative restart weight, which is meaningless — PPR's restart vector must be a distribution.

21
Chapter 21 · Read path

Bounded neighbourhood expansion

Take eight seeds, walk three hops, stop. This stage is where the multi-hop capability physically lives, and it is also where the isolation boundary is enforced most carefully — because a traversal is exactly the thing that walks across boundaries if you let it.

One round trip per hop

const sourceNodes = alias(nodes, 'expand_source');
const targetNodes = alias(nodes, 'expand_target');

const rows = await db.select({ id: edges.id, sourceId: edges.sourceId,
                              targetId: edges.targetId, confidence: edges.confidence })
  .from(edges)
  .innerJoin(sourceNodes, eq(edges.sourceId, sourceNodes.id))
  .innerJoin(targetNodes, eq(edges.targetId, targetNodes.id))
  .where(and(
    or(inArray(edges.sourceId, frontier), inArray(edges.targetId, frontier)),
    domainWhere(edges.domain, domainId),
    domainWhere(sourceNodes.domain, domainId),      // ← both
    domainWhere(targetNodes.domain, domainId)       // ← endpoints
  ))
  .limit(maxNodes * 4);   // hub fan-out cap

The double self-join proves both endpoints are in-domain inside the same statement. Previously this was a second query per hop — doubling latency for a check the database can do in the join. Three hops meant six round trips; now it means three.

Why both endpoints, not just the edge

Invariant 2, stated as a mechanism An edge carries a single domain stamp. If a boundary-crossing edge ever exists — from a bug, a migration, a partial backfill — it carries one side's stamp, so an edge-only filter passes it and drags the out-of-domain node in. And once that node is in the visited set, the next hop expands from it, pulling in its entire neighbourhood.

One corrupt row does not leak one node; it leaks a connected component. That asymmetry — small cause, large blast radius — is why the check is applied on both endpoints in four separate places (retrieve.ts, graph.ts subgraph, graph.ts node detail, MCP get_subgraph) rather than being assumed away.

Two caps, two different attacks

maxSubgraphNodes = 4000total

Checked at the top of each hop and before adding each node, setting truncated = true. Bounds the PPR working set, which is the direct latency driver of stage 4.

LIMIT maxNodes * 4per hop

The one people forget. A hub node can have tens of thousands of edges. Without a per-hop limit, a single hop from a hub pulls all of them into memory before the total cap is even consulted — reintroducing exactly the unbounded read this design exists to remove.

The ×4 ratio assumes an average degree around 4 for the useful part of the graph. It is a heuristic, and it is honest to say so: at very high average degree, expansion truncates and the truncated flag is the only signal. That flag is returned to the caller and asserted by a DB test, which is the minimum acceptable handling of a heuristic bound.

Traversal is undirected

or(inArray(sourceId, frontier), inArray(targetId, frontier)) — an edge is followed in both directions. "Method A extends method B" makes B relevant to a question about A and A relevant to a question about B. Directionality is preserved in the stored edge and available to any consumer that wants it; it just does not constrain relevance propagation. Getting this backwards is a common and quiet mistake: a directed-only walk from a leaf finds nothing, and the failure looks like "the graph is sparse".

What the caller sees

"stats": { "seedCount": 8, "subgraphNodes": 1122, "subgraphEdges": 1446,
           "candidateEvidence": 130, "truncated": false }

Returning the working set to the caller is unusual and correct. It makes invariant 12 observable in production rather than only in tests: if subgraphNodes starts climbing with corpus size, something has regressed toward whole-corpus reads. It is also the honest answer to "how much did you actually look at?", which is the first thing a technical buyer asks about a retrieval claim.

22
Chapter 22 · Read path

Personalized PageRank

The mathematics is chapter 8. This chapter is about the engineering around it: what it operates on, why it runs in memory rather than in SQL, how it is bounded, and the two things it deliberately does not do.

The setup

const seeds = new Map<string, number>();
for (const s of seedRows) if (s.score > 0) seeds.set(s.id, s.score);

const ppr = personalizedPageRank(nodeIds, graphEdges, seeds,
  opts.pprAlpha !== undefined ? { alpha: opts.pprAlpha } : {});

nodeIds and graphEdges come from expansion — typically ~1,100 nodes and ~1,450 edges. Thirty iterations over that is roughly 45,000 multiply-adds: single-digit milliseconds, in JavaScript, on one thread. Which answers the obvious question about why it is not in SQL:

Why PPR runs in Node and not in Postgres A recursive CTE could do it, but iterative numeric algorithms in SQL are awkward to write, hard to read, and hard to profile — and the data is already in memory from expansion, so moving it back to the database costs a round trip to save nothing. The rule that produced this decision: put work where the data already is, and put iteration where iteration is cheap to express.

This flips if maxSubgraphNodes ever needs to be 100,000, at which point moving the working set across the wire dominates and the calculus reverses. That is the revisit trigger.

Three deliberate simplifications

SimplificationConsequenceWhy it is acceptable here
Undirected propagationAn edge conducts mass both ways.Relevance is symmetric even when the relation is not. Direction is preserved in the data for anyone who needs it.
Edge type is ignoredAn extends edge and a cites edge conduct identically at equal confidence.Honest gap. cites is a much weaker signal of relevance than extends, and type-weighted PPR is an obvious, unbuilt, sweepable improvement — the harness already exists to evaluate it.
Fixed 30 iterationsNo convergence check; occasionally more work than needed.Determinism. The same input always yields the same ranking, which is what makes eval reproducible and a regression attributable.

Reading a PPR result

The top 25 nodes come back as rankedNodes, and only those have their names fetched (missingMeta → one inArray query). A subtle efficiency: PPR scores 1,100 nodes, and the system pays to look up names for 25.

/api/field/query returns topNodes: rankedNodes.slice(0, 10) alongside the answer. That is a genuine debugging affordance: if an answer is wrong, the ranked node list tells you immediately whether retrieval went to the wrong region of the graph (a seeding or expansion problem) or found the right region and picked the wrong evidence (a fusion or MMR problem). Two very different fixes, and without that list you cannot tell them apart.

Forward reference The restart probability α is the most-tuned parameter in this system and the site of its most instructive mistake. That story is chapter 49, and it is the one to read if you only read one chapter of Part VII.
23
Chapter 23 · Read path

Three signals, and rank fusion

The stage that took retrieval from "differentiated but losing on easy questions" to "best or tied-best on every question family". It is also the clearest example in this codebase of a design that was discovered by measurement rather than chosen by reasoning.

The three signals and what each is uniquely good at

graphGIN on node_ids

"What do we know about the entities this question is about?"

jsonb_exists_any(node_ids,
  ARRAY[$1,$2,…]::text[])

The only signal that reaches an answer two hops away whose wording has nothing in common with the question. 100% recall on multi-hop where the other two score 0%.

vectorHNSW

"What reads like the question?"

ORDER BY embedding_vec <=> $q

Catches paraphrase, where exact lexical matching fails. The signal that degrades most gracefully when the question is phrased unlike anything in the corpus.

lexicalGIN on tsvector

"What contains these exact words?"

to_tsvector('english', text)
  @@ plainto_tsquery('english', $q)
ORDER BY ts_rank(…) DESC

Near-perfect on direct lookups, where the graph pipeline alone buried the answer at rank eight. 100% nDCG on the hub family.

Why plainto_tsquery and not to_tsquery plainto_tsquery treats the input as a bag of words. to_tsquery parses it as an operator expression — so &, |, ! and : in a user question become syntax, and a question containing an ampersand throws a parse error from inside the retrieval hot path. Punctuation in user input must never become syntax. Same class of reasoning as parameterising SQL, applied one level up.

Per-signal depth, not a shared budget

const perSignal = max(1, ceil(maxCandidates / 2));   // 200 each from a 400 cap

Each signal gets maxCandidates / 2, so together they may return up to 1.5× the cap before deduplication — and the fused set is capped afterwards. That looks like sloppy budgeting and is the opposite:

War story · the shared budget that lost a multi-hop answer Splitting one 400-item budget three ways gave each signal ~133 slots. That shortened the graph list enough to drop a multi-hop answer out of the candidate set entirely — recall 100% → 95%. The answer was never even a candidate for fusion to rank.

The thing that actually needs bounding is what reaches MMR, because MMR is quadratic. What each signal proposes is cheap. Budgeting the input to a cheap stage in order to protect an expensive one two steps later is a false economy that costs recall silently.

The graph list must be re-sorted

const pprMassOf = (id: string): number =>
  (byId.get(id)?.nodeIds ?? []).reduce((sum, nodeId) => sum + (ppr.get(nodeId) ?? 0), 0);

const graphRanking = [...rankings.graph].sort((a, b) => pprMassOf(b) - pprMassOf(a));

The GIN query returns rows in whatever order the index yields — not ranked. RRF consumes positions, so an unranked list contributes noise dressed as signal: position 1 would be an arbitrary row, and the fusion would weight it as the graph signal's most confident pick.

The ranking function is the sum of PPR mass over the node ids a proposition mentions. A proposition connecting two highly-ranked entities outranks one mentioning a single peripheral node. This is where node-level PageRank becomes evidence-level ranking — the one line that connects stage 4 to stage 5, and the reason PPR matters at all to the final output.

Fusion and normalisation

const fused = reciprocalRankFusion([
  { name: 'graph',   ids: graphRanking,     weight: 2 },
  { name: 'vector',  ids: rankings.vector,  weight: 1 },
  { name: 'lexical', ids: rankings.lexical, weight: 1 },
], { limit: maxCandidates });

const relevanceById = normalizeFusedScores(fused);   // → [0,1] for MMR's lambda to mean anything

FusedResult.sources records which rankers surfaced each item and at what rank — the explanation of the score. Nothing currently surfaces it through the API, and it should: "this evidence was ranked 3rd by graph and 1st by lexical" is precisely the debugging information you want when a ranking looks wrong, and precisely the transparency an enterprise buyer asks for. A small, high-value unbuilt improvement.

A weight of 0 makes RRF skip a list entirely (if (weight === 0) continue) — which is how the eval harness isolates single signals (field (graph only) sets lexical: 0). The mechanism for disabling a signal in production and the mechanism for measuring its contribution are the same mechanism, which is why the graph-only arm can be kept permanently and cheaply.

Ties are broken deterministically

fused.sort((a, b) => {
  if (b.score !== a.score) return b.score - a.score;
  return a.id < b.id ? -1 : a.id > b.id ? 1 : 0;   // stable across runs
});

RRF produces exact ties frequently — 1/(60+3) from one ranker equals 1/(60+3) from another. Without an explicit tie-break, order depends on Map insertion order, which depends on the order three concurrent-ish queries returned. A benchmark that flickers is a benchmark nobody trusts, and a ranking that flickers is a support ticket.

24
Chapter 24 · Read path

MMR compression

400 candidates in, at most 10 items and 2,000 characters out. This is the stage that converts a ranking into a context — and it is the only quadratic step in the read path, which is why everything upstream is capped.

The loop

while (selected.length < maxItems && remaining.length > 0) {
  let bestIdx = -1, bestScore = -Infinity;

  for (let i = 0; i < remaining.length; i++) {
    const cand = remaining[i];
    if (usedCost + cand.cost > maxCost) continue;      // budget check per candidate

    let maxSimToSelected = 0;
    for (const v of selectedVectors) {
      const sim = cosine(cand.vector, v);
      if (sim > maxSimToSelected) maxSimToSelected = sim;
    }
    const mmr = lambda * cand.relevance - (1 - lambda) * maxSimToSelected;
    if (mmr > bestScore) { bestScore = mmr; bestIdx = i; }
  }

  if (bestIdx === -1) break;                            // nothing fits the budget
  ...
}

Cost: maxItems × maxCandidates × maxItems cosine computations in the worst case — at 10 × 400 × 10 with 768-dimension vectors that is roughly 30 million multiply-adds. Single-digit milliseconds, but it scales as the product of two caps, and doubling maxCandidates doubles it linearly while doubling maxItems doubles it quadratically. That is the arithmetic behind the cap.

Three design details

Budget check per candidate

continue, not break. An oversized candidate is skipped and a smaller one may still fit. Breaking would end selection at the first long proposition and leave budget unused.

λ = 0.7

Relevance dominates 7:3. At λ=1 you get top-k with duplicates; at λ=0.5 diversity starts overriding relevance and off-topic evidence appears. Never swept. It should be — the harness supports it trivially.

Injected relevance

Uses the fused score, not cosine(query, vector). Recomputing would discard the graph and lexical signals one line before the end. The vector is still needed — for redundancy between selected items, a different question.

Pre-sorting is not redundant

const candidates = fused.map(...)   // already in fused rank order

MMR scans all remaining candidates each round, so order should not matter — except that > (strict) means the first candidate wins a tie. Feeding candidates in fused order means that when λ is high and MMR scores tie, the fused ranking survives. A one-word detail (> vs >=) with a real behavioural consequence, and it is documented in the code.

Candidates without vectors are filtered out

.filter((c): c is NonNullable<typeof c> =>
   c !== null && Array.isArray(c.vector) && c.vector.length > 0)

A proposition with a NULL embedding (created before backfill, or embedded during a provider outage) cannot participate in redundancy measurement. It is dropped rather than defaulted to a zero vector — which would be maximally dissimilar to everything and therefore always selected first. Missing data must never be silently coerced into a value that happens to win. That failure mode is subtle, common, and catastrophic for ranking.

What MMR is standing in for

The state of the art in context compression is learned soft-prompt compression (ICAE, gist tokens): train a model to compress a long context into a handful of embedding vectors the consumer model reads directly. That requires access to model internals, which an API does not give you.

MMR is the buildable equivalent — it optimises the same objective (maximum information per token) with pure vector arithmetic and zero training. That framing is worth internalising: when the ideal technique needs capabilities you do not have, the question is not "how do we get those capabilities" but "what is the closest thing that optimises the same objective with what we do have?"

25
Chapter 25 · Read path

Verbalization: the one call

Fifteen lines, one model call, and the entire product claim resting on the word ONLY in the system prompt. 

export async function fieldQuery(question, opts = {}): Promise<FieldQueryResult> {
  const result = await retrieveField(question, opts);
  const verbalize = opts.verbalize !== false;

  if (!verbalize || result.evidence.length === 0) return { ...result, answer: '', llmCalls: 0 };

  const context = result.evidence.map((e, i) => `[${i + 1}] ${e.text}`).join('\n');

  const system = 'You are a knowledge-graph assistant. Answer the question using ONLY the ' +
                 'numbered evidence provided. Be concise. If the evidence is insufficient, say so.';
  const user = `Question: ${question}\n\nEvidence:\n${context}\n\nAnswer:`;

  const completion = await generateCompletion(system, user, 0.2, 'verbalize');
  return { ...result, answer: completion.text.trim(), llmCalls: 1 };
}

Five decisions in fifteen lines

DecisionReasoning
Empty evidence ⇒ 0 calls, empty answerAsking a model to answer from nothing invites it to answer from its training data — producing a confident, ungrounded, uncitable answer that looks exactly like a grounded one. The system would be paying to be wrong.
Numbered evidence [1] … [n]Gives the model a citation vocabulary. Also gives a grader one: a groundedness metric can check whether every claim maps to a numbered item. The numbering is infrastructure for a metric that does not exist yet (ch. 51).
temperature 0.2This is transcription-with-synthesis, not creative writing. Near-deterministic output also makes answer regressions attributable to retrieval changes rather than to sampling.
"If the evidence is insufficient, say so"An explicit escape hatch. Without it, a model handed weak evidence will pad rather than decline — models are strongly biased toward producing an answer.
llmCalls in the responseThe product's central claim, returned as a field the caller can assert on. Making the headline metric machine-readable is how a claim stays true through refactors.

The two-mode API

GET /api/field/retrieve?q=…0 generative calls

Ranked evidence, seeds, top nodes, stats. For another agent, which does its own reasoning and does not want Manifold's prose in its context window.

POST /api/field/query0 or 1

The same, plus an answer. For a human, or an agent that wants a summary. verbalize: false collapses it to the first mode.

That split is the difference between a component and an application. Most RAG systems only offer the second mode, which makes them impossible to compose — you cannot use them as a retrieval layer because they insist on generating. The MCP surface mirrors it exactly: search_knowledge is always zero-call, query_field takes a verbalize flag defaulting to false.

What is missing here

  • No streaming. The call is awaited whole, so perceived latency is total latency. Time to first token is the metric that matters for a human-facing answer, and it is not measured because it cannot be — stream: false is hardcoded in both providers. This is the single biggest UX gap in the read path (ch. 41).
  • No citation post-processing. The model is told to use numbered evidence; nothing verifies that it did, and nothing maps [3] back to a sources row for the caller. The data to do it is right there in evidence[i].nodeIds.
  • No groundedness check. The system cannot currently answer "is every claim in this answer supported by the evidence returned?" — which is the second question any buyer asks after "is it cheaper?".
  • No result caching. Agent traffic is repetitive. A cache keyed on (domain, quantised query embedding) is named in the plan and unbuilt.
26
Chapter 26 · Read path

Hyperbolic hierarchy

A second geometry, trained on the same graph, answering a question the first one cannot: "what generalises this, and what specialises it?"

Why a second geometry at all

Euclidean embeddings encode similarity. They do not encode subsumption. "Mip-Splatting" and "3D Gaussian Splatting" are similar; nothing in a cosine score says one is a specialisation of the other. And similarity is symmetric while subsumption is not — the relation you want is directional and the metric you have is not.

Hyperbolic space encodes it for free, via the norm. Chapter 8 covers the mathematics; here is the query it enables: 

// hyperbolic.ts — two lines that turn a metric into a taxonomy
neighbors.sort((a, b) => a.distance - b.distance);
const near = neighbors.slice(0, k * 3);
return {
  generalizations: near.filter(n => n.norm <  targetNorm).slice(0, k),   // closer to origin = more abstract
  specializations: near.filter(n => n.norm >= targetNorm).slice(0, k),
};

Take the 3k nearest neighbours by Poincaré distance, then split on norm relative to the target. Nobody labelled anything; the abstraction level is learned from the shape of the edge set.

Training, and the parts that are load-bearing

ElementValueWhy
Edges usedhierarchicalEdgeTypesPer domain. Research: extends, improves, cites. API surface: belongs_to, exposes — "an endpoint belonging to a capability is the hierarchy that matters here". The same algorithm learns a different taxonomy per domain because it is fed different edges.
Dimensions10Hyperbolic space is dramatically more efficient for trees; 10 dimensions here does what 100+ Euclidean dimensions would.
Negatives5Per positive edge. Contrastive: push the positive close, push five random nodes away.
Burn-in10 epochs @ lr/10Standard for Poincaré training. Early large steps near the origin destabilise everything that follows.
Riemannian rescale(1−‖θ‖²)²/4Essential, not optional. Near the ball boundary a Euclidean-sized step is an enormous hyperbolic move. Without the metric tensor the optimisation diverges.
project()after every updateClips back inside the ball. At ‖θ‖ = 1 the distance formula divides by zero and every subsequent value is NaN.

Scoping: three separate places

GET /api/field/hierarchy/:nodeId resolves the node's own domain, 404s if a requested scope disagrees, and filters coordinates to that domain. The comment in field.ts names the reason:

Why hierarchy is especially unsafe to leak across domains "Poincaré coordinates are trained per domain, so mixing them compares positions from unrelated embeddings." This endpoint previously loaded every node in the database and happily returned another field's entities as "generalizations".

That is worse than an ordinary leak. Two independently-trained Poincaré balls have no shared origin and no shared scale — comparing a norm from one against a norm from another is not merely unauthorised, it is meaningless. The output would be confident, structured nonsense. A leak that also corrupts the semantics is the worst of both categories.

Honest status

  • Batch, not incremental. POST /api/field/train-hyperbolic retrains from scratch. New papers do not update coordinates until someone reruns it. Known gap.
  • Math.random() in init and negative sampling ⇒ non-deterministic across runs. Every other stochastic thing here was made deterministic; this was not. A seeded PRNG (one exists already in seed-load.ts) would make hierarchy results reproducible.
  • Persistence is a loop of single-row updates — one UPDATE … RETURNING per node. At 100k nodes that is 100k round trips. A single UPDATE … FROM (VALUES …) would be orders of magnitude faster. It has not mattered yet because training is on-demand; it will.
  • Not in the retrieval path. Hyperbolic coordinates power one endpoint and nothing else. Using hierarchy to re-rank — preferring evidence at the right abstraction level for the question — is an obvious and unexplored idea.
27
Chapter 27 · Read path

Communities & cached summaries

The one place in the field pipeline that adds a language-model call — and it is worth including precisely because the reasoning for adding it is the same reasoning that removed calls everywhere else.

The problem it solves

Some questions are not about entities. "What are the main research directions here?" has no useful seed, no traversal, no top-k. Answering it from propositions means reading a large fraction of the corpus every time it is asked.

The GraphRAG answer: cluster the graph, summarise each cluster once, cache the summaries. A broad question then reads N summaries instead of N thousand propositions. The call is not eliminated — it is amortised, computed once and reused across every future query. Same objective, different lever.

The pipeline

1
Load all in-domain nodes and edges. Batch operation — a full scan is acceptable here in a way it never is in the hot path.
2
Label propagation, 10 iterations max, early exit on no change (ch. 8).
3
Group by label; discard clusters below minSize = 3 — a two-node "community" is an edge.
4
Delete previous communities for this domain only (domainWhere), then rebuild. Rebuilding one domain must not wipe another's.
5
For each cluster: member names + up to 8 sample propositions → one summary call → embed the summary → insert.

The fallback that does not lie

let summary = `Cluster of: ${memberNames.slice(0, 12).join(', ')}`;   // mechanical default
try {
  const completion = await generateCompletion(system, user, 0.3, 'community-summary');
  if (completion.text.trim()) { summary = completion.text.trim(); summarized++; }
} catch (e) {
  console.warn('Community summary failed, using member-list fallback:', e);
}

Three things right in nine lines. The community is still created if summarisation fails — a cluster with a member list is useful. The summarized counter only increments when a real summary came back, so the response distinguishes "12 communities, 12 summarized" from "12 communities, 0 summarized" — the second means the model was down and you should rerun. And the failure is logged, not swallowed. Graceful degradation that is honest about having degraded is the pattern; graceful degradation that reports success is invariant 5 violated with extra steps.

Built, and not wired in

The honest gap communities rows are written, embedded, and never read by retrieval. retrieveField does not consult them. There is no GET /api/field/communities. The infrastructure for the broad-question case exists and is not connected to anything.

The wiring is small and obvious: embed the query, ANN against communities.embedding, and if the best community similarity exceeds the best proposition similarity by some margin, the question is broad — return summaries instead of propositions. That routing decision is itself a geometric comparison, which fits the system's grain exactly. It is unbuilt, and it is a good first task for someone new to the codebase.

Two smaller notes: communities.embedding is jsonb, not vector — the only vector column that was not migrated, so it cannot be ANN-searched today. And label propagation is genuinely stochastic across runs even with the correct shuffle, so community boundaries move between rebuilds. For summaries that is acceptable; if communities ever become user-visible entities with stable names, it will not be.

28
Chapter 28 · Read path

What retrieval costs, measured

The 142× improvement, and — far more useful — the two things it revealed that no amount of reasoning would have. This chapter is the strongest argument in the book for measuring before optimising, because the biggest single win came from a storage decision nobody thought was a performance decision.

Before and after

p50p95Work per query
whole-corpus (before)21,021 ms27,435 ms100k nodes · 100k vectors · 300k edges · 100k propositions ≈ 1.5 GB moved per question
indexed (after)153 ms193 ms8 seeds · 1,122 subgraph nodes · 1,446 edges · 130 candidates

100,000 entities / 300,000 edges / 100,000 propositions, 768-dimension vectors. Reproduce with pnpm --filter api seed:load -- --nodes 100000 then pnpm --filter api bench:retrieval.

The code that made it 21 seconds

// The old retrieve.ts — note the missing WHERE clause entirely
const vecRows = (
  await db.select({ nodeId: nodeVectors.nodeId, embedding: nodeVectors.embedding })
          .from(nodeVectors)                      // ← every vector in the DATABASE
).filter((v) => nodeIdSet.has(v.nodeId));       // ← filtered in JavaScript, after transfer

Not "unoptimised" — structurally impossible at scale. Vectors were JSONB, so each 1,536-dimension embedding serialised to roughly 30 KB of decimal text that Postgres encoded and Node parsed, per query. The arithmetic that should have been done before writing this code:

EntitiesVector bytes / queryCosine ops / queryViability
1,000~31 MB1.5 Mworks — demo size
10,000~307 MB15 Mseconds/query, GC pressure
100,000~3,072 MB154 Mfails
1,000,000~30 GB1.5 Bfails
The lesson, stated as a rule Any operation whose cost is proportional to corpus size is a bug the moment the corpus is a variable. It works perfectly in every demo and fails on the first real customer — and it fails silently for a while, getting slower by a few hundred milliseconds a week, which is exactly the curve nobody alerts on.

Cost-model your hot path on paper before you write it. The table above takes ten minutes and would have prevented all of this.

Finding 1 · Storing the same data twice was a latency bug

The schema kept both embedding jsonb and embedding_vec vector(768) during the migration. That looks like harmless redundancy. It was a 1.8× p50 regression with no change in work done: 

JSONB float storage   ~15 KB per 768-dim vector   (each float as decimal text)
pgvector float4       ~ 3 KB per 768-dim vector   (packed binary)
                      ─────────────────────────
per table @ 100k        ~1 GB  vs  ~300 MB

HNSW indexes           ~400 MB  ← must stay in the buffer cache to be fast
shared_buffers          1 GB    ← 2 GB of dead JSONB evicted them
The mechanism The JSONB columns were never read after the migration. They still destroyed performance, because table pages compete with index pages for the buffer cache. Doubling the corpus cost 1.8× p50 latency even though the per-query working set had not changed at all.

Unused data is not free. It occupies the cache your hot path depends on. This is the single least intuitive finding in this book, and it generalises: wide tables with rarely-read columns, oversized text blobs, denormalised copies "just in case" — all of them tax every query that touches the table, whether or not they select the column.

Migration 0001 drops them, and it refuses to run unless every row already has a populated embedding_vec.

Finding 2 · The defaults ship broken for this workload

# docker-compose.yml — both values are load-bearing, both found by measurement
shm_size: 1gb                    # Docker default: 64 MB
command:
  - postgres
  - -c
  - shared_buffers=1GB           # Postgres default: 128 MB
  - -c
  - effective_cache_size=3GB     # planner hint, not an allocation
  - -c
  - maintenance_work_mem=512MB   # index builds and VACUUM
shared_buffers: 128 MB → 1 GB

A 400 MB HNSW index cannot live in a 128 MB cache. Every ANN search hits disk. Size this to your index, not to your row count — the usual "25% of RAM" heuristic is derived from OLTP workloads and is the wrong shape for vector search, where one index must stay resident.

shm_size: 64 MB → 1 GB

Postgres uses POSIX shared memory for parallel workers. With Docker's 64 MB default, VACUUM FULL, REINDEX, and large index builds fail outright with "could not resize shared memory segment" — long before disk is a concern. A failure that looks like a Postgres bug and is a container setting.

The finding that mattered most: the working set stayed flat

Corpusp50p95Subgraph nodesReading
50,000 entities69 ms102 ms1,044Corpus doubled; work per query rose 7% while latency rose 2.2×. The algorithm is bounded. The remaining growth is storage, not algorithmic — index and table pages competing for the buffer cache.
100,000 entities153 ms193 ms1,122

This is why stats is returned to the caller. Without it you would see latency rise with corpus size and conclude the algorithm does not scale — and go rewrite the wrong thing. With it, you can prove the algorithm is bounded and the cost is in the storage layer, which has an entirely different and much cheaper set of fixes.

The diagnostic principle Instrument the work done, not just the time taken. Time is a composite of algorithm, data volume, cache behaviour and contention. Work done separates them. Two numbers — latency and working-set size — answer a question that neither answers alone.

The remaining path to 1M entities

The exit criterion is not met. The target is p95 < 150 ms at one million entities; the measurement is 193 ms at one hundred thousand. Levers, cheapest first:

1
halfvec (float16). Halves index and table size at negligible recall cost for cosine similarity. A migration plus an index rebuild. Directly attacks the cache-pressure term, which is the term that is actually growing.
2
Lower hnsw.ef_search. Currently the default 40 for k = 8 — generous. Dropping to 20 is a session variable and free to try.
3
Matryoshka-truncated dimensions. text-embedding-3-* supports truncation with graceful degradation; 768 → 512 is another ~33% off. Requires a re-embed.
4
Per-domain partitioning. Partition nodes, propositions and their vector tables by domain. Each partition gets its own smaller index, and every query already carries a domain predicate, so pruning is automatic. The isolation model and the scaling model turn out to be the same model — which is a strong signal the boundary was drawn in the right place.
5
Result caching. Keyed on domain + quantised query embedding. Agent traffic is repetitive; this is the highest-leverage change if the traffic pattern justifies it, and the lowest if it does not. Measure the repeat rate first.
Part IV

Tenancy, governance & security

The layer that turns "it works" into "someone else can safely pay for it". Domains, identity, the authorization chokepoint, the audit trail, rate limiting, a threat model written as an attacker would write it — and an honest account of the gap that remains.

29
Chapter 29 · Governance

Domains: config-as-code

A domain is an isolated knowledge space with its own ontology. Today it is a TypeScript file. That has real advantages and one disqualifying limitation, and both are worth being precise about.

What a domain is

export interface DomainConfig {
  id: string;                  // 'gaussian-splatting' — lowercase kebab, the canonical persisted value
  name: string;                // human-readable, also injected into the extraction prompt
  description: string;

  entityTypes: string[];       // PREFERRED, not enforced — types stay open (invariant 19)
  relationshipTypes: string[];

  domainContext: string;       // one sentence of expertise framing for the system prompt
  entityExamples?: Record<string, string[]>;
  relationshipExamples?: Array<{ source, type, target }>;

  hierarchicalEdgeTypes?: string[];   // which edges are a hierarchy, for Poincaré training
  seedPaperIds?: string[];            // curated bootstrap corpus
}

Four ship today, and the fourth is the interesting one:

default

Field-agnostic. Adopts legacy NULL-domain rows. The home for anything unscoped.

gaussian-splatting

25 curated seed papers, graphics-specific examples, extends/improves/cites as hierarchy.

nlp

The second research domain — its existence is what makes the isolation tests meaningful.

api-surface

api/endpoint/capability/schema/auth, hierarchy = belongs_to/exposes. Not a research domain at all — proof the abstraction generalises past its origin.

Three resolvers, three contracts

This is the most instructive API design in the codebase. One concept — "turn a string into a domain" — is three functions, because it is genuinely three different questions with three different safe answers.

FunctionInputUnknown idUse for
resolveDomainCaller-supplied (query, body, MCP arg, CLI flag)throwsNothing directly — routes must use requireDomain, which wraps it.
resolveStoredDomainA value read back out of the databasethrows, naming the rowProcessing a paper, resolving a node's domain during traversal.
getDomainAnythingfalls back to defaultPrompt context only. Never to decide what to read, write, or filter.

The resolveStoredDomain contract deserves a note. NULL is legitimate — it marks rows created before domains existed, which the default domain adopts. A non-null but unregistered value is not legitimate: it means the row was written before ingress validation existed, or a domain was deleted from the registry while its data remained. Processing it under the default ontology is exactly what caused contamination, so it throws — and the error names the row so an operator can fix or backfill it. Distinguishing "absent" from "invalid" is the whole design.

Normalisation at the edge

export function normalizeDomainId(id: string): string { return id.trim().toLowerCase(); }

" NLP " and "nlp" name the same domain, and only the normalised id is ever persisted. That is what keeps papers.domain and nodes.domain from drifting apart on nothing more than whitespace or capitalisation — two rows that should be in the same domain and are not, because a caller sent a trailing space once.

A test asserts the registry is self-consistent: "every registered domain has a normalized id". That is a meta-test in the same spirit as the routes grep — it checks a property of the configuration rather than of a function, and it catches the class rather than the instance.

The domainWhere asymmetry

export function domainWhere(column: Column, domainId: string): SQL | undefined {
  if (domainId === DEFAULT_DOMAIN_ID) return or(eq(column, domainId), isNull(column));
  return eq(column, domainId);
}

Nine lines carrying the entire backward-compatibility story. default matches its own rows and legacy NULLs; every other domain matches only its own. Nothing breaks for existing data, and no forced backfill was required. The cost is a special case that every reader of this function must know about — which is why it is nine lines with a comment rather than inlined at forty call sites.

Adding a domain, and the limitation that matters

1
New file in apps/api/src/domains/ exporting a DomainConfig.
2
One line in index.ts's registry array.
3
Done. No schema migration — the domain is a string in five text columns.
The disqualifying limitation A new vertical ships as a code release. A customer who wants their own ontology needs an engineer, a pull request, and a deploy. That is fine for four built-in domains and impossible as a product.

It compounds with tenancy: grants name domains from a global code registry, so a tenant's domain is visible-by-name to every other tenant's admin and the namespace is shared. GET /api/domains currently lists every registered domain to any authenticated caller, regardless of grants — a minor information leak today and a real one with customer-named domains.

Phase 4 moves the registry into the database with a falsifiable exit criterion: "a new vertical, with its own ontology and sources, goes live without a code deploy." The design work is mostly done — the DomainConfig interface is the table schema, and the three resolvers become cache-backed lookups with the same contracts.
30
Chapter 30 · Governance

The isolation proof

"Domains are isolated" is a claim. This chapter is the evidence — the four mechanisms that enforce it, the four bugs that each mechanism exists because of, and the 61 database-backed tests that hold it in place.

The four mechanisms

1
Fail closed on unknown. An unregistered ?domain= is a 400 naming the valid domains, at every entry point — graph, papers, field, ingest, backfill, MCP. It is never silently treated as default.
2
Stamp at write, resolved. Only the canonical registry id is persisted, on five tables. A paper and its extracted entities can never disagree about which domain they are in.
3
Filter both endpoints at read. Traversal is pinned to the centre node's domain and follows an edge only when both endpoints are in it.
4
404, not 403, for out-of-scope entities. The endpoint cannot be used to confirm other domains' entities exist.

Why 404 for nodes but 403 for domains

This looks inconsistent and is a precise, deliberate distinction — the clearest small example of threat modelling in the codebase:

Ungranted domain → 403

The domain registry is public (GET /api/domains). Naming a domain the caller cannot use leaks nothing they could not already list — and "you may not" is actionable, where "not found" sends an operator hunting for a missing row that exists.

Out-of-scope node → 404

Here the existence of the row is itself the secret. A 403 on /graph/nodes/:uuid confirms that uuid names a real entity in some other domain, turning the endpoint into an enumeration oracle. Indistinguishable-from-nonexistent is the only safe answer.

The rule: return "forbidden" when the resource identifier is public knowledge; return "not found" when the identifier's validity is itself information. Most codebases pick one and apply it everywhere, which is wrong in one direction or the other.

The tests, by what they prove

TestThe bug it holds shut
"the same entity name in two domains stays two separate nodes"Resolution scoped to the domain. attention (NLP) and attention (vision) never merge.
"subgraph traversal refuses to follow a cross-domain edge"Invariant 2. Edge-only filtering admits the far node and, from there, its entire neighbourhood.
"a node detail view only returns in-domain relationships"The same bug via a different endpoint — which is why the test is repeated per surface.
"requesting a node under the wrong domain returns 404, not 403"The enumeration oracle above.
"provenance for an edge is hidden from another domain"Provenance carries extractedText — the leakiest payload in the system.
"graph stats are scoped, not global"Aggregates leak too. "How many nodes does the other tenant have" is commercially sensitive even without names.
"an unregistered domain is a 400, not the default domain's data" — parameterised across seven surfacesThe original leak. Parameterising it across surfaces is what makes a new endpoint inherit the test.
"a paper created via the API stores the canonical (normalized) domain id"Invariant 4 — drift between paper and entities.
"no row in the database carries an unregistered domain"A whole-database assertion. Not "this endpoint behaves" but "the corpus is in a valid state". The strongest kind of test in the suite.
"a malformed id is a 404, not a 500 leaking a database error"Postgres rejects a malformed UUID literal with a query error, which the catch blocks turned into a 500 — so /graph/nodes/not-a-uuid read as a server fault and echoed database internals.

Why these tests hit real Postgres

The testing decision that makes the rest credible The DB suite drives the real Hono app against real Postgres rather than mocking the query layer — "because the defects worth catching here (a scope that widens to the wrong domain, a traversal that crosses a boundary) live in the SQL, not above it."

A mocked test of domainWhere proves the function returns the SQL you expected. It cannot prove the join filtered both endpoints, that the planner used the index, or that an OR across a NULL behaved. Those are the bugs. Mocking the database in a system whose correctness lives in its queries tests the mock.

It runs against a separate knowledge_graph_test database, gated on TEST_DATABASE_URL, so it can never write to a working database — a guard worth copying, because the alternative failure mode is "the test suite deleted staging".

What isolation still is not

It is enforced in the application layer only. Postgres row-level security beneath it would make a forgotten filter harmless rather than merely detectable. Chapter 36 is that design.

31
Chapter 31 · Governance

Identity: tenants, principals, keys

A credential belongs to a tenant, carries capability scopes, and names the domains it may touch. The key format, the hash choice, and the bootstrap path each embed a decision that is commonly made the other way — and each has a defensible reason.

The model

tenant  ──< principal ──> scopes:  ['read'] | ['read','write'] | ['admin']
                       └─> domains: ['nlp'] | ['nlp','api-surface'] | ['*']
                       └─> expiresAt · revokedAt · lastUsedAt

key = mk_<16 hex>_<32 random bytes, base64url>
       ns   prefix (stored clear, INDEXED)   secret (only SHA-256 stored)

Why the prefix is stored in the clear

The prefix is a lookup handle, not a secret. Without it, verifying a presented key means fetching every principal and comparing hashes — a table scan per request, and a growing timing surface as the principal count rises. With it, verification is one indexed row fetch plus one constant-time compare.
