With MODEL_EXTRACT=gpt-4o-mini at roughly 500 input + 300 output tokens per chunk, a 26-chunk paper is about 20,800 tokens: on the order of half a cent per paper. A 10,000-paper corpus is tens of dollars. That is cheap in absolute terms and it is still the dominant cost of the entire system — which is exactly why invariant 22 says to spend it deliberately.

Query cost

OperationGenerativeEmbeddingContext sent
GET /api/field/retrieve01—
POST /api/field/query11≤ 2,000 chars ≈ 500 tokens
Naive RAG equivalent1110 × 2,000-char chunks ≈ 5,000 tokens
Agentic loop equivalent3–73–7Growing context per hop
The claim, stated precisely Against naive RAG: the same number of generative calls with roughly a tenth of the context, plus multi-hop capability the baseline does not have at all.

Against an agentic loop: one call instead of three to seven, deterministic, and an order of magnitude faster.

The honest qualifier: you paid for this at ingest time. The architecture wins when the corpus is queried many times. A corpus ingested and queried twice is worse than naive RAG. Knowing where your own break-even sits is more valuable than the headline.

The break-even, computed

Ingest premium (field vs none) ≈ 26 calls/paper × 800 tokens ≈ 20,800 tokens/paper
Query saving vs naive RAG       ≈ 4,500 tokens/query (5,000 → 500 context)

Break-even ≈ 20,800 / 4,500 ≈ 4.6 queries per paper

At 10,000 papers → ~46,000 queries to break even on tokens alone.
Before counting: multi-hop capability (which the baseline cannot do at any price),
provenance, and the latency difference against an agentic loop.

Under five queries per document. For an institutional-memory or tool-selection workload — where the same corpus is queried continuously — that is reached in days. For a one-shot analysis it is never reached, and the honest answer to that customer is "use naive RAG". Knowing which customer you are not for is worth as much as knowing which you are.

What is measured, and what is not

measured
  • llmCalls per operation, per pipeline mode
  • Prompt / completion tokens (both providers)
  • Embedding calls, separately
  • Latency per operation
  • Which model served each operation
  • Context characters, field vs naive
not measured
  • Dollars. No price table. Tokens → cost is manual.
  • Per-tenant attribution. Metrics are a global in-process singleton (ch. 36).
  • Infrastructure cost. Postgres, storage, egress — ch. 65.
  • Embedding cost at scale. ~2 calls/chunk is invisible per paper and material at a million documents.
  • Re-ingest cost. A model change means re-extracting. Nothing budgets for it.

The first two are days of work and unlock billing. services/metrics.ts already records model and latency per operation; adding a price table at recordLLM time and threading the principal through turns an engineering metric into a commercial one.

Part VII

Measurement

How anything in this book is known to be true. The eval harness, the scorecard, the two tuning decisions that measurement reversed, the load harnesses — and a plain list of what remains unmeasured.

46
Chapter 46 · Measurement

Measurement as an architectural component

The eval harness is not tooling. It is load-bearing architecture, and treating it as such is one of the two or three things that most separates this codebase from a competent prototype.

The failure that motivated it

"We claim better retrieval and measure the wrong thing" GET /api/field/benchmark reported character reduction and called it a benchmark.

Compression without a quality axis is not merely incomplete — it is gameable in the wrong direction. Returning nothing scores a perfect 100%. The system could not answer "is it more accurate?", which is the first question anyone asks.

That is the archetypal proxy-metric failure: optimise the number and the product gets worse while the dashboard improves.

What makes a harness architectural rather than incidental

1
It runs anywhere. EMBED_PROVIDER=local is a deterministic lexical embedder needing no model server, no API key, no network. If measurement requires a GPU and a credential, it happens once and then never again.
2
It is reproducible. Constructed corpus with known gold answers, built and torn down per run. Same input, same numbers, on any machine.
3
It compares against alternatives. Four arms: top-k vector, Postgres full-text, field-graph-only, field-hybrid. A number with no baseline is decoration.
4
It sweeps. --sweep-alpha and --sweep-weights are built in, so tuning a parameter is a command rather than a project. The cost of measuring must be lower than the cost of arguing, or people will argue.
5
It keeps historical arms permanently. field (graph only) is retained forever so fusion's contribution stays attributable rather than assumed. Most teams delete the old implementation and lose the ability to answer "how much did that buy us?"
6
It reports what it cannot establish. The run prints: "the local provider is lexical, not semantic. Ranking here reflects retrieval mechanics, not language understanding." A harness that overstates its own scope is worse than none.

The invariant it created

Invariant 23: run pnpm --filter api eval before and after any change to PPR, MMR, fusion weights, seed count, or hop limits. That rule only exists because the harness makes it cheap. Process rules that are expensive to follow decay within a quarter; the mitigation is always to reduce the cost, never to increase the reminders.

Three harnesses, three questions

eval

Is it accurate? recall / nDCG / MRR against three competing strategies on a gold set.

bench:retrieval

Is it fast, and does it stay fast? p50/p95 with the working set reported, on a synthetic corpus of any size.

models:compare

Which model, for which role, on this hardware? Runs the system's own prompts.

Quality, latency, and cost — the three axes any retrieval system trades against each other. Having a command per axis is what turns "we think this is better" into a claim someone can check.

47
Chapter 47 · Measurement

The eval harness

A constructed corpus with known answers, three question families designed to expose different weaknesses, four competing strategies, and four standard IR metrics. This chapter is how to build one for any retrieval system, using this one as the worked example.

The three question families, and why each exists

single-hopthe control

The answer is directly about the entity named in the question. Both baselines should score near-perfect. Its job is to catch regressions — if the clever pipeline loses here, the cleverness is costing something.

multi-hopthe thesis

The answer is two typed edges away and shares no vocabulary with the question. Vector similarity cannot reach it. Keyword search cannot reach it. This family is the entire product claim, isolated.

hubthe adversary

A high-degree node dominates the neighbourhood. Designed to make the graph approach fail by drowning the relevant spoke in hub mass. Every harness needs a family designed to break your favourite approach.

The design principle for gold sets Include a family you expect to lose. A benchmark composed only of cases your approach is good at is marketing. The hub family is what surfaced the ranking deficit that motivated fusion — the single most valuable finding the harness has produced, and it came from the arm designed to embarrass the system.

The four metrics, and what each is for

MetricQuestionWhy it is in the set
recall@kDid the gold evidence appear anywhere in the top k?The headline. A retrieval layer feeding an LLM cares more about recall than precision: evidence never retrieved cannot be reasoned over, while a few extra items only cost tokens.
nDCG@kWas it ranked early?Position matters when the consumer has a finite context. Unlike recall it rewards ordering; unlike MRR it accounts for every relevant item.
MRRHow high was the first hit?Proxy for "would a human trust the first result".
precision@kHow much of what came back was relevant?The token-efficiency side of the trade.

Plus meanContextChars and p50/p95 latency on every row — because quality alone is not a scorecard. A strategy that scores 3 points higher for 10× the context and 20× the latency has not won.

Relevance is binary — retrieved or not. Graded relevance would be better and needs human labelling. "Binary is what a constructed gold set can honestly support" — being precise about the strength of your own instrument is the difference between a benchmark and a claim.

Four strategies, one interface

export interface Strategy {
  name: string; description: string;
  run: (question: string, domain: string, k: number) => Promise<{ propositionIds: string[]; contextChars: number }>;
}
ArmWhat it is
vectorTop-k ANN over propositions. The standard RAG baseline — what the field pipeline has to beat to justify existing.
keywordPostgres ts_rank. Included "because lexical search is a genuinely strong baseline that vector pipelines are often quietly worse than, and because it fails differently: where embeddings blur, BM25-style ranking is exact but brittle."
field (graph only)The pre-fusion behaviour, kept permanently.
field (hybrid)The shipping pipeline.

Three details that make the comparison fair

1
maxChars: MAX_SAFE_INTEGER in the field arm. "The default char budget is a production choice; for evaluation we let the ranking be judged rather than the budget." Otherwise you are measuring MMR's budget against baselines that have none.
2
All arms read the same corpus, in the same domain, through the same database. Comparing a domain-scoped field retrieval against a whole-database naive baseline would flatter the field arm for entirely the wrong reason — a trap the benchmark endpoint explicitly avoids too.
3
A strategy that errors scores zero rather than aborting the comparison — but the failure is printed, not swallowed. Invariant 5 applied to the measurement layer: a harness that silently drops a failing arm reports a comparison that did not happen.

The local embedding provider

// services/embed-local.ts — hashed character n-grams, FNV-1a, signed, unit-normalised
for (const feature of features) {
  const index = fnv1a(feature) % dimensions;
  const sign  = fnv1a(feature, 0x9dc5811c) % 2 === 0 ? 1 : -1;   // so unrelated features cancel
  vector[index] += sign;
}

Features are words, character trigrams (partial credit for morphology — "splatting" vs "splat"), and adjacent word pairs (a little word order). It captures lexical overlap and nothing else: "cat" and "kitten" are unrelated to it.

Two details in it worth stealing Empty input gets a fixed direction (vector[0] = 1) rather than an all-zero vector, because a zero vector has no direction — cosine against it is 0 for everything, and pgvector's HNSW cannot order by distance from the origin.

It is deliberately not a fallback. Nothing silently degrades to it; a deployment gets it only by naming it, "because a knowledge product quietly answering from lexical hashes would be worse than refusing to answer." An escape hatch that can be reached by accident is a footgun with a friendly name.

The hand-curated gold set — the second, harder corpus

Alongside the constructed corpus, eval/gold.ts holds real, verifiable domain knowledge for the Gaussian-Splatting literature — 3DGS, Mip-Splatting, Scaffold-GS, Mip-NeRF360, Tanks and Temples. It is explicitly "NOT generated or placeholder data", and it measures three things the constructed corpus cannot:

ExtractionCase

A passage plus the entities and relations that should be found in it. Each gold entity is an alias set, so a correct extraction can use any accepted surface form.

This is the first instrument that measures extraction quality — the gap ch. 51 calls critical.

ResolutionCase

A canonical entity, its aliases (all of which must collapse to one node), and its distractors — which must not.

Testing both directions is what makes it a real threshold test rather than a merge test. Ch. 13's unswept SIM_THRESHOLD now has something to be swept against.

QACase

A question plus mustMention: alias groups a grounded answer has to reference at least one of, from each group.

That is the shape of a groundedness metric — the second critical gap in ch. 51.

One deliberate simplification, stated in the file "Passages are phrased with explicit relation verbs ('extends', 'evaluates on') so the score measures extraction + canonicalization fidelity, not the harness's tolerance for ambiguous phrasing."

That is the right first step and it is honest about its own limit: it isolates can the pipeline find a relation that is plainly stated from can it find one that is implied. A gold set that mixes both produces a single number that answers neither question. Phase two is passages phrased the way papers actually write — and the harness should keep both, for the same reason it keeps the graph-only arm.

Running it

EMBED_PROVIDER=local DATABASE_URL=…knowledge_graph_eval pnpm --filter api eval
pnpm --filter api eval -- --sweep-alpha       # 0.15, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 0.85
pnpm --filter api eval -- --sweep-weights     # six graph/vector/lexical combinations

The corpus is built at the start and torn down in a finally, so the run is repeatable and leaves nothing behind. Sixty questions, k=10, roughly a minute.

48
Chapter 48 · Measurement

The scorecard, read properly

The numbers, what they establish, what they do not, and how to read a scorecard without fooling yourself.

The result

Metricvectorkeywordfield (graph only)field (hybrid)
overall nDCG57.4%66.7%72.1%87.7%
overall recall65.0%66.7%98.3%100.0%
multi-hop recall0.0%0.0%95.0%100.0%
single-hop nDCG96.3%100.0%100.0%100.0%
hub nDCG75.9%100.0%63.1%100.0%
p95 latency2 ms1 ms34 ms28 ms

60 questions · k = 10 · lexical embedding space · constructed corpus with known gold.

What this establishes

The central claim is real On questions whose answer shares no vocabulary with the question and sits two typed edges away, graph traversal retrieves it every time and both baselines retrieve it never.

That is not a marginal gain. It is a capability the alternatives do not have. 100% versus 0% is not a tuning difference; it is a difference in what the system can do at all.

And the field pipeline does not pay for it on easy questions — it ties or beats both baselines on single-hop. That second sentence is what makes the first one usable: a capability you only get by sacrificing the common case is not a capability, it is a trade.

What it also establishes, less comfortably

  • Latency is 14–28× the baselines. 28 ms against 1–2 ms. In absolute terms fine; as a ratio, real. A system that only ever asks single-hop questions is paying 20× for nothing.
  • Graph-only lost to plain keyword search overall (72.1% vs 66.7% nDCG is close, and on the hub family it was 63.1% vs 100%). The clever approach was worse than ts_rank at the thing ts_rank is for. That finding is what produced fusion — and it would have been invisible without an arm nobody expected to win.
  • The corpus is constructed and the embedding space is lexical. It measures retrieval mechanics, not language understanding. With real embeddings the vector baseline would be stronger and the gaps would narrow.

How to read a scorecard without fooling yourself

1
Read the family breakdown before the overall number. An overall average hides that graph-only was best on multi-hop and worst on hub. Averages are where capabilities go to be invisible.
2
Look for 0% and 100%. They usually mean a capability boundary rather than a quality difference, and capability boundaries do not close with tuning.
3
Check the cost columns. Quality per token and quality per millisecond are the real axes. A scorecard without them is half a scorecard.
4
Ask what the corpus cannot show. Here: real language, real ambiguity, contradictory sources, adversarial input, and extraction quality — because the corpus was constructed, not extracted.
5
Ask who built the gold set. Here, the same people who built the system. That is an acknowledged limitation, not a disqualification — and it is the reason the real-corpus, human-labelled version is the top item in chapter 51.
The exit criterion is only partially met The harness is reproducible from the repository and covers four strategies and three question families. Still required before this can be published as a competitive claim: a real corpus with real embeddings and human-labelled gold, a groundedness metric, a contradiction rate, and a second domain. Saying so is what makes the numbers above worth reading.
49
Chapter 49 · Measurement

Two decisions reversed by data

The most valuable chapter in this book for anyone who wants to develop judgement, because both decisions were reasonable, well-argued, and wrong — and the first one was wrong in the specific way that is hardest to detect.

Reversal 1 · The PPR restart probability

The argument for raising α to 0.5correct in its own terms

On an undirected graph the walk component drifts toward a degree-proportional distribution. On a star graph, the seed scored 0.18 against a 12-neighbour hub's 0.46.

The node that actually matched the question was ranking below a generic hub. Raising the restart probability keeps mass on the seeds and fixes that ordering.

Every sentence of that is true and measurable.

What the harness saidthe outcome metric
familyα=0.15α=0.5
multi-hop nDCG63.1%30.1%
hub nDCG31.6%31.6%

Multi-hop nDCG halved. And the hub family — the thing the change was for — scored an identical 31.6% at every α tested.

Why it failed: the proxy had no relationship to the outcome Node-level hub dominance never propagated to evidence selection. The relevant spoke still entered the top-ranked nodes, and its proposition was still gathered — because evidence gathering asks "which propositions mention any of these 25 nodes", not "which node ranked first". Whether the seed was rank 1 or rank 4 changed nothing downstream.

Meanwhile high restart does keep mass on the seed — which is precisely what starves the distant answer a multi-hop question is asking for. So the change had no effect on its target and a large negative effect elsewhere.

The lesson: it was optimising a proxy (seed rank vs hub rank) that did not map to the outcome (was the right evidence retrieved, and ranked highly). This is the hardest class of mistake to catch, because the proxy was real, measurable, and improved. Only an outcome metric could reveal it.

Reverted to 0.15, with a twenty-line comment in ppr.ts "for the next person tempted by the same argument."

Reversal 2 · Equal fusion weights

Equal weighting across three signals is the neutral, defensible default. It cost multi-hop recall:

Weights (g/v/l)multi-hop recallmulti-hop nDCGElsewhere
1 / 1 / 195.0%54.8%unchanged
2 / 1 / 1100.0%63.1%unchanged
3 / 1 / 1100.0%63.1%unchanged — identical to 2
4 / 1 / 1100.0%63.1%unchanged — identical to 2

The graph list is the only signal that can surface an answer sharing no vocabulary with the question. Diluting it one-to-one against two lexical/semantic signals pushed those answers out of the top k. Doubling it restores multi-hop completely at no cost elsewhere.

The second-order lesson: pick the smallest value that buys the gain 3 and 4 measured identically to 2. Choosing 2 rather than 4 is not superstition — it is how you avoid accumulating parameters nobody can justify later. A value chosen because it is the smallest that works has a reason; a value chosen because it is safely large has a habit. Six months later nobody can tell the difference except by re-running the sweep, which nobody does.

Corollary: when several settings measure identically, that is information — it says the parameter has a plateau, and it tells you how much headroom you have before the next change matters.

What both reversals have in common

PropertyReversal 1Reversal 2
The argument was…Correct and measurableThe neutral default
It optimised…A node-level proxyNothing in particular
Detected by…An outcome metric on a family it was not aimed atAn outcome metric on a family nobody suspected
Cost of the mistake33 points of multi-hop nDCG5 points of multi-hop recall
Cost of finding itOne commandOne command
Recorded where20-line comment in ppr.ts12-line comment in retrieve.ts

Recording the reversal in the code, at the constant, is the part most teams skip — and it is what stops the same change being re-proposed every eighteen months by someone who has the same reasonable idea. A comment that says "0.15" teaches nothing. A comment that says "this was 0.5 for a good reason and here is the measurement that reversed it" is an argument that survives the person who made it.

50
Chapter 50 · Measurement

Load & latency harnesses

Quality is one axis. The other two — does it stay fast as the corpus grows, and which model should serve which role — each get their own command.

seed:load — a synthetic corpus of any size

pnpm --filter api seed:load -- --nodes 100000 --domain nlp

Generates a graph of the requested size with deterministic pseudo-random vectors. Three details make it a tool rather than a script:

DetailWhy
Mulberry32 PRNGDeterministic, so a run is reproducible without shipping a corpus on disk. Contrast with hyperbolic.ts, which uses bare Math.random() and is not.
Box–Muller unit vectorsNormal components give directions spread over the sphere, "which is closer to how real embeddings sit than uniform noise." Uniform-random vectors cluster near the corners of the hypercube and would make ANN look artificially easy.
Writes to DATABASE_URLWith an explicit warning to point it at a scratch database. A load generator that can be pointed at production by omission is a footgun.

Honest limitation, stated in the file: "The vectors are not semantically meaningful and this says nothing about answer quality — it exists purely to measure how retrieval behaves as the corpus grows, which was the untested axis: everything worked at demo scale and nobody had run it at a size a customer would bring."

bench:retrieval — the A/B against the old path

Runs the indexed pipeline against the previous whole-corpus implementation on the same corpus, reporting p50/p95 and the working set. That second output is what turned "it got faster" into "the algorithm is bounded and the remaining growth is storage" (ch. 28) — a distinction that determines which fix to build next.

Keeping the old implementation as a benchmark arm Most teams delete the code they replaced. Keeping it — like the field (graph only) eval arm and the legacy pipeline mode — costs a little maintenance and buys the ability to answer "how much did that actually buy us?" at any point in the future. Three instances of the same discipline in one codebase is a pattern, not an accident.

models:compare — on your hardware, with your prompts

Runs the system's own extraction and verbalization prompts against candidate models, reporting median latency and what came back (entity/relationship counts for extraction, a grounding check for verbalization). Two things it gets right:

  • It uses the real prompts. A generic benchmark tells you about the model; this tells you about the model on this task, which is the only question that matters. The measured answer inverted the intuition (ch. 43) precisely because it was task-specific.
  • It reports median, not mean. Local inference has a long tail from model loading; a mean would be dominated by the first call.

What is still missing from the measurement suite

HarnessWould answer
Concurrent load testEverything is measured sequentially. What is p95 at 50 concurrent queries? Where does the connection pool (max: 10) saturate? This is the biggest measurement gap — the system has never been measured under concurrency at all.
Ingestion throughputPapers per hour at a given JOB_CONCURRENCY, and where the bottleneck sits (model, database, arXiv throttle).
GroundednessDoes every claim in the generated answer appear in the retrieved evidence? Ch. 51.
Extraction qualityPrecision/recall of entities and relationships against a hand-labelled document. The gap that caps every other number.
Cold-startAll measurements are on a warm buffer cache. First query after a restart is unmeasured, and at 1 GB of index it will be materially different.
51
Chapter 51 · Measurement

What is still unmeasured

A short chapter, deliberately blunt. This is the list you hand a technical buyer before they find it themselves — which is both an integrity position and, in practice, a commercial advantage.

The list

UnmeasuredSeverityWhy it matters, and what it would take
Extraction qualitycritical → in progressGraph quality caps answer quality. A real run (arXiv 1706.03762, 26 chunks, qwen2.5:7b) produced a 111-node/47-edge graph with genuinely useful entities — Transformer, multi-head attention, BLEU, WMT 2014 En-De — and noise: run-on phrases stored as entities, and a relationship extracted from the acknowledgments. eval/gold.ts now supplies hand-curated ExtractionCases with alias sets; the instrument exists, the scored number does not yet.
Groundednesscritical → in progressDoes every claim in the answer appear in the returned evidence? Evidence is numbered [1]…[n] specifically to make this checkable, and gold.ts's QACase.mustMention is the assertion shape. Wiring it into the scorecard is the remaining work.
Resolution — false mergesclosedMeasured, and the result inverted the design. Every same-type pair above 0.82 in the live graph was two different things (ch. 13). Cosine alone cannot decide identity; merge-guard.ts now requires a justification and all 7 observed pairs are refused with a stated reason.
Resolution — false splitshighThe guard is deliberately conservative, so the open question flipped: how many genuine synonyms is it now keeping apart? Splits are visible and fixable, which is why they were chosen — but nobody has counted them. A merge-decision audit table would make both directions measurable; today only refusals are logged.
Contradiction ratehighWhen two sources disagree, both become edges with confidence scores. Silently averaging contradictions is the wrong behaviour for a system meant to prevent errors. agents/repair.ts now detects temporal and mutual contradictions at zero LLM cost (ch. 14) — but it has no route, no schedule, and no metric, so the rate is still unmeasured.
Real-corpus qualityhighThe scorecard is a constructed corpus in a lexical embedding space. Real embeddings, a real corpus, human-labelled gold, and a second domain.
Concurrent latencyhighNever measured under concurrency. The connection pool is max: 10.
Latency at 1M entitieshighThe stated exit criterion. Measured at 100k (193 ms p95); the target is <150 ms at 1M.
Dollar costmediumTokens are measured; cost is manual. No per-tenant attribution.
Extraction relationship densitycriticalOnly ~24% of edges are semantic (101 of 422); the rest are mentions. And some "entities" are sentence fragments, which are unmergeable by construction and inflate the node count permanently. This is now the binding constraint on graph quality (ch. 17b), and there is no harness for it.
Extraction lane widthmediumPROCESS_CONCURRENCY=1 was justified in comments with "extraction serialises inside Ollama anyway (measured)" — and that measurement did not exist. bench:lane-width now exists to decide it on the target hardware, alternating arm order so a drifting machine cannot fake a result. The default stays 1 until a clean number says otherwise.
Chunk size / MMR λ / seedK / expandHopsmediumReasoned about, never swept — despite the harness supporting it trivially. Invariant 23 applied unevenly.
Cold-startlowAll figures are on a warm cache.
Why publishing this list is the right move A buyer will run their own evaluation. A scorecard where you win on cost and hold on quality is a strong, defensible pitch. A scorecard where you win on cost and lose on quality is one you need to know about before they run it for you.

And handing over the list first changes the conversation from "what are they hiding" to "they know their system better than we will after a week of diligence". The technical demo script in chapter 70 ends with exactly this: "be candid about the known gaps — this document is the same list we would hand them."

The order to close them

1
Extraction quality. gold.ts's ExtractionCases are written; score them and print a precision/recall row. It caps everything else, and doing it first tells you whether the next four items are even the constraint.
2
Groundedness. The second question every buyer asks. QACase.mustMention is the assertion; the numbered evidence is the substrate. Both halves exist.
3
Concurrent load. Cheap, and it is the measurement most likely to surface a surprise rather than confirm an expectation.
4
1M-entity latency. The stated exit criterion, and the levers are already identified (ch. 64).
5
Real corpus, second domain. The expensive one, and the one that makes the scorecard publishable.
Part VIII

Infrastructure & operations

What exists today (a docker-compose file), what has to exist to run this for someone else's money, and the infrastructure-as-code that gets you there — Terraform and Pulumi both written out, with the decision between them made properly rather than by preference.

52
Chapter 52 · Infrastructure

The topology today

One docker-compose.yml containing one service, plus two processes you start by hand. That is the entire deployment story. It is worth studying anyway, because the compose file contains two settings that were discovered by measurement and that most production Postgres deployments still get wrong.

What runs

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  developer laptop                                                │
│                                                                  │
│   pnpm dev                                                       │
│    ├─ api  :3000   tsx watch src/index.ts    (in-process worker) │
│    └─ web  :5173   vite                                          │
│                                                                  │
│   docker compose up -d                                           │
│    └─ manifold-db  pgvector/pgvector:pg16    host :5433 → :5432  │
│         shm_size 1gb · shared_buffers 1GB                        │
│         volume postgres_data                                     │
│                                                                  │
│   ollama serve  :11434   (or OPENAI_API_KEY)                     │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The four Postgres settings, and why they are not defaults

SettingDefault → hereReasoning
shared_buffers128 MB → 1 GBThe HNSW indexes (~400 MB at 100k entities) must stay in the buffer cache to give consistent ANN latency. "Size this to your index, not your row count." Measured: a 1.8× p50 regression purely from cache eviction.
shm_size64 MB → 1 GBA Docker setting, not a Postgres one. Postgres uses POSIX shared memory for parallel workers, so VACUUM FULL, REINDEX and index builds fail with "could not resize shared memory segment" long before disk matters.
effective_cache_size4 GB → 3 GBA planner hint, not an allocation. Tells the optimiser how much OS-level caching to assume when costing an index scan versus a sequential one.
maintenance_work_mem64 MB → 512 MBHNSW index builds are memory-hungry. At 64 MB a 100k-vector index build spills to disk and takes an order of magnitude longer.
Why this compose file is better than most production configs Every one of those four values has a comment explaining what it protects. Most production Postgres configs are a wall of numbers copied from a blog post, and nobody can tell which ones matter for their workload. When these values move to Terraform (ch. 56) the comments must move with them — a parameter group with no rationale is a parameter group nobody dares change.

Everything that is missing

MissingSeverityConsequence today
DockerfileblockerThe API cannot be deployed anywhere. Nothing downstream — CI, ECS, Kubernetes — is possible without it. Ch. 53.
BackupscriticalA Docker volume. No snapshot schedule, no PITR, no restore drill. The single highest-severity operational gap in the system: the corpus represents real money in model calls and cannot currently be recovered.
CIcritical250 tests and a clean typecheck that nobody is required to run. Ch. 59.
Graceful shutdownmediumNo SIGTERM handler; stopWorkers() exists but nothing calls it. On deploy, in-flight jobs are cut mid-transaction. Downgraded from "high" by the durable queue — the lease lapses and the reaper re-queues them within 60 s, so the work is no longer lost, only delayed and one attempt poorer. Ch. 61.
Structured logshighconsole.log with emoji and box-drawing characters. Unparseable, uncorrelatable, no request id.
Metrics / traceshighAn in-process singleton that dies with the process. No Prometheus, no OpenTelemetry. Ch. 60.
Secrets managementhigh.env files. OPENAI_API_KEY and DATABASE_URL in plaintext on disk and in process environment.
TLShighPlain HTTP. API keys travel in clear text.
Least-privilege DB rolemediumThe app connects as postgres superuser. It should have a role that cannot DROP, and — for RLS (ch. 36) — must be NOBYPASSRLS.
53
Chapter 53 · Infrastructure

The container image

Nothing downstream is possible without this file. Here it is, written out, with the reasoning for every stage — because a Dockerfile is one of the few places where a fifty-line file determines your deploy time, your image size, your attack surface, and your build cache hit rate all at once. 

# ─── Stage 1: dependencies ─────────────────────────────────────────────
# Separate from the build so a source-only change does not re-resolve the
# dependency tree. This is the single largest build-time saving available.
FROM node:20-slim AS deps
RUN corepack enable && corepack prepare pnpm@9 --activate
WORKDIR /app

# Only the manifests. Docker caches this layer until one of them changes.
COPY pnpm-lock.yaml pnpm-workspace.yaml package.json ./
COPY apps/api/package.json      apps/api/
COPY packages/shared/package.json packages/shared/

# --frozen-lockfile: fail rather than silently resolve a different tree than
# the one that was tested. A CI build that "fixes" the lockfile is a CI build
# testing something other than what you will ship.
RUN pnpm install --frozen-lockfile --filter api... --filter shared...

# ─── Stage 2: build ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
FROM deps AS build
COPY tsconfig.json ./
COPY packages/shared packages/shared
COPY apps/api       apps/api
RUN pnpm --filter shared build \
 && pnpm --filter api typecheck \
 && pnpm --filter api build          # tsc -p tsconfig.build.json → dist/

# ─── Stage 3: production dependencies only ─────────────────────────────
# A second, clean install with no dev dependencies. Typescript, drizzle-kit
# and tsx are ~150 MB of build tooling with no business being in a runtime
# image — every one of them is also attack surface.
FROM deps AS prod-deps
RUN pnpm install --frozen-lockfile --prod --filter api... --filter shared...

# ─── Stage 4: runtime ──────────────────────────────────────────────────
FROM node:20-slim AS runtime
ENV NODE_ENV=production
WORKDIR /app

# tini as PID 1. Node does not reap zombies and, more importantly, does not
# forward signals to children by default — without an init, SIGTERM from the
# orchestrator may never reach the graceful-shutdown handler (ch. 61).
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends tini \
 && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*

# Non-root. The base image ships a `node` user; use it.
USER node

COPY --from=prod-deps --chown=node:node /app/node_modules ./node_modules
COPY --from=prod-deps --chown=node:node /app/apps/api/node_modules ./apps/api/node_modules
COPY --from=build     --chown=node:node /app/apps/api/dist ./apps/api/dist
COPY --from=build     --chown=node:node /app/packages/shared/dist ./packages/shared/dist

# Migrations travel WITH the image. The image and the schema it expects are
# one artefact — see ch. 59 for why the migration job runs the same image.
COPY --chown=node:node apps/api/drizzle ./apps/api/drizzle

EXPOSE 3000

# Uses the real readiness endpoint, which checks the database, the vector
# extension AND indexes, the chat provider, and the embedding provider.
# A health check that only proves the process is listening proves nothing.
HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=5s --start-period=40s --retries=3 \
  CMD node -e "fetch('http://127.0.0.1:3000/health').then(r=>process.exit(r.ok?0:1)).catch(()=>process.exit(1))"

ENTRYPOINT ["/usr/bin/tini", "--"]
CMD ["node", "apps/api/dist/index.js"]

The decisions, and what each costs if you get it wrong

DecisionCost of the alternative
Four stagesA single-stage image ships typescript, drizzle-kit, tsx and every dev dependency: roughly 3× the size, a much larger CVE surface, and a slower pull on every scale-out.
Manifests copied before sourceEvery source edit re-runs pnpm install. On this workspace that is 40–90 seconds per build, paid on every commit.
--frozen-lockfileWithout it, CI silently resolves a different tree than the one that was tested. This matters more than usual here: several dependencies are pinned to "latest" (ch. 67), so the lockfile is the only thing making a build reproducible at all.
typecheck inside the buildA type error becomes a runtime failure in production instead of a failed build. The build is the last place the check is free.
tini as PID 1SIGTERM may never reach the application. Graceful shutdown silently does not happen, and you discover it during an incident.
Non-rootA container escape starts as root. Free to fix, expensive to explain.
Migrations in the imageSchema and code drift. The image that runs and the migrations that shaped its database must be one artefact, or "which version is this database at?" becomes unanswerable.
/health as the healthcheckA TCP check passes while the model provider is unreachable and the vector index is missing — exactly the states ch. 17 built this endpoint to expose.

One thing to be careful about

Do not gate task startup on the full /health /health returns 503 when the model provider is unreachable. If the orchestrator's readiness probe uses it, an OpenAI outage takes your API out of the load balancer — even though GET /api/field/retrieve, /api/graph/* and every read path still work perfectly at zero generative calls.

Split the endpoint: /health/live (process is up), /health/ready (database + vector storage — the things without which nothing works), and keep /health as the full diagnostic for humans and dashboards. Readiness should gate on hard dependencies only; everything else is a degraded mode you want to serve, not a reason to disappear.
54
Chapter 54 · Infrastructure

Target production topology

Where this goes. One reference architecture on AWS, deliberately boring, with the reasoning for each component and honest notes on the two cheaper alternatives.

Deliberately boring. Two ECS services, one RDS, one Redis, one ALB — and every choice traceable to a chapter.

Why each component, and why not something else

ComponentChosenReasoning
ComputeECS FargateTwo long-running services with different scaling profiles and a run-once migration task. Kubernetes buys workload density and portability this does not need yet — and costs a platform team. Lambda is disqualified outright: the worker runs for minutes and the API holds a warm connection pool.
DatabaseRDS Postgres 16, Multi-AZpgvector is available on RDS. Multi-AZ is availability, the read replica is capacity — different problems, and confusing them is common. Aurora is a defensible alternative (faster failover, storage autoscaling) and costs more; take it when failover time becomes a stated SLO.
Instance sizer6g.2xlargeMemory-optimised, because shared_buffers must hold the HNSW indexes (ch. 28). At 1M entities the indexes are several GB. Size the instance to the index, not the dataset. Graviton for the price/performance.
CacheElastiCache Redis optionalNote what it is not: the queue. Postgres SKIP LOCKED already gives multi-instance claiming with no broker (ch. 16), so Redis is down to two jobs — shared rate-limit windows (ch. 34) and a retrieval result cache (ch. 28). Neither is needed at one instance. Add it when you scale out, not before.
Two ECS servicesapi and worker splitIngestion load and query load are uncorrelated. One service means scaling query capacity to absorb an ingest burst, and an ingest burst starving queries of CPU. Split anything whose scaling triggers are independent. The durable queue makes this trivial: the worker service simply runs startWorkers() and claims — it needs no ALB target and no coordination with the api service.
Autoscaling signalqueue.byType from /healthThe worker service scales on process-lane depth, the api service on ALB requests-per-target. Two services, two signals, because they are two different pressures.
SecretsSecrets ManagerRotation for the database credential; no plaintext in the task definition, the image, or the repository.

Two cheaper topologies, honestly

Fly.io / Railway / Render~$150/mo

One fly.toml, managed Postgres with pgvector, built-in TLS and health-gated rolling deploys. For a design-partner deployment this is the right answer and the AWS build is over-engineering.

Costs: less control over Postgres parameters (and shared_buffers is load-bearing here), weaker compliance story, and a migration later when a customer asks for VPC peering or a signed BAA.

Single large EC2 + Docker Compose~$200/mo

Literally the compose file, on one machine, with EBS snapshots. Cheapest and simplest.

Costs: no HA, deploys are downtime, and you now own patching. Defensible for an internal tool; indefensible the moment there is a customer contract — mostly because the backup story is manual and manual backups are, empirically, absent backups.

The sequencing recommendation Start on Fly. Move to the AWS topology when the first of these becomes true: a customer requires VPC/compliance controls, ingest and query load need independent scaling, or you need more than one worker process. Do not build the target topology before one of those is true — it is three weeks of work that buys nothing you can currently use, and every week of it is a week not spent on chapter 51's list.
55
Chapter 55 · Infrastructure

IaC: Terraform vs Pulumi, decided

Both tools work. Both are mature. The choice between them is usually made by preference and then defended with reasons — which is exactly backwards. This chapter makes it properly: first establish what has to be managed, then derive which properties matter, then pick.

First: what actually needs to be managed?

An IaC decision made before this inventory exists is a decision about tooling taste. Made after, it is usually obvious.

ResourceCountChange rateCharacter
VPC, subnets, routing, NAT, security groups~25Almost neverPure declaration. Written once, read often, changed under review.
RDS instance + parameter group + replica3RareThe parameter group is load-bearing (ch. 28) and must carry its rationale as comments.
ECS cluster, 2 services, 3 task definitions~8Every deployThe image tag changes constantly; everything else almost never.
ALB, target group, listener, ACM cert, WAF~7RareDeclaration.
ElastiCache (when N>1)1NeverDeclaration.
IAM roles + policies~8RareSecurity-critical. Must be reviewable as a diff by someone who did not write it.
Secrets Manager entries~4Rotation onlyResource is managed; value must never be.
CloudWatch alarms, log groups, dashboards~20OccasionalRepetitive. Twenty alarms differing by metric and threshold — the one place a loop earns its keep.
S3 buckets + lifecycle~3NeverDeclaration.
Read the shape of that table Roughly 75 resources. One cluster of repetition (alarms). One value that changes every deploy (the image tag). Everything else is static declaration that must be reviewable and auditable.

There is no dynamic provisioning — nothing here creates infrastructure per tenant, per customer, per region, or in response to runtime state. That single observation removes the strongest argument for a general-purpose programming language, because the strongest argument for Pulumi is precisely the case where "how many of these do I need?" is answered by code rather than by a person.

The properties that actually matter here

PropertyWeightWhy, for this system
Reviewable plancriticalThe blast radius includes an RDS instance holding a corpus that cost real money in model calls. plan/preview output must be readable by someone who did not write the change — and must be readable enough that "replace RDS instance" jumps off the screen.
Team familiaritycriticalInfrastructure is touched rarely and always under pressure. A tool nobody uses weekly is a tool nobody remembers during an incident. HCL is a smaller thing to remember than an SDK.
Module ecosystemhighA VPC is a solved problem. terraform-aws-modules/vpc is thousands of person-hours of edge cases nobody should re-derive.
Loops / abstractionlowOne cluster of repetition (alarms), well served by for_each over a map.
Testable infra logiclowThere is no logic. Nothing computes a subnet plan or decides a topology at apply time.
Type safetylow–mediumGenuinely nice. Mostly redundant with a reviewed plan and a provider that validates.

The decision

Terraform (or OpenTofu), for this system, at this stage Not because Pulumi is worse. Because the two properties that matter most here — a plan that reads like a plan, and a language the whole team can read cold — are Terraform's strengths, and the property Pulumi wins on decisively (dynamic, computed infrastructure) is a property this inventory does not need.

The honest form of the argument: HCL's limitations are a real cost that this system does not pay, because it has no infrastructure logic to express. Choosing the more powerful tool to solve a problem you do not have means paying its cost — a heavier plan diff, an SDK version matrix, and a language boundary between "code that runs" and "code that provisions" — for nothing.
When this decision flips to Puluminame the trigger
  • Per-tenant infrastructure. If enterprise customers get a dedicated database or a dedicated VPC, the count becomes dynamic and driven by application state. This is the likely trigger for Manifold specifically — a single-tenant deployment tier is a normal enterprise ask.
  • Multi-region with per-region variation beyond what a module's variables express.
  • The team is TypeScript-only and the context-switch cost genuinely dominates. Manifold is a TypeScript codebase, which is the strongest argument in Pulumi's favour here and is outweighed by review quality — but reasonable people differ, and that is fine as long as the trade is named.
OpenTofu vs Terraformlicensing

HashiCorp moved Terraform to the BUSL. OpenTofu is the MPL-2.0 fork under the Linux Foundation, a drop-in replacement at the versions that matter here.

Use OpenTofu. The licence removes a class of future conversation with a customer's legal team for zero technical cost, and every module referenced in this chapter works unchanged. Where this book says "Terraform", it means either.

Repository layout

infra/
├── modules/                        # reusable, no environment knowledge
│   ├── network/                    # VPC, subnets, NAT, endpoints
│   ├── database/                   # RDS + parameter group + replica
│   ├── service/                    # ONE ECS service: task def, SG, autoscaling, alarms
│   └── observability/              # log groups, alarms, dashboard
├── envs/
│   ├── staging/
│   │   ├── main.tf                 # composes the modules
│   │   ├── variables.tf
│   │   ├── terraform.tfvars        # sizes, counts — NEVER secrets
│   │   └── backend.tf              # S3 state + DynamoDB lock, staging key
│   └── production/
│       └── … same shape, different tfvars
└── global/
    ├── state-backend/              # the S3 bucket + lock table (bootstrapped once)
    └── ecr/                        # the image registry, shared by all envs
Directories, not workspaces, for environments Terraform workspaces share one configuration with different state. That is fine when environments are genuinely identical and only sized differently — and they never stay that way. Staging gets a single-AZ database; production gets a replica and deletion protection. Expressed as workspaces, those differences become count = terraform.workspace == "production" ? 1 : 0 scattered through the configuration, which is conditional logic in the least testable place you own.

Separate directories cost some duplication and buy something more valuable: you can read envs/production/main.tf and know what production is, without simulating a variable in your head. And a mistake in staging cannot reach production state, because it is a different state file behind a different key.

The module boundary that matters most

modules/service/ is the one worth designing carefully, because Manifold has two ECS services with genuinely different shapes — the api is load-balanced and scales on request rate; the worker has no ingress and scales on queue depth (ch. 16). A module that assumes a load balancer forces the worker into a fiction; a module that makes everything optional becomes a soup of nulls. 

# modules/service/variables.tf — the shape that handles both
variable "name"            { type = string }
variable "image"           { type = string }
variable "command"         { type = list(string), default = null }   # worker overrides CMD
variable "cpu"             { type = number }
variable "memory"          { type = number }
variable "desired_count"   { type = number }
variable "environment"     { type = map(string) }
variable "secrets"         { type = map(string) }   # name → Secrets Manager ARN

# The seam: a service either has ingress or it does not.
variable "load_balancer" {
  type = object({
    target_group_arn = string
    container_port   = number
  })
  default     = null
  description = "Null for services with no ingress (the worker)."
}

# And it scales on ONE of two signals, never both.
variable "autoscaling" {
  type = object({
    min           = number
    max           = number
    metric        = string   # "alb_request_count" | "queue_depth"
    target_value  = number
  })
}

Two nullable seams, both named after a real difference between the two services rather than after a feature flag. This is the same instinct as the connector contract's structured flag (ch. 9): model the variation that exists, at the point where it exists, and let everything else be shared.

56
Chapter 56 · Infrastructure

The Terraform, written out

Real configuration for the topology in chapter 54, with the reasoning inline. Not a complete apply-ready tree — the parts that teach nothing are elided — but every decision that has a reason is here, and several of those reasons come straight out of measurements made earlier in this book.

State backend — the first thing, and the one people rush

# envs/production/backend.tf
terraform {
  required_version = ">= 1.6"

  backend "s3" {
    bucket         = "manifold-tfstate"
    key            = "production/terraform.tfstate"   # distinct key per environment
    region         = "eu-west-1"
    encrypt        = true
    dynamodb_table = "manifold-tflock"                 # the lock that prevents concurrent applies
  }

  required_providers {
    aws = { source = "hashicorp/aws", version = "~> 5.40" }   # pinned minor
  }
}
Three properties, each preventing a specific disaster Remote state. Local state means one laptop knows what production is. When that laptop is lost or two people apply from different copies, the recovery is terraform import for every resource you own.

Locking. Two concurrent applies interleave writes to one state file and corrupt it. The DynamoDB table costs cents and turns the second apply into a wait.

Encryption. State contains secrets in plaintext. RDS passwords, generated tokens, anything a resource returns. This is the most commonly under-appreciated fact about Terraform, and it is why chapter 58 argues that values must not be managed here at all.

The chicken-and-egg: the bucket and table must exist before the backend can use them. global/state-backend/ creates them with local state, applied once, and that tiny state file is committed — it manages two resources nobody will ever change, and the alternative is a wiki page nobody reads.

Network

# envs/production/main.tf
module "network" {
  source  = "terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws"
  version = "~> 5.5"

  name = "manifold-prod"
  cidr = "10.0.0.0/16"

  azs              = ["eu-west-1a", "eu-west-1b", "eu-west-1c"]
  public_subnets   = ["10.0.0.0/20",   "10.0.16.0/20",  "10.0.32.0/20"]
  private_subnets  = ["10.0.64.0/20",  "10.0.80.0/20",  "10.0.96.0/20"]   # app
  database_subnets = ["10.0.128.0/24", "10.0.129.0/24", "10.0.130.0/24"]  # no internet route

  # One NAT gateway, not three. ~$32/month each plus data processing; the
  # only egress is arXiv, OpenAI and ECR. Losing an AZ's NAT costs egress
  # from that AZ, not availability — a deliberate cost/HA trade, named here
  # so the next person does not "fix" it without knowing it was chosen.
  enable_nat_gateway = true
  single_nat_gateway = true

  # VPC endpoints keep ECR pulls and Secrets Manager reads off the NAT.
  # They pay for themselves at any real deploy frequency, and they remove
  # the internet from the path of a secret fetch.
  enable_s3_endpoint = true

  tags = local.common_tags
}

Database — where this system's measurements become configuration

resource "aws_db_parameter_group" "manifold" {
  name_prefix = "manifold-pg16-"
  family      = "postgres16"

  # ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  # These are NOT generic tuning. Each was found by measurement (ch. 28)
  # and each protects a specific, measured failure. Do not "clean up".
  # ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  # The HNSW indexes must stay in the buffer cache or ANN latency collapses.
  # Measured: a 1.8x p50 regression purely from cache eviction when 2 GB of
  # dead JSONB pushed 400 MB of index out. SIZE THIS TO THE INDEX, not to a
  # percentage of RAM: 16 GB on a 64 GB r6g.2xlarge.
  parameter { name = "shared_buffers", value = "2097152", apply_method = "pending-reboot" }  # 8 kB pages

  # HNSW index builds spill to disk below this and take an order of magnitude longer.
  parameter { name = "maintenance_work_mem", value = "2097152" }   # kB = 2 GB

  # Planner hint. Tells the optimiser how much OS caching to assume when
  # costing an index scan against a sequential one.
  parameter { name = "effective_cache_size", value = "5242880" }    # 8 kB pages = 40 GB

  # MMR + PPR run in the app; Postgres does per-query sorting for ANN and ts_rank.
  parameter { name = "work_mem", value = "32768" }                  # kB, per sort node

  # Observability: log anything slower than the retrieval p95 budget, so a
  # regression to a sequential scan (ch. 20 — correct results, unusable speed)
  # appears in logs rather than in a support ticket.
  parameter { name = "log_min_duration_statement", value = "250" }
  parameter { name = "shared_preload_libraries", value = "pg_stat_statements", apply_method = "pending-reboot" }

  lifecycle { create_before_destroy = true }   # name_prefix + this = safe param changes
}

resource "aws_db_instance" "manifold" {
  identifier     = "manifold-prod"
  engine         = "postgres"
  engine_version = "16.3"
  instance_class = "db.r6g.2xlarge"   # memory-optimised: see shared_buffers above

  allocated_storage     = 200
  max_allocated_storage = 2000               # storage autoscaling
  storage_type          = "gp3"
  storage_encrypted     = true

  db_subnet_group_name   = module.network.database_subnet_group_name
  vpc_security_group_ids = [aws_security_group.database.id]
  parameter_group_name   = aws_db_parameter_group.manifold.name

  multi_az                = true             # AVAILABILITY (automatic failover)
  backup_retention_period = 35               # PITR window — see ch. 61
  backup_window           = "03:00-04:00"
  copy_tags_to_snapshot   = true

  # The corpus represents real money in model calls (ch. 45) and cannot be
  # regenerated cheaply. Three independent guards, because `terraform destroy`
  # in the wrong directory is a real thing that happens to real teams.
  deletion_protection       = true
  skip_final_snapshot       = false
  final_snapshot_identifier = "manifold-prod-final"
  lifecycle { prevent_destroy = true }

  performance_insights_enabled = true
  enabled_cloudwatch_logs_exports = ["postgresql"]
}

# CAPACITY, not availability — a different problem from multi_az above.
# Retrieval reads can move here; writes and the queue's SKIP LOCKED claims
# CANNOT (ch. 64) — a claim is an UPDATE and must hit the primary.
resource "aws_db_instance" "replica" {
  identifier          = "manifold-prod-ro"
  replicate_source_db = aws_db_instance.manifold.identifier
  instance_class      = "db.r6g.xlarge"
  skip_final_snapshot = true
}
The comment block is the most valuable part of that file Those parameters look like generic tuning. They are not — each is the residue of a measurement described in chapter 28, and without the comments the next engineer will "tidy" shared_buffers back toward a default and reintroduce a 1.8× latency regression with no idea why.

Infrastructure code needs comments more than application code does, because it is read orders of magnitude less often, always under pressure, and its failure modes are slow rather than loud.

The two services

# The API: load-balanced, scales on request rate.
module "api" {
  source = "../../modules/service"

  name          = "manifold-api"
  image         = "${data.aws_ecr_repository.manifold.repository_url}:${var.image_tag}"
  cpu           = 1024
  memory        = 2048
  desired_count = 2

  environment = {
    NODE_ENV            = "production"
    AUTH_MODE           = "required"      # ch. 17: the banner exists for a reason
    PIPELINE_MODE       = "field"
    EMBED_PROVIDER      = "openai"
    MODEL_EXTRACT       = "openai:gpt-4o-mini"   # ch. 43: pay for extraction…
    MODEL_VERBALIZE     = "openai:gpt-4o-mini"   # …economise on verbalization
    TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS  = "1"              # EXACTLY ONE ALB. ch. 34 — getting this
                                          # wrong silently reopens rate-limit bypass
    CORS_ORIGINS        = "https://app.manifold.example"
    MAX_PENDING_JOBS    = "2000"           # ch. 16 admission ceiling
  }

  secrets = {
    DATABASE_URL   = aws_secretsmanager_secret.database_url.arn
    OPENAI_API_KEY = aws_secretsmanager_secret.openai_key.arn
  }

  load_balancer = {
    target_group_arn = aws_lb_target_group.api.arn
    container_port   = 3000
  }

  autoscaling = { min = 2, max = 10, metric = "alb_request_count", target_value = 200 }
}

# The worker: no ingress, scales on QUEUE DEPTH.
module "worker" {
  source = "../../modules/service"

  name          = "manifold-worker"
  image         = "${data.aws_ecr_repository.manifold.repository_url}:${var.image_tag}"
  cpu           = 2048
  memory        = 4096
  desired_count = 1

  environment = merge(local.shared_env, {
    # INSTANCE_ID must be unique per task or two workers reap each other's
    # jobs (ch. 16). The ECS task ARN is injected by the runtime.
    FETCH_CONCURRENCY   = "4"       # network-bound, parallelisable
    PROCESS_CONCURRENCY = "4"       # hosted extraction ⇒ network-bound, so >1 is real
                                    # (on a local GPU this MUST stay 1 — ch. 16)
  })

  secrets       = local.shared_secrets
  load_balancer = null                  # ← the seam. No ingress.

  autoscaling = { min = 1, max = 5, metric = "queue_depth", target_value = 20 }
}
Two environment variables that are security controls, not configuration TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS = "1" encodes a fact about the deployed topology: exactly one ALB. Add a CloudFront distribution in front and this becomes 2 — and if nobody updates it, the rate limiter starts trusting a client-supplied header and the protection silently becomes opt-out (ch. 34 / ch. 35 A4). Any config value that must agree with the topology belongs in the same file as the topology, which is exactly what putting it here achieves.

INSTANCE_ID must be unique per task. The ECS task ARN satisfies that; a hardcoded name would mean two workers both computing api:3000 and reaping each other's live jobs on every deploy (ch. 16).

The queue-depth autoscaling policy

# The worker scales on work waiting, not on CPU — a worker blocked on a
# model call is idle by CPU and busy by every measure that matters.
resource "aws_appautoscaling_policy" "worker_queue_depth" {
  name               = "manifold-worker-queue-depth"
  policy_type        = "TargetTrackingScaling"
  resource_id        = "service/${aws_ecs_cluster.main.name}/${module.worker.service_name}"
  scalable_dimension = "ecs:service:DesiredCount"
  service_namespace  = "ecs"

  target_tracking_scaling_policy_configuration {
    target_value = 20        # queued process-lane jobs per worker task

    customized_metric_specification {
      metric_name = "QueuedJobs"       # published from /health's queue.byType
      namespace   = "Manifold"
      statistic   = "Average"
      dimensions { name = "Lane", value = "process" }
    }

    # Scale IN slowly: a task killed mid-extraction loses ~15 minutes of GPU
    # work and burns a retry attempt. Scaling out is cheap; scaling in is not.
    scale_in_cooldown  = 900
    scale_out_cooldown = 60
  }
}

The asymmetric cooldowns are the interesting part, and they are derived directly from chapter 16's arithmetic: the cost of a wrong scale-in is fifteen minutes of GPU time and one of three retry attempts; the cost of a wrong scale-out is a few minutes of task cost. Symmetric cooldowns treat those as equivalent. They are not.

Alarms — the one place a loop earns its keep

locals {
  alarms = {
    api_5xx           = { metric = "HTTPCode_Target_5XX_Count", threshold = 10,  period = 60 }
    api_p95_latency   = { metric = "TargetResponseTime",       threshold = 1.5, period = 300, stat = "p95" }
    db_cpu            = { metric = "CPUUtilization",           threshold = 80,  period = 300 }
    db_connections    = { metric = "DatabaseConnections",      threshold = 80,  period = 60 }
    db_replica_lag    = { metric = "ReplicaLag",               threshold = 30,  period = 60 }
    queue_depth       = { metric = "QueuedJobs",               threshold = 500, period = 300 }
    # ─── the two nobody thinks of, and both are Manifold-specific ───
    chunk_failure_rate = { metric = "ChunkFailureRate",       threshold = 0.2, period = 900 }
    audit_write_fail   = { metric = "AuditWriteFailures",     threshold = 1,   period = 300 }
  }
}

resource "aws_cloudwatch_metric_alarm" "this" {
  for_each = local.alarms
  alarm_name          = "manifold-prod-${each.key}"
  metric_name         = each.value.metric
  threshold           = each.value.threshold
  period              = each.value.period
  extended_statistic  = try(each.value.stat, null)
  comparison_operator = "GreaterThanThreshold"
  evaluation_periods  = 2
  alarm_actions       = [aws_sns_topic.alerts.arn]
  treat_missing_data  = "notBreaching"
}
The last two alarms are the ones that make this system's own failure modes visible chunk_failure_rate — a rising rate means extraction is degrading. Chapter 17 established that failures are counted honestly rather than hidden; this is where that honesty becomes an alert. Without it, a model regression shows up weeks later as "the graph seems sparse".

audit_write_fail — audit writes are fire-and-forget by design (ch. 33), so a failure is invisible to every caller. An alarm is the only thing standing between "best-effort" and "silently absent". Any deliberately-swallowed error needs a counter and an alarm, or the swallowing is not a design decision, it is a bug with a comment.
57
Chapter 57 · Infrastructure

The same design in Pulumi

The identical topology, in TypeScript, so the comparison is concrete rather than tribal. Pulumi is genuinely better at several things visible here — and the chapter ends by naming the case where it would become the right answer for this system specifically.

The same two services

// infra/index.ts
import * as aws from '@pulumi/aws';
import * as awsx from '@pulumi/awsx';
import * as pulumi from '@pulumi/pulumi';

const cfg = new pulumi.Config();
const env = pulumi.getStack();                       // 'production' | 'staging'
const isProd = env === 'production';

const vpc = new awsx.ec2.Vpc(`manifold-${env}`, {
  cidrBlock: '10.0.0.0/16',
  numberOfAvailabilityZones: 3,
  natGateways: { strategy: isProd ? 'Single' : 'None' },
});

// ── A real class, with real types. This is Pulumi's strongest card. ──
interface ServiceArgs {
  name: string;
  image: pulumi.Input<string>;
  cpu: number;
  memory: number;
  environment: Record<string, pulumi.Input<string>>;
  secrets: Record<string, pulumi.Input<string>>;
  // The compiler enforces the seam: a service either has ingress or does not,
  // and it scales on exactly one signal. HCL cannot express either constraint.
  ingress?: { targetGroup: aws.lb.TargetGroup; port: number };
  scaling: { min: number; max: number } &
    ({ on: 'requests'; perTarget: number } | { on: 'queueDepth'; lane: 'ingest' | 'process'; perTask: number });
}

class Service extends pulumi.ComponentResource {
  readonly service: aws.ecs.Service;
  constructor(args: ServiceArgs, opts?: pulumi.ComponentResourceOptions) {
    super('manifold:infra:Service', args.name, {}, opts);
    /* task definition, service, autoscaling, alarms … */
  }
}

const api = new Service({
  name: 'manifold-api', image, cpu: 1024, memory: 2048,
  environment: { ...sharedEnv, TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS: '1', MAX_PENDING_JOBS: '2000' },
  secrets: sharedSecrets,
  ingress: { targetGroup: apiTargetGroup, port: 3000 },
  scaling: { min: 2, max: 10, on: 'requests', perTarget: 200 },
});

const worker = new Service({
  name: 'manifold-worker', image, cpu: 2048, memory: 4096,
  environment: { ...sharedEnv, FETCH_CONCURRENCY: '4', PROCESS_CONCURRENCY: '4' },
  secrets: sharedSecrets,
  // no `ingress` — and the discriminated union makes `lane` REQUIRED here
  scaling: { min: 1, max: 5, on: 'queueDepth', lane: 'process', perTask: 20 },
});

Where Pulumi is genuinely better

CapabilityConcretely, in this configuration
Discriminated unionsscaling is either request-based or queue-based, and choosing queueDepth makes lane mandatory. In HCL that is an object with nullable fields plus a validation block plus hope. This is a real correctness win, not ergonomics — it makes an invalid service definition unrepresentable.
Reusing application constantsimport { MAX_JOB_ATTEMPTS } from '../apps/api/src/queue'. The alarm threshold and the application's retry cap can be the same symbol. In Terraform they are two numbers in two languages that must be kept in agreement by memory.
Real testingpulumi.runtime.setMocks() lets you unit-test infrastructure logic — "the worker service has no ingress", "production sets deletion protection" — with the same test runner as the application.
Dynamic provisioningAn await db.query('select … from tenants where tier = $1', ['dedicated']) followed by a loop that provisions per-tenant infrastructure is natural. In Terraform it is an external data source, a JSON contract and a two-phase apply.
One languageThe team already writes TypeScript. No context switch, one linter, one formatter, one dependency manager.

Where Terraform stays ahead — for this system

PropertyWhy it decides this case
Plan readabilityterraform plan is a flat, greppable diff of resources and attributes. pulumi preview is good and is a tree of component resources — genuinely harder to scan for the one line that says ~ replace on an RDS instance. Given the blast radius here, that difference decides the chapter.
Cold readabilityHCL has one shape. TypeScript infrastructure can be written in a dozen styles, and a helper someone wrote six months ago is now a thing you must read before you can read the infrastructure. Under incident pressure, the boring language wins.
Module ecosystemterraform-aws-modules/vpc encodes thousands of hours of edge cases. awsx is good and thinner.
Dependency surfaceTerraform: one binary, pinned providers. Pulumi: a Node runtime, an SDK, a provider plugin, and package.json. Chapter 05's version-drift war story was caused by exactly this class of surface.
Accidental logicHCL's weakness — hard to express logic — is a feature when there should be no logic. TypeScript makes it easy to write infrastructure whose result depends on when it ran.
The verdict, restated so it can be overturned Terraform/OpenTofu today, because this infrastructure is 75 static resources with one loop and no logic, and because the plan diff is the primary safety control on a database that cannot be regenerated.

Switch to Pulumi when per-tenant infrastructure exists — a dedicated database or VPC per enterprise customer. At that point the resource count becomes a function of application state, which is precisely the case HCL cannot express well and TypeScript expresses naturally. For a product whose central claim is tenant isolation, that is a likely destination rather than a hypothetical one, so keep the module boundaries clean enough that the port is mechanical.
58
Chapter 58 · Infrastructure

Secrets, state, and adoption

The genuinely hard part of infrastructure as code, and the part most guides skip: what happens to a secret that passes through a tool whose state file is a plaintext record of everything it has ever seen.

The problem, stated bluntly

Terraform state contains secrets in plaintext. So does Pulumi state, unless encrypted. If a resource returns a value — an RDS master password, a generated token, an access key — that value is written to state in the clear. Encrypting the S3 bucket protects it at rest from someone with bucket access; it does not protect it from anyone who can run terraform state pull, which is everyone who can apply.

And the corollary people miss: marking a variable sensitive = true only redacts it from console output. It is still in state, in full.

The rule

Manage the secret's existence, never its value 
# IaC creates the container and grants access to it.
resource "aws_secretsmanager_secret" "openai_key" {
  name                    = "manifold/${var.environment}/openai-api-key"
  recovery_window_in_days = 7
}

# The VALUE is written out of band, once, by a human or a CI job:
#   aws secretsmanager put-secret-value --secret-id manifold/production/openai-api-key \
#     --secret-string "sk-..."
# It never enters a .tf file, a .tfvars file, a state file, or a git history.

# The task definition references the ARN. ECS resolves it at container start.
# The value is never in the task definition either.
secrets = [{ name = "OPENAI_API_KEY", valueFrom = aws_secretsmanager_secret.openai_key.arn }]
This is the same instinct as chapter 31's key handling — store the handle, never the secret — and the same as the bootstrap CLI's: solve the problem out of band through a channel that already requires higher privilege, rather than weakening the channel you are using.

The database password, which is the awkward case

RDS needs a master password at creation, and if Terraform generates it, it is in state. Three options, ranked:

ApproachVerdictDetail
RDS-managed master password (manage_master_user_password = true)bestAWS generates it, stores it in Secrets Manager, and rotates it. Terraform never sees the value — it only gets the secret ARN. Removes the problem rather than managing it.
random_password + Secrets ManageracceptableWorks, and the password is in state forever. Acceptable only with strict state access control and an accepted rotation procedure.
Password in .tfvarsnoIn state and in the repository, or in a file everyone forgets is on their laptop.

State access is production access

  • The state bucket is as sensitive as the database. Access to it is access to every secret any resource ever returned. IAM on it should be as tight as IAM on RDS.
  • Versioning on, with MFA delete. A corrupted state file is recoverable from a previous version; without versioning it is a full terraform import of everything you own.
  • Nobody applies from a laptop in production. CI applies, with an OIDC-federated role scoped to what it manages. A human's credentials should be able to plan and not apply — which is both a safety control and a review-forcing function.
  • Never commit .tfstate or .tfstate.backup. The one exception is the two-resource bootstrap state, which contains nothing sensitive.

The adoption plan

Manifold has no deployed infrastructure today. That is unusually fortunate: there is nothing to import, so the sequence can be clean. Each phase ends in a state where stopping is acceptable.

1
Dockerfile + CI first. Not IaC. (Ch. 53, 59.) Until there is a tested image, IaC has nothing to deploy and every hour spent on it is speculative. This ordering is the most commonly inverted one, because IaC feels more architectural.
2
Bootstrap the state backend. global/state-backend/: the S3 bucket and lock table, local state, applied once, committed. Half a day.
3
Staging, end to end, by hand-in-code. Network, database, one service, ALB. Write it flat first — no modules. Modules extracted from one working configuration are shaped by reality; modules designed up front are shaped by imagination. Two to three days.
4
Extract modules/service/ when the worker arrives. The second service is what reveals the true seams (ingress-or-not, scaling signal). One day.
5
Production as a sibling directory with different tfvars: multi-AZ, replica, deletion protection, larger instance. One day.
6
CI applies staging automatically on merge; production behind a manual approval that shows the plan. One day. The plan-in-the-approval is the control — an approval on a change nobody can see is a rubber stamp.
7
Observability and alarms last (ch. 56, 60), because the useful thresholds are the ones you learn from running the thing.
Roughly a week, and the sequencing rule behind it Every phase leaves the system in a state where stopping is acceptable. That is the property to preserve in any infrastructure migration: no step should require the next one to have been worth doing. Phase 3 alone gives you a reproducible staging environment, which is most of the value.
59
Chapter 59 · Infrastructure

CI/CD & release engineering

250 tests and a clean typecheck that nobody is required to run. This chapter is the pipeline that makes them load-bearing, and the two release rules that keep a schema migration from becoming an outage.

The pipeline

# .github/workflows/ci.yml
name: ci
on: { push: { branches: [main] }, pull_request: {} }

jobs:
  verify:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    services:
      postgres:
        image: pgvector/pgvector:pg16          # NOT plain postgres — the extension is required
        env: { POSTGRES_PASSWORD: postgres }
        options: >-
          --health-cmd pg_isready --health-interval 5s --health-retries 10
          --shm-size=1g                        # ch. 28: index builds fail below this
        ports: ['5432:5432']
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: pnpm/action-setup@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with: { node-version: 20, cache: pnpm }

      - run: pnpm install --frozen-lockfile
      - run: pnpm typecheck                    # api + web
      - run: pnpm test                         # 180 unit tests, no services

      - name: Postgres-backed suite
        env:
          TEST_DATABASE_URL: postgresql://postgres:postgres@localhost:5432/knowledge_graph_test
          EMBED_PROVIDER: local                # ← deterministic, no key, no network (ch. 47)
        run: |
          createdb -h localhost -U postgres knowledge_graph_test
          pnpm --filter api db:migrate         # migrations, never db:push (invariant 14)
          pnpm test:db                         # 70 tests: isolation, authz, retrieval, queue

      - name: Retrieval quality gate
        env: { EMBED_PROVIDER: local, DATABASE_URL: '…/knowledge_graph_eval' }
        run: pnpm --filter api eval            # and fail if multi-hop recall regresses

  image:
    needs: verify
    if: github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'
    steps:
      - uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4
        with: { role-to-assume: ${{ secrets.CI_ROLE_ARN }} }   # OIDC, no static keys
      - run: docker build -t $ECR/manifold:${{ github.sha }} .
      - run: docker push $ECR/manifold:${{ github.sha }}        # tagged by SHA, never :latest

  deploy-staging:
    needs: image
    steps:
      - run: tofu -chdir=infra/envs/staging apply -auto-approve -var image_tag=${{ github.sha }}

  deploy-production:
    needs: deploy-staging
    environment: production                    # ← manual approval, plan shown in the gate
    steps:
      - run: tofu -chdir=infra/envs/production apply -var image_tag=${{ github.sha }}
Two things this pipeline does that most do not EMBED_PROVIDER=local makes the whole suite runnable in CI. No model server, no API key, no network. That single design decision (ch. 47) is what turns "we have tests" into "the tests run on every pull request" — and it is worth noticing that it was made for the eval harness and paid off here. Capabilities built for measurement tend to pay off in operations.

The eval harness is a merge gate. Invariant 23 says to run it before and after any retrieval change. A process rule decays; a required check does not. Gate on the number that matters — multi-hop recall must not regress — not on an overall average that can hide a capability loss (ch. 48).

Migrations: the two rules that prevent an outage

1
Migrations run as a separate task, to completion, before the service update. Same image, different CMD. Not on application boot — N tasks starting simultaneously would each try to migrate, and the migration table's advisory lock turns that into N−1 tasks blocked at startup. And a migration that fails at boot fails every task, turning a schema problem into a total outage.
2
Every migration must be compatible with the currently-running code. During any rolling deploy, old and new code run against the new schema simultaneously. So: additive changes only in the deploy that introduces them. Adding a column is safe. Dropping one is safe only in a later deploy, after no running code references it. This is expand/contract, and it is not optional with more than one task.

Migration 0001 is a good citizen of rule 2 in an unusual way: it refuses to drop the JSONB embedding columns unless every row already has a populated embedding_vec. That is the contract phase of expand/contract with a machine-checked precondition — a migration that verifies its own safety rather than trusting that the expand phase completed.

The migration that would break rule 2 today Changing the embedding model (ch. 19) alters vector(n) on two columns and rebuilds two HNSW indexes. There is no version of that which is compatible with running code, and no zero-downtime path exists. The design — a second column, dual-write, background backfill, cutover flag, drop — is written in chapter 19 and unbuilt. It should be built before the first customer corpus, not during it.

Deployment safety

MechanismDetail
Health-gated rolloutECS replaces tasks only when the new one passes its health check. Use /health/ready (database + vector storage), not the full /health — an OpenAI outage must not block a deploy or drain the load balancer (ch. 53).
