Circuit breakerdeployment_circuit_breaker { enable = true, rollback = true }. If the new tasks fail health checks, ECS rolls back automatically.
Immutable tags:${GITHUB_SHA}, never :latest. A rollback is a re-apply with the previous tag, and the tag identifies the commit exactly.
Worker drainmissing — no SIGTERM handler (ch. 61). Mitigated by the durable queue: cut jobs are re-queued by the reaper within 60 s. A clean drain would save the wasted attempt.
RollbackRe-apply the previous image_tag. Schema rollbacks are not automatic and mostly should not exist — which is why rule 2 matters: if every migration is backward-compatible, rolling back code is always safe on its own.

What should also be in CI, and is not

  • Dependency audit. pnpm audit --audit-level high. Especially pointed here, since several dependencies are pinned to "latest" (ch. 67) — the lockfile is the only thing making a build reproducible.
  • Container scan. Trivy or ECR scan-on-push, failing the build on high-severity CVEs.
  • Terraform plan on pull requests, posted as a comment. Reviewing infrastructure without seeing the plan is reviewing prose.
  • A concurrency load test against the staging deployment — the biggest measurement gap in the system (ch. 50), and CI is where it would run for free.
  • Bundle-size budget on the web build, currently 452 KB in one chunk.
60
Chapter 60 · Infrastructure

Observability

What exists, what to build, and — the part that matters — the order, because instrumenting in the wrong order produces dashboards nobody looks at and leaves the questions you actually ask unanswerable.

What exists today

SignalStateDetail
Logsunstructuredconsole.log with box-drawing characters and emoji. Genuinely readable by a human at a terminal; unparseable, uncorrelatable, and unqueryable by anything else. No request id.
Metricsin-processservices/metrics.ts records LLM calls, tokens, embedding calls, latency and the model per operation — in a module-level singleton that dies with the process and is not attributed to a tenant.
TracesnoneNo OpenTelemetry, no spans, no correlation across the queue boundary.
HealthgoodThe strongest signal in the system. Database, vector extension and indexes, chat provider (including whether the model is pulled), embedding provider, model routing table, embedding space, auth state, and queue depth by lane. Every failure names its fix.
AuditgoodEvery allow and deny, queryable, indexed four ways. Not an observability system, but it answers "what did this credential see" better than most APM tools would.
Retrieval statsgoodRetrieveResult.stats returns the working set to the caller. Unusual and correct — it makes invariant 12 observable in production.

The instrumentation order, and why order matters

1
Structured logs with a request id. One JSON line per event: {level, msg, requestId, principalId, tenantId, domain, action, durationMs}. Generate the id in middleware; thread it through AsyncLocalStorage so no function signature changes.
Why first: without correlation you cannot reconstruct a single request, and every later signal is an aggregate you cannot drill into. A metric tells you something is wrong; a correlated log tells you what.
Extend it across the queue boundary — put the originating request id in jobs.metadata so a batch submitted at 09:00 can be traced through a job claimed at 14:00 on a different instance. That is the one thing a durable queue makes harder and the one thing that makes it debuggable.
2
RED metrics on every route. Rate, Errors, Duration — a histogram, not an average, because p95 is the number in the NFRs and an average hides it. Labels: route, status, domain.
Why second: this answers "is it working, and is it fast", which is 80% of what you ask, and it is where alerts come from.
3
The domain metrics nobody else would have. This is where a generic APM stops being enough, and it is the highest-value step per hour of work:
  • manifold_retrieval_subgraph_nodes — a histogram. If this climbs with corpus size, invariant 12 has regressed and retrieval is drifting back toward whole-corpus reads. No off-the-shelf tool would ever know to watch this.
  • manifold_chunk_failures_total{reason} — extraction quality, degrading (ch. 17).
  • manifold_llm_calls_total{operation,model} and tokens_total — the product's headline claim, per tenant, continuously.
  • manifold_queue_depth{lane} — already computed by queueDepth(); publish it. It is the autoscaling signal and the batch-progress answer.
  • manifold_job_attempts_total{type,outcome} — a rising retry rate is the earliest signal of an unhealthy dependency, well before jobs start failing.
  • manifold_audit_write_failures_total — the counter behind the deliberately-swallowed error (ch. 33).
4
Traces, last. OpenTelemetry spans for the retrieval pipeline — embed → annSeeds → expand → ppr → gather → fuse → mmr → verbalize — and for the job lifecycle (enqueued → claimed → running → terminal, linked by the request id from step 1).
Why last: traces are the most expensive to add and the most valuable once you already know which requests to look at. Adding them first gives you a beautiful waterfall of a request nobody complained about.

The dashboard that would actually be used

Is it working?
  • Request rate + error rate by route
  • Retrieval p50/p95 (the NFR)
  • Subgraph-nodes p95 — the invariant-12 canary
  • Queue depth by lane, and job attempts
  • /health component status
Is it costing what we think?
  • LLM calls / hour by operation and model
  • Tokens / hour, and estimated spend
  • LLM calls per answered query — should be exactly 1.0. Any drift is a bug in the thesis.
  • Chunk failure rate (the quality canary)
  • Per-tenant retrievals, once metering exists (ch. 36)
The principle Instrument the invariants, not just the infrastructure. CPU and memory are table stakes and any hosting provider gives them to you. "Did retrieval start scanning the corpus again?" and "is it still one model call per answer?" are questions only this system can answer about itself — and they are the ones whose answers change silently.
61
Chapter 61 · Infrastructure

Reliability & the degradation ladder

What fails, what should happen when it does, and the single highest-severity operational gap in the entire system — which is not a subtle one.

The degradation ladder

A well-designed system does not have "up" and "down". It has a ladder of states where each rung still serves something. Manifold's ladder is unusually good by construction, because the read path deliberately does not depend on the model provider:

FailureStill worksBehaviour
Chat provider downretrieval, graph, MCP evidence tools/api/field/retrieve is unaffected — zero generative calls by design. /query returns 503 naming the provider. Ingestion aborts papers after 3 consecutive failures rather than grinding. The most common outage costs the least.
Embedding provider downgraph reads onlyRetrieval cannot embed the query, so it fails. Graph browsing and provenance still work. A result cache (ch. 28) would keep repeated queries alive through this.
arXiv down / throttlingeverything except new arXiv ingestpoliteFetch backs off host-wide; the job re-queues and retries minutes later. Other connectors unaffected.
Worker instance dieseverythingLease lapses; the reaper re-queues within 60 s; another instance claims. Queued work is never lost. Ch. 16.
HNSW index missingcorrect, unusably slowSequential scan. No error. Caught only by checkVectorStorage() at startup and in /health — ch. 20.
Database downnothingTotal outage. Single point of failure by design (ch. 05), mitigated by Multi-AZ in the target topology.
Audit writes failingeverythingFire-and-forget; loud in logs, invisible to callers. Needs the alarm from ch. 56, or the degradation is silent.
Why that first row is an architectural achievement In most RAG systems, "the model provider is down" means total outage. Here it means evidence retrieval keeps working and only prose generation stops — because the expensive reasoning was moved into geometry that runs on your own database.

That is the reliability dividend of the central bet (ch. 02), and it was not designed for reliability. It is worth noticing when an architectural choice pays out in a dimension it was not chosen for: those are usually the choices that were right for structural reasons rather than tactical ones.

The highest-severity gap: backups

There is no backup story, and the data cannot be regenerated cheaply Today: a Docker volume. No snapshot schedule, no point-in-time recovery, no restore drill, no defined RPO or RTO.

What is at risk is not just "data". The corpus is the accumulated output of one model call per chunk across every document ever ingested — real money and, at ~15 minutes per paper (ch. 16), real days of GPU time. Losing it means re-ingesting everything, and the extraction is non-deterministic, so the rebuilt graph would not even be the same graph. Every stored provenance span would point at a slightly different extraction.

This ranks above RLS, above CI, above observability. It is the one gap where the failure is unrecoverable.
1
RDS automated backups, 35-day PITR. One line of Terraform (ch. 56). Do it with the first deploy.
2
Cross-region snapshot copies. A region is a failure domain; so is an account.
3
Define RPO and RTO explicitly. Suggested: RPO 5 minutes (PITR gives this), RTO 1 hour. Numbers you have not written down are numbers you will not meet.
4
A restore drill, quarterly, on a schedule, in the calendar. Restore into a scratch instance, run pnpm test:db against it, and verify checkVectorStorage() reports every index present. An untested backup is a hypothesis — and vector indexes are exactly the thing a naive pg_dump/restore gets subtly wrong.

Graceful shutdown, written out

let shuttingDown = false;

async function shutdown(signal: string) {
  if (shuttingDown) return;
  shuttingDown = true;
  log.info({ signal }, 'shutting down');

  // 1. Stop claiming new work. In-flight jobs keep running and keep renewing
  //    their leases, so nothing else steals them while we drain.
  stopWorkers();

  // 2. Stop accepting new HTTP connections; let in-flight requests finish.
  await server.close();

  // 3. Wait for running jobs, bounded. ECS gives 30 s by default;
  //    stopTimeout can be raised to 120 s in the task definition.
  await waitForRunningJobs({ timeoutMs: 25_000 });

  // 4. Anything still running is about to be killed. Release its lease NOW
  //    rather than making the reaper wait out the full TTL — the difference
  //    between a job resuming in seconds and resuming in two minutes.
  await releaseLeases(runningJobIds());

  await closeDb(5);
  process.exit(0);
}

process.on('SIGTERM', () => void shutdown('SIGTERM'));
process.on('SIGINT',  () => void shutdown('SIGINT'));

Step 4 is the one the durable queue makes possible and worthwhile: proactively releasing a lease turns a two-minute recovery into a two-second one. The queue already tracks runningJobIds and stopWorkers() already exists — this is perhaps thirty lines, and it is the last piece of the deploy story.

The remaining reliability gaps

GapSeverityFix
No backupscriticalAbove. Highest priority in the entire operational backlog.
No graceful shutdownmediumThirty lines. Downgraded by the durable queue.
No circuit breaker on providersmediumPer-paper abort (invariant 10) approximates it per job. A process-wide breaker would stop all workers hammering a dead provider.
Connection pool max: 10mediumNever load-tested (ch. 50). With N api tasks + M worker tasks all polling the queue every second, total connections is (N+M) × 10 — which needs checking against the RDS instance's max_connections before scaling out.
Queue poll adds constant DB loadlowTwo SELECT … SKIP LOCKED per instance per second when idle. Trivial at this scale; worth knowing it grows linearly with instance count.
No chaos testinglowThe kill-9 test (ch. 16) is a real start and covers the most valuable case.
62
Chapter 62 · Infrastructure

Runbooks

Seven incidents, each with the symptom as it will actually present, the diagnosis path, and the fix. Written in the order of "what you will see first", because that is the only order that is useful at 3 a.m.

R1 · "Retrieval got slow" — p95 climbing over days or weeks

1
Check stats.subgraphNodes on a live query. Flat as the corpus grew ⇒ the algorithm is fine, this is storage. Climbing ⇒ an expansion bound regressed (invariant 12).
2
GET /health → services.vectors. A missing HNSW index gives correct results by sequential scan with no error anywhere (ch. 20).
3
Storage path: is the index still in cache? Compare index size against shared_buffers. Ch. 28's finding — dead data in the table evicts live index pages.
4
Levers in order: halfvec, lower hnsw.ef_search, larger shared_buffers, per-domain partitioning (ch. 64).

R2 · "Answers are worse" — no error, no alert

1
Did the embedding model change? /health → embeddingSpace, then SELECT space, count(*) FROM node_vectors GROUP BY space. More than one row is a mixed-space corpus — the invariant-13 disaster (ch. 19), and everything downstream is noise.
2
Did retrieval tuning change? Run pnpm --filter api eval and compare to the scorecard in ch. 48. This is what the harness is for.
3
Did extraction degrade? SELECT processing_error FROM papers WHERE processing_error IS NOT NULL ORDER BY updated_at DESC — partial chunk failures are recorded there even on completed papers (ch. 17).
4
Did the model routing change? /health → models.routes. A MODEL_EXTRACT downgrade costs graph density permanently (ch. 43).

R3 · "A batch is stuck" / "Everything is queued"

1
GET /health → queue. A deep process queue with running: 1 is not a bug — it is the GPU (ch. 16). Do the arithmetic: depth × 15 min ÷ PROCESS_CONCURRENCY.
2
GET /api/ingest/batches/:id for per-status counts and per-job errors.
3
Depth high and running: 0 ⇒ no worker is claiming. Is startWorkers() running in this deployment? Is the worker service scaled to zero? Is the handler registered for that job type (an unregistered type re-queues forever)?
4
Rows with high attempts and repeating errors ⇒ a real dependency failure, not a queue problem. Read jobs.error.
5
If a genuine dead-end is retrying, the handler is missing a PermanentJobError case. That is a code fix, not an ops action.

R4 · "Ingestion is failing" — jobs failing en masse

1
/health → services.llm. Naming the exact fix is the point: "Model X not pulled. Fix: ollama pull X".
2
Errors mentioning "aborted after N consecutive chunks" ⇒ the model is unreachable, not the documents (invariant 10).
3
arXiv 429s ⇒ lower FETCH_CONCURRENCY or raise ARXIV_MIN_INTERVAL_MS. The queue retries these; check whether they are actually failing or merely slow.
4
Log line "worker unavailable — parsing on the main thread; the event loop WILL stall" ⇒ the PDF worker is failing to spawn. Latency across all endpoints will be affected (ch. 11).

R5 · "A tenant says they can see another tenant's data"

Treat as a P0 incident, in this order 1. Get the exact request: endpoint, ?domain=, credential prefix. 2. GET /api/admin/audit?principalId=… — every allow and deny for that credential. 3. Reproduce against staging with the same shape. 4. Run pnpm test:db: 70 tests, many of which are exactly this class. 5. Check for corrupt data — a boundary-crossing edge:
select e.id from edges e join nodes s on s.id=e.source_id join nodes t on t.id=e.target_id where coalesce(s.domain,'default') <> coalesce(t.domain,'default');
That query should return zero rows. If it does not, one corrupt row leaks a connected component (ch. 21) and the priority is finding how it was written, not just deleting it.
6. Also check the one known leak: GET /api/admin/audit is currently cross-tenant readable (ch. 35 A10).

R6 · "The graph has duplicate entities"

1
Look for the log line "domain X has at least 2000 nodes; entity resolution only compares against the most recent". That is the candidate-window cap (ch. 13) and it is the most likely cause at any real corpus size.
2
done Resolution reads the index (ch. 13). If duplicates still appear, they are false splits from the merge guard, not window misses — check the [resolve] keeping "…" and "…" apart logs, which state the reason for every refusal.
3
If duplicates are near-synonyms rather than exact repeats, SIM_THRESHOLD = 0.82 may be too high. Sweep it against gold.ts's ResolutionCases (aliases must merge, distractors must not) before changing it — and remember over-merging is irreversible where fragmenting is not.
4
If an edge attached to the wrong node, suspect findEntityId's substring fallback (ch. 15) — "GS" matching "3D Gaussian Splatting".

R7 · "We need to change the embedding model"

Not an incident, but the most dangerous planned change in the system, and the one most likely to be attempted as a config edit. Full procedure in chapter 19. The short version: it is a migration plus a full re-embed plus a hyperbolic retrain plus a community rebuild, and there is no zero-downtime path today. Refuse the config edit.

Part IX

Scale & cost

What breaks first at each order of magnitude, the fix for each, and what the whole thing costs per tenant. Written as a sequence of next constraints rather than a target architecture, because the useful question is never "how would we build this for a million users" — it is "what is the very next thing that stops working".

63
Chapter 63 · Scale

How this scales: 1 → 1,000 tenants

Five stages. At each one: what breaks first, what it costs to fix, and — the part that matters — what does not need to change, because knowing which parts of an architecture are already correct at the next scale is what stops premature rewrites.

Stage 1 — one tenant, one domain, ~10k entities

Statewhere the code is today

One API process (workers in-process), one Postgres, one Ollama or an OpenAI key. Retrieval p50 well under 100 ms. Ingestion is minutes per paper.

Everything works. This is the shape the system was developed and measured against.

What breaks first

Nothing technical. The gaps are operational: no backups (ch. 61), no CI (ch. 59), no container image (ch. 53).

The instinct at this stage is to build for stage 3. Resist it. The correct work here is the boring operational floor, plus chapter 51's measurement list — because the measurements tell you which stage-3 problem is real.

Stage 2 — a handful of tenants, ~100k entities, real ingest volume

BreaksFixEffort
Retrieval latency drifts — 153 ms p50 / 193 ms p95, up from 69/102 at 50kStorage-side, not algorithmic: the working set stayed flat (ch. 28). Raise shared_buffers to hold the indexes; consider halfvec.hours
Entity resolution starts duplicatingwindow removed Candidates come from the index, so this is no longer a scale limit (ch. 13). Duplicates now mean either a false split from the merge guard — every refusal logs its reason — or a corpus split across domains, which looks identical (ch. 17b). Check select domain, count(*) from papers group by 1 first.hours
A batch runs for a dayalready solved — the durable queue survives restarts and retries interrupted work (ch. 16). This is what stage 2 would otherwise have forced.—
Deploys interrupt workGraceful shutdown (ch. 61). Thirty lines; the queue makes it cheap.hours
Audit log grows unboundedPartition by month + a drop schedule. And fix the cross-tenant read (ch. 35 A10) before tenant two exists.1 day
What does not change The retrieval algorithm, the schema, the isolation model, the queue, the connector contract, the API surface. Stage 2 is entirely tuning and operational work — which is the payoff for having bounded the hot path by parameters rather than by corpus size (invariant 12).

Stage 3 — tens of tenants, ~1M entities, concurrent queries

This is the first stage that demands architectural change rather than tuning.

BreaksFixEffort
The 1M-entity latency target — unproven, currently 193 ms p95 at 100k against a <150 ms goalIn order: halfvec (halves index + table size), lower hnsw.ef_search, Matryoshka-truncated dimensions, per-domain partitioning. Measure each with bench:retrieval.1–2 weeks
Concurrency — never measuredThe single biggest measurement gap (ch. 50). Connection pool is max: 10 per process; with N api + M worker tasks, total is (N+M) × 10 against RDS max_connections. Measure before scaling out, not after.days
Rate limiting is per-instanceN instances permit N× the limit. Move the windows to Redis, and re-key on principal.id rather than IP while you are in there (ch. 34).2 days
One tenant's batch blocks everyonePer-tenant fairness in claiming — round-robin rather than strict ORDER BY created_at. Plus a priority column so an interactive ingest can jump a bulk backlog (ch. 16).2–3 days
No quotas; abuse is unboundedusage_events + per-tenant ceilings (ch. 36). Also the prerequisite for billing.1 week
A forgotten filter is one bug from a leakPostgres RLS beneath the application check (ch. 36). At this stage it stops being defence-in-depth and starts being table stakes for diligence.1 week
Per-domain partitioning: the scaling fix that is also the isolation model Partition nodes, edges, propositions and the vector tables by domain. Each partition gets its own smaller HNSW index, and every hot-path query already carries a domain predicate, so partition pruning is automatic and requires no query changes at all.

The isolation model and the scaling model turn out to be the same model. That is a strong signal the boundary was drawn in the right place — architectural boundaries that pay off twice usually reflect something true about the domain rather than a convenience.

Stage 4 — hundreds of tenants, ~10M entities

BreaksFix
One Postgres primary — write throughput on nodes/edges, and index memory across all tenantsRead replicas for retrieval. Not for the queue — a SKIP LOCKED claim is an UPDATE and must hit the primary. Then shard by tenant: tenants are perfectly separable, so this is the easy kind of sharding.
Ingest cost dominatesAn extraction cache keyed on hash(chunk text) + model + prompt version. Corpora overlap far more than people expect — the same paper ingested by three tenants is currently extracted three times.
Batch layers are staleHyperbolic training and community detection are on-demand batch jobs (ch. 26–27). At this scale they need incremental maintenance, triggered by ingest volume.
The code-owned domain registryA new vertical is a code release (ch. 29). Move to the DB-backed, tenant-owned registry. This is the change that makes the product self-serve, and it is a Phase 4 item that keeps getting deferred.
Single-tenant deployments demandedThe likely trigger for the Pulumi switch (ch. 57): per-tenant infrastructure means resource counts driven by application state.

Stage 5 — thousands of tenants, 100M+ entities

Beyond anything this design has been measured for, so this is reasoning rather than evidence — and it is labelled as such:

  • Tenant sharding becomes the primary axis. A shard map, tenant → cluster. Cross-tenant queries do not exist by design, which makes this the tractable case.
  • The vector layer may outgrow pgvector. At 100M vectors a dedicated ANN engine's quantisation and disk-based indexes start to matter. The seam is annSeeds() and the proposition gather — two functions. That is what the single-datastore bet costs, and it is a bounded cost, which is why the bet was reasonable.
  • The graph layer probably does not. Bounded 3-hop traversal over indexed edges stays a Postgres-shaped problem; it is unbounded traversal that forces a graph database, and this design never performs one.
  • Extraction becomes a fleet. The process lane scales horizontally the moment extraction is hosted rather than local — the queue already supports it; only PROCESS_CONCURRENCY changes.

The summary table

StageEntitiesFirst constraintCharacter of the work
110kOperational floor (backups, CI, image)Boring. Do it anyway.
2100kBuffer cache; resolution windowTuning. No architecture changes.
31MIndex size; concurrency; multi-instance stateFirst real architectural work. Partitioning, Redis, RLS, quotas.
410MSingle primary; ingest costReplicas, sharding, extraction cache, DB-backed registry.
5100M+The vector layer itselfSpeculative. Two functions are the seam.
The meta-point Nothing in stages 1–4 requires rewriting the retrieval algorithm, the schema, the isolation model, or the queue. That is the return on bounding the hot path by parameters instead of by corpus size, and on making the durability guarantee a property of the database rather than of a process. An architecture is good not when it is fast today but when the next order of magnitude is a configuration change and the one after that is a known, bounded piece of work.
64
Chapter 64 · Scale

The scaling ladder, per component

The same territory as chapter 63, cut the other way: one component at a time, from what exists to what it becomes, with the trigger for each step. Use this when you own one surface and need to know what is next for it.

Retrieval

✓
Now: HNSW ANN seeds, bounded expansion, in-memory PPR, three-signal RRF, MMR. 153 ms p50 at 100k.
1
halfvec — float16 vectors. Halves index and table size at negligible cosine-recall cost. Trigger: index size approaches shared_buffers. Migration + index rebuild.
2
Lower hnsw.ef_search — currently the default 40 for k=8, which is generous. Trigger: try it immediately; it is a session variable. Verify recall with eval.
3
Result cache keyed on (domain, quantised query embedding). Trigger: measure the repeat rate first — agent traffic is repetitive in theory, and this is worth nothing if yours is not.
4
Per-domain partitioning. Automatic pruning, no query changes. Trigger: a single domain's index no longer fits comfortably in cache.
5
Read replicas for the retrieval path. Trigger: read load affects write latency. Note the queue's claims must stay on the primary.
6
Dedicated ANN engine. Trigger: ANN alone exceeds ~40% of retrieval p95 at target size. Seam: annSeeds() + the proposition gather.

The queue

✓
Now: Postgres SKIP LOCKED claims, two lanes, leases, retries, reaper, admission control. Already multi-instance.
1
Graceful shutdown — stopWorkers() on SIGTERM, drain, release leases (ch. 61). Trigger: the first rolling deploy.
2
Priority column in the claim's ORDER BY. Trigger: an interactive ingest queues behind a bulk batch and someone complains. One line.
3
Per-tenant fair claiming — round-robin rather than global FIFO. Trigger: tenant two submits a batch.
4
Dead-letter handling — a route to inspect and re-queue failed jobs, rather than re-submitting the document.
5
Broker (BullMQ/Redis, or River/pgboss). Trigger: claim contention shows up in query latency — thousands of jobs per minute — or you need delayed/scheduled execution. The enqueue/claim contract keeps this local to one file (ch. 16).

Ingestion

✓
Now: two lanes, PDF parsing in a worker thread, retries with permanent/transient classification, batches.
1
Hosted extraction (MODEL_EXTRACT=openai:…). The single biggest throughput change available — it moves the process lane from GPU-bound to network-bound, so PROCESS_CONCURRENCY above 1 becomes genuinely parallel instead of buying queueing.
2
Horizontal worker tasks. Already supported — run more instances, they claim from the same table. Trigger: the lane is saturated and extraction is hosted.
3
Extraction cache on hash(chunk) + model + prompt version. Trigger: the same documents ingested by multiple tenants.
4
ANN blocking for resolution (ch. 13). Removes the candidate window entirely and makes resolution independent of corpus size. Trigger: the cap warning appears in logs.
5
Section filtering — never extract from references or acknowledgments. Cheap, and it improves graph quality more than a model upgrade (ch. 11).

Governance

✓
Now: tenants, principals, scoped keys, per-domain grants, one chokepoint, full audit of allows and denies.
1
Fix the cross-tenant audit read (ch. 35 A10). One WHERE. Trigger: today.
2
SSRF guard on connector fetches (ch. 35 A8). A shared assertPublicUrl() so the next connector inherits it.
3
Quotas + metering. usage_events, thread the principal through metrics.ts. Unlocks billing. Trigger: the first paying customer.
4
Row-level security. Makes a forgotten filter harmless rather than merely detectable. Trigger: a security review, or the third domain-scoped surface added by someone new.
5
DB-backed, tenant-owned domain registry. Removes the code deploy from onboarding a vertical. Trigger: the second customer who wants their own ontology.

The order, if you can only do five things

1
Backups + a restore drill. The only unrecoverable failure in the system (ch. 61).
2
Dockerfile + CI. Nothing else is deployable or verifiable without them, and 250 tests currently run only when someone remembers.
3
Fix the cross-tenant audit read. One line, and it is a live multi-tenancy defect in the governance layer itself.
4
Score extraction quality against gold.ts. It caps every other quality number, the instrument now exists, and it tells you whether the rest of the roadmap is even aimed correctly.
5
The "Ask" console page. Converts the differentiated capability into something a non-engineer can see in ten seconds (ch. 41).

Note what is not in that list: partitioning, RLS, Redis, sharding, streaming. All correct eventually, none of them the next constraint. The discipline is to fix what is broken before optimising what is not.

65
Chapter 65 · Scale

Cost engineering & unit economics

What a tenant costs, where the money actually goes, and the levers in order of impact. Modelled rather than measured — the instrumentation records tokens but not dollars (ch. 45) — and labelled accordingly.

The cost model, per tenant per month

Assumptions: 10,000-document corpus, 20% refreshed monthly, 50,000 queries/month, 30% verbalized.

LineEst. / monthDerivation
Ingest — extraction~$262,000 docs × 26 chunks × ~800 tokens at gpt-4o-mini rates. The dominant marginal cost.
Ingest — embeddings~$1~2 calls/chunk at text-embedding-3-small. Nearly free per document; material at 10M documents.
Query — embeddings~$0.5050,000 query embeddings. The "zero LLM calls" asterisk (ch. 02).
Query — verbalization~$415,000 calls × ~700 tokens. Naive RAG at ~5,000 tokens of context would be ~$25 for the same answers.
Database~$700 ÷ Ndb.r6g.2xlarge Multi-AZ + replica + storage, shared across tenants. Per-tenant only when a customer demands isolation.
Compute~$180 ÷ N2 api + 1 worker Fargate task, shared.
Network / NAT / logs~$60 ÷ NSingle NAT (ch. 56), CloudWatch retention.
At 20 tenants on shared infrastructure: ≈ $79/tenant/month — of which $32 is marginal (model calls) and $47 is amortised infrastructure.
The number that decides the business model Marginal cost is dominated by ingestion, not by queries. That inverts the usual SaaS shape, where usage drives cost, and it has three direct consequences:

1. Pricing should be anchored to corpus size and refresh rate, not to query volume. A per-query price mis-prices the product in both directions.
2. A tenant who ingests once and queries constantly is enormously profitable. One who re-ingests weekly and rarely queries is the opposite — and the naive-RAG break-even (ch. 45) says they should not be a customer.
3. An extraction cache is the highest-leverage cost lever available, because it attacks the dominant term directly.

The levers, in order of impact

1
Extraction cache on hash(chunk) + model + prompt version. Corpora overlap far more than anyone expects — arXiv papers, public documentation, standard runbooks. Could remove most of the dominant cost line at multi-tenant scale. Nothing else on this list is close.
2
Structured connectors wherever possible (ch. 9). Not a discount — zero. A 500-operation API surface costs nothing to import and would be ~500 model calls as text.
3
Per-role model routing (ch. 43). Already built, and the measured allocation is counter-intuitive: pay for extraction, economise on verbalization. Getting this backwards costs more and produces a sparser graph.
4
MMR's character budget. Already an order of magnitude below naive RAG. Tightening maxChars trades answer quality for tokens — measure with eval before touching it (invariant 23).
5
Result caching for repeated queries. Removes both the embedding and the verbalization call on a hit. Value depends entirely on your repeat rate — measure it first.
6
Infrastructure right-sizing. Graviton, reserved instances, single NAT, log retention. Real money, and last — it is the only line that does not scale with customers, so effort here does not compound.

What is not costed, honestly

  • Re-ingestion on a model change. Changing MODEL_EXTRACT to improve graph quality means re-extracting the corpus — the full ingest line again, for every tenant. Nothing budgets for it and it will happen at least once.
  • Re-embedding on an embedding-model change. Cheaper, but touches every node and proposition, plus a hyperbolic retrain and community rebuild (ch. 19).
  • The GPU, if extraction stays local. The whole model assumes hosted extraction. On local hardware the marginal cost is electricity and the constraint is 15 minutes per paper (ch. 16) — a completely different economics with the same architecture.
  • Support and onboarding. Until the operator console and the DB-backed registry exist (ch. 41, 29), onboarding a vertical costs engineering time. That is the largest uncosted line in the model and the strongest commercial argument for Phase 4.
66
Chapter 66 · Scale

Performance engineering

Where the time actually goes on both paths, and the critical-path surgery in priority order — with the unmeasured parts labelled, because a performance chapter that guesses is worse than none.

Read path — where the 153 ms goes

StageEst. shareCharacter, and the lever
embed(query)~30–50%Network to the embedding provider. Often the largest single term and the least discussed. Levers: queryVector passthrough, a result cache, or a local embedding model.
ANN seeds~10%One indexed query. Levers: ef_search, halfvec.
Expansion~20%Three round trips, one per hop. Lever: a recursive CTE would make it one — at the cost of readability, and it has not been measured as worth it.
PPR~5%~45,000 multiply-adds in JS. Not the problem at this working-set size.
Three signals~20%Three queries, issued sequentially. They are independent — Promise.all is free parallelism nobody has taken.
RRF + MMR~10%~30M multiply-adds. Lever: maxCandidates.
These proportions are reasoned, not profiled There is no flame graph of the retrieval path. The end-to-end number is measured (bench:retrieval); the breakdown is inferred from what each stage does. The first honest performance task is to instrument the stages (ch. 60, step 4) — anything else is optimising by intuition, which is the mistake chapter 49 is about.

Critical-path surgery, in priority order

1
Parallelise the three evidence queries. They are independent, they are already written as three separate awaits, and Promise.all turns ~20% of the path into ~7%. Perhaps the best effort-to-payoff ratio in the entire system.
2
Instrument the stages. Turn the table above from estimates into measurements. Everything below this step is guesswork until it is done.
3
Streaming verbalization (ch. 41). Does not reduce total time; changes perceived latency from 3 s to 400 ms, which is the number a human experiences.
4
Result cache. Removes the embedding round trip and the whole pipeline on a hit. Measure the repeat rate first.
5
halfvec + lower ef_search. Attacks the storage term that measurement showed is the real growth driver (ch. 28).

Write path — where the 15 minutes go

~95% is the model. 34 s/chunk × 26 chunks. Everything else — chunking, embedding, resolution, validation, inserts — is a rounding error against it, with two exceptions worth naming:

  • The 500 ms inter-chunk sleep in the processor: 13 seconds on a 26-chunk paper, for no benefit when the provider is a hosted API. It should be provider-aware (ch. 11).
  • Resolution is O(mentions × 2,000) in JavaScript per chunk. Invisible next to a 34-second model call, and it becomes visible the moment extraction is hosted and fast. ANN blocking fixes it (ch. 13).
The rule this illustrates When one term is 95% of the cost, everything else is noise — until that term changes. Moving extraction from a local GPU to a hosted API takes 34 s/chunk down toward 2 s and instantly promotes two previously-invisible costs into the critical path. Optimising the other 5% today is wasted work; knowing exactly which 5% will matter tomorrow is not.

The thing most likely to bite, and it is not on either list

Concurrency is completely unmeasured Every number in this book is sequential. Nobody has measured p95 at 50 concurrent queries. The connection pool is max: 10 per process. PPR and MMR run on the main thread, so N concurrent retrievals contend for one CPU — and unlike the PDF parse (ch. 11), nobody has checked whether that blocks the event loop enough to matter.

That is the measurement most likely to produce a surprise rather than a confirmation, which makes it the most valuable one to run. It is also nearly free: the harnesses exist, and CI could run it against staging.
Part X

Judgement

The part that is hard to hire for. Every open defect stated plainly, the bugs that encode principles, how decisions actually get made here, what the product is worth and to whom, and the recipes and checklists that make the rest of this book operational.

67
Chapter 67 · Judgement

The defect register

Every known defect in one table, ranked by severity, including the ones found while writing this book. A register you would be embarrassed to hand a buyer is a register that is not being maintained — this is the same list you would hand them.

Open — security & correctness

IDDefectSeverityDetail & fix
D1Audit log is cross-tenant readablecriticalGET /api/admin/audit requires admin and returns every tenant's rows — no WHERE tenant_id = principal.tenantId. Invisible with one customer; a cross-tenant leak in the governance layer itself with two. One line. Ch. 33, 35 A10.
D2SSRF in connector fetchhighThe OpenAPI connector validates scheme but not destination — 169.254.169.254 and private ranges are reachable, and the response becomes graph nodes readable via retrieval. Fix: a shared assertPublicUrl() with redirect re-validation, so the next connector inherits it. Ch. 35 A8.
D3GET /api/papers/processing is not domain-filteredhighReturns in-progress papers across every domain, including titles, to any authenticated caller. A real isolation leak that the 70-test suite does not cover because the endpoint takes no ?domain=.
D4No backupscriticalNot a code defect, and the only unrecoverable failure in the system. The corpus is days of GPU time and extraction is non-deterministic, so a rebuild is not the same graph. Ch. 61.
D5findEntityId substring fallbackmediumA second, far weaker entity resolver running after the careful one. "GS" matches "3D Gaussian Splatting"; first-match-wins over an unordered Map, so the result depends on insertion order. The most likely source of remaining silent graph corruption. Ch. 15.
D6Dead condition in resolve-embed.tsmediumThe nested if (nodeType !== cand.type) is always true — the block reduces to continue. Behaviour happens to match intent. A correct outcome reached by incorrect code, which is the most dangerous kind: the next refactor has a coin-flip chance of inverting it. Ch. 13.
D7No auth-failure rate limitmediumEvery failed authentication writes an audit row, with no limit on that path and no retention on the table. Unbounded write amplification. Ch. 35 A9.
D8/api/ingest/status/:jobId has no ownership checklowAny authenticated caller can read any job id. Ids are unguessable, so low severity — still wrong, and trivially fixed once jobs carry a tenant.

Open — product surface & hygiene

IDDefectSeverityDetail
D93DGS hardcoded into the public APImedium/api/graph/queries/improves-3dgs and extends-3dgs. A prospect notices in under a minute. Replace with /queries/relationships?type=&target=. Ch. 37.
D10Benchmark defaults are 3DGS-specificmediumGET /api/field/benchmark is meaningless in any other domain. Evaluation sets belong in the domain registry.
D11Resolution prompt teaches "3DGS" to every domainmediumAcronym examples should come from DomainConfig.entityExamples. Two lines, real quality cost in any non-graphics domain. Ch. 44.
D12MCP tool descriptions name a verticallowAn agent reads them. Generate from the registry. Ch. 39.
D13Web console cannot authenticatehighfetchAPI sends no Authorization header, so the UI is unusable in the only configuration safe to deploy (AUTH_MODE=required). Ch. 40.
D14MCP retrievals are not auditedmediumThe HTTP path records every retrieval; the MCP path records none. A hole in the compliance story ("reconstruct what this agent saw"). The principal exists; only the recordAudit call is missing. Ch. 39.
D15Dependencies pinned to "latest"mediumdrizzle-orm, hono, postgres, zod, pdf-parse, typescript. Reproducibility rests entirely on the lockfile. There is also no pnpm audit in CI, because there is no CI.
D16packages/shared declares closed type unionslowNodeType/EdgeType are closed unions, contradicting invariant 19. Harmless today (the API returns text) and a trap for a frontend that trusts them.
D17ASCII coercion destroys scientific notationlow[^\x20-\x7E\n] → ' ' in cleanExtractedText: "α-blending" → " -blending", "Müller" → "M ller". Needs a Unicode-aware cleaner. Ch. 11.
D18Validation matrix is global, ontologies are per-domainmediumThe api-surface domain gets no type validation — nothing stops schema --requires--> endpoint. Fix: optional typeRules on DomainConfig. Ch. 14.
D19Structured JSON output not constrained at the samplermediumBoth providers support it natively; instead there is regex repair of malformed JSON that can change string semantics. ~20 lines to replace a rung-5 mechanism with a rung-1 one. Ch. 44.
D20Communities are built and never readlowRows are written, embedded, and no retrieval path consults them. Also the only vector column still jsonb, so it cannot be ANN-searched. Ch. 27.
D21agents/repair.ts has no route or schedulelowContradiction detection exists and nothing invokes it. Ch. 14, 51.
D22Hyperbolic training is non-deterministiclowBare Math.random() for init and negative sampling, while every other stochastic component was made deterministic. A seeded PRNG already exists in seed-load.ts. Ch. 26.
D23Hyperbolic persistence is a loop of single-row updateslowOne UPDATE … RETURNING per node — 100k round trips at scale. One UPDATE … FROM (VALUES …) fixes it. Ch. 26.
D24Docs are stalelowREADME still claims "117 unit + 40 DB tests"; the real numbers are 180 + 70. Small, and exactly the rot chapter 69 warns about.

Closed — kept because the pattern recurs everywhere

DefectThe transferable lesson
AI SDK version drift produced empty graphsAn abstraction with a hidden version contract, plus error swallowing. Two defects compounding into "papers marked completed with no data" on the documented default configuration. → Do not abstract over a boundary whose failure modes you need to see.
cosine() truncated to the shorter operandA 1536-d vector scored 0.71 against a 768-d one: a confident number from unrelated coordinate systems. → The worst bugs return values of the right type in the right range.
.limit(500) with no ORDER BYPostgres returned an arbitrary subset; every mention outside it silently became a duplicate entity, compounding with corpus size. → A LIMIT without an ORDER BY is a non-deterministic result masquerading as a bounded one.
Retrieval read every vector in the database21 s p50 at 100k. Worked perfectly at demo scale. → Any operation proportional to corpus size is a bug the moment the corpus is a variable.
JSONB copies evicted the HNSW indexesData that was never read caused a 1.8× latency regression by taking the cache. → Unused data is not free.
Startup swept all non-terminal jobsTwo processes sharing a database silently killed each other's live work. → "The server restarted" is knowledge about this process, not about the world.
Queued work lived only in process memoryA restart during a 20-hour batch lost every job not yet started. The status was durable; the work was not. → Persisting a mirror of state is not the same as persisting the state.
split('_') on a base64url secretThree keys in four rejected as malformed — an intermittent auth failure that looks like anything but a parser. → Know your encoding's alphabet before you choose a delimiter.
X-Forwarded-For trusted unconditionallyMade the rate limit opt-out; the 'global' fallback made it a self-DoS. Two failures in one three-line function. → Client-supplied headers are input, not identity.
Rate limit counted status pollsPolling the jobs you just created returned 429. → Shape a limit like the cost curve, never like the URL tree.
.sort(() => Math.random() - 0.5)An inconsistent comparator is not a shuffle; communities depended on V8's sort internals. → If a result depends on randomness, it must be correct randomness.
PDF parsing blocked the event loop400 ms of zero timer ticks per document; invisible until something else needed the loop. → In a single-threaded runtime, synchronous CPU is a shared resource.
Type normaliser folded unknown typesThe first structured connector's entire taxonomy silently became concept. → A defensive default that discards information is worse than an error.
PPR α raised on a correct argumentImproved the proxy, halved multi-hop nDCG. → Optimising a proxy that does not map to the outcome is the hardest mistake to detect, because the proxy improves.
Why the closed section is longer than the open one A register that only lists open items is a to-do list. The closed section is where the judgement lives: each line is a class of mistake that will be re-proposed by someone reasonable within two years.

Notice how many of them share one shape — a failure that produces plausible output instead of an error. Empty graphs, confident cosines, arbitrary subsets, silent duplication, a queue that reports queued for work nobody will run. That is the failure mode this codebase is organised against, and it is why "no fabricated success" outranks almost everything else in chapter 04.
68
Chapter 68 · Judgement

War stories that encode principles

Six bugs, told properly. Not because the bugs matter — they are fixed — but because each one is a class, and recognising the class in unfamiliar code is most of what senior engineering is.

1 · The system that lied about its own emptiness

ollama-ai-provider@1.2.0 implements AI SDK spec v1; the installed ai@5 accepts only v2. Every Ollama completion threw before a byte hit the network. The callers caught it and returned {entities: [], relationships: []}. Papers ingested on the documented default configuration were marked completed with an empty graph — and then answered future questions with "no evidence found", permanently, with nothing recording that they had never been read.

The class: compounding defects, each individually survivable Neither defect alone is fatal. A version mismatch that throws is a loud, ten-minute fix. Error swallowing with a working provider is latent. Together they produced silent, permanent, invisible data loss on the happy path.

Look for pairs. Ask of any catch block: "what is the worst thing that could be happening upstream, and would I be able to tell?" If the answer is "no", the catch is not handling an error, it is hiding one.

2 · The confident wrong number

cosine() iterated Math.min(a.length, b.length). Comparing a 1536-dimension OpenAI vector to a 768-dimension Ollama vector returned 0.71 — a plausible similarity computed from the first 768 coordinates of two unrelated coordinate systems.

The class: valid-looking output from invalid input No crash, no exception, no log line. A float in the right range, distributed plausibly, ranked confidently. Every dashboard green while retrieval quietly became noise.

Numerical code needs preconditions asserted, not inferred, because its failure mode is a number rather than an error. Math.min in a vector operation is almost always a bug wearing defensive-programming clothes: it converts "these are incompatible" into "these are compatible enough".

3 · The arbitrary subset

Entity resolution compared against .limit(500) with no ORDER BY. Past 500 nodes, Postgres returned an arbitrary subset — different at the planner's discretion — and every mention outside it silently became a duplicate entity.

The class: correctness that degrades with data volume The bug did not exist at 400 nodes. It appeared gradually, compounded with corpus size, and was invisible until someone read the node list by hand.

Any LIMIT without an ORDER BY is a non-deterministic result set masquerading as a bounded one. And the fix has three parts, all necessary: make the bound deterministic, configurable, and observable. A bound nobody can see is a bound nobody will raise in time.

4 · The data that was never read and still cost 1.8×

Keeping embedding jsonb alongside embedding_vec vector(768) looked like harmless redundancy during a migration. JSONB stores each float as decimal text (~15 KB per vector) against pgvector's packed float4 (~3 KB), which inflated the vector tables to ~1 GB each and evicted 400 MB of HNSW index from the buffer cache. Doubling the corpus cost 1.8× p50 with no change in work done.

The class: a storage decision that is secretly a latency decision The columns were never selected. They still destroyed performance, because table pages and index pages compete for the same cache.

Unused data is not free. This generalises further than it looks: wide tables with rarely-read columns, oversized blobs, "just in case" denormalised copies — all of them tax every query that touches the table, whether or not the column appears in the SELECT.

5 · The recovery that destroyed live work, twice over

First version: startup failed every non-terminal job, on the reasoning "the server restarted, so anything running must be orphaned". Correct with one process; with two — a second instance, a test run, tsx watch reloading on a file save — it silently killed the other's in-flight ingestion. Ownership and leases fixed that.

Second version: ownership was correct, and queued work still lived only as closures in one process's memory. A restart during a 100-document batch lost every job not yet started. The jobs table said queued for work that nothing would ever run.

The class: mistaking a mirror for the thing Both versions had a durable jobs table. Both looked, from outside, like a durable queue. The first bug was about authority ("what do I actually know?"); the second was about location ("where does the work actually live?").

Persisting a mirror of state is not the same as persisting the state. The test that distinguishes them is brutal and simple: kill the process at a random moment and see what survives. That test now exists, and it is the only reason the current design can be claimed rather than believed.

6 · The correct argument that made retrieval worse

PPR's restart probability was raised from 0.15 to 0.5 because on a star graph the seed scored 0.18 against a hub's 0.46 — the node matching the question ranking below a generic hub. True, measurable, and fixed by the change. Multi-hop nDCG then fell from 63.1% to 30.1%, while the hub family scored an identical 31.6% at every α tested.

The class: a proxy metric with no causal path to the outcome Node-level hub dominance never propagated to evidence selection — the relevant spoke still entered the top 25 nodes and its proposition was still gathered. So the change had no effect on its target and a large negative effect elsewhere.

This is the hardest class to catch, because the proxy genuinely improved. Only an outcome metric could reveal it. Before optimising a proxy, ask: what is the mechanism by which this number affects the thing I care about — and is there one?

The reversal is recorded in a twenty-line comment at the constant. That comment is the artefact that matters, because it turns a fixed bug into an argument that survives the person who made it.
69
Chapter 69 · Judgement

How decisions get made here

Not a values statement. A precedence chain — an ordered list you can actually run an argument through — plus the distinction between law and preference, and the honesty disciplines that keep the rest of this book from becoming fiction.

The precedence chain

When two people disagree, walk down this list. The first rung that applies decides it.

1
An invariant (ch. 06). Not negotiable in a pull request. Changing one is its own change, with its own argument, its own tests, and an update to CLAUDE.md. "But my case is special" is how invariants die.
2
A measurement. If the harness can answer it, the harness decides — not the more senior person. Two decisions have already been reversed this way (ch. 49), both against arguments that were correct in their own terms.
3
The architectural drivers, in order (ch. 04). Isolation > failure honesty > provenance > token efficiency > latency > operational simplicity > ingest throughput. Most disagreements are two people optimising different rungs without saying which.
4
Reversibility. Prefer the decision that is cheaper to undo. Over-merging entities is irreversible; fragmenting is not — so the resolution threshold errs high (ch. 13). This rung settles more arguments than it gets credit for.
5
The reader. Code is read far more often than written, and infrastructure code is read rarely and under pressure — which makes clarity worth more there, not less.
6
Preference. Pick one, write down why, move on. Do not promote it to a rule.

Law versus preference — the distinction that matters most

Lawhas a scar

Every invariant in chapter 06 exists because something already went wrong. They are not principles someone admired; they are the shape of a fixed bug.

You earn an invariant by paying for it. That is the entry requirement, and it is what keeps the list at ~20 items instead of 200 — a list nobody can hold in their head is not enforcing anything.

Preferencehas a reason, not a scar

node:test over vitest. Directories over Terraform workspaces. Hand-rolled force layout. Each has a defensible reason and none is law.

The failure mode is promoting preference to law by repetition: a choice made once, copied twice, and defended the fourth time as "how we do things". If it has no scar, it can be revisited by anyone with an argument.

The never-list, and why each entry carries its reason

NeverBecause
Return an empty result to signal a failureA total outage becomes "this corpus contains no facts", permanently and invisibly.
Fall back to the default domain on an unknown oneReturning one field's graph to a caller who asked for another's is the exact failure this product exists to prevent.
Call fetch outside services/http.tsA hung socket permanently consumes a worker slot, with no error anywhere.
Write an edge without its sources row, in the same transactionAn unfalsifiable claim in a system whose value is traceability, with no way to identify it later.
Compare vectors without asserting equal dimensionYou get a confident number from unrelated coordinate systems.
LIMIT without ORDER BYA non-deterministic result set masquerading as a bounded one.
db:push on anything holding dataAn inferred plan nobody reviewed can drop a column it believes was renamed.
Hold queued work in process memoryThe backlog dies with the process — fatal for a batch measured in GPU-days.
Let a bookkeeping write fail the operation it is bookkeepingA logging outage becomes a service outage.
Call resolveDomain from a routeEvery isolation defect here came from a route that omitted a check; the fix was to make omission impossible.
Change a retrieval parameter without running evalTwice now, a correct-sounding argument made retrieval measurably worse.
Fold an unknown type into a known oneIt silently discarded the entire taxonomy of the first structured source that used it.

A never-list without reasons is a cargo cult. With reasons, each entry is falsifiable: if the reason stops applying, the rule goes. That is the difference between a rule you can maintain and a rule that accretes.

The honesty disciplines

1
Verify, then claim. Typecheck, tests, and a real request. State plainly what you could not verify. This book re-ran the suite while being written and corrected the README's stale numbers rather than repeating them.
2
Read the code, not the README. The README has been wrong about working behaviour before. When they disagree, the code is the truth and the README is a bug.
3
Leave the docs true. If behaviour changes, README.md and CLAUDE.md change in the same commit. Docs that are updated separately are docs that are updated never — and D24 in the defect register is the proof that this one is the hardest to hold.
4
Prefer the root fix. If a bug can recur at a sibling call site, you fixed the symptom. The chokepoint, services/http.ts, and routeError are all this rule applied.
5
Publish the gaps. Chapter 51 and the defect register are the same list you would hand a buyer. A buyer will run their own evaluation; the only question is whether you knew first.
6
Record reversals at the constant. Not in a wiki, not in a commit message — in the code, next to the value. A comment saying "0.15" teaches nothing; one saying "this was 0.5 for a good reason and here is the measurement that reversed it" is an argument that outlives its author.
The one habit that produced most of the quality in this codebase Comments explain why, and specifically why not the obvious thing.

Read any hard file here and the comments are disproportionately about roads not taken: why SHA-256 and not bcrypt, why direct fetch and not an SDK, why 0.15 and not 0.5, why re-queue and not fail, why the smallest weight that works. The code says what it does; the comment says what it is not doing and what happened when it did.

That is also why this book was possible to write. A codebase that only records what can be documented; one that records why can be taught.
70
Chapter 70 · Judgement

Product thinking: the wedge

Engineering judgement without product judgement builds excellent things nobody buys. This chapter is the other half: who this is for, why they would pay, what would make them not, and how to demo it in three minutes.

The reframe, and why it is an engineering decision

Today it says: a geometric, low-LLM knowledge field over 3D Gaussian Splatting research papers.
What it actually is: a token-efficient, domain-isolated knowledge substrate that lets an agent retrieve grounded, cited evidence without spending a model call.

The current framing undersells twice — it ties a general capability to one literature, and it describes the pipeline rather than the value. And the reframe is not cosmetic: it dictates where the seams go. The connector contract, the domain registry and the MCP surface exist because the substrate must be source-agnostic, tenant-isolated and agent-callable. A "3DGS paper tool" needs none of them.

The buyer, and the layer we are

Target: platforms connecting AI agents to fragmented enterprise software. Their architecture converges on three layers, and their binding constraint is staying token-efficient across hundreds of connected systems — precisely the constraint this retrieval core was designed against.

Platform layerWhat it requiresManifold
Connector
APIs → MCP		Inventory of systems, endpoints, capabilitiespartial The OpenAPI connector does exactly this at zero LLM cost. More adapters needed.
Knowledge
graph, suggestion, error prevention		Typed graph, cheap relevance ranking, per-vertical ontology, dependency awarenessthis is the core Graph, PPR ranking, per-domain ontologies, provenance — all built. Missing: tool-ranking mode, precondition edges, contradiction detection.
Governance
auth, permissions, monitoring		Per-principal scopes, risk-aware permissions, full auditcore shipped Tenants, principals, grants, chokepoint, audit. Missing: RLS, quotas, metering.
The assumption to validate before pitching This mapping is drawn from how such platforms describe themselves publicly, not from a conversation with one. Confirm how a specific buyer's knowledge layer is actually built. If they already have a graph and a ranking layer, the wedge is narrower and the honest pitch becomes the retrieval core as a component rather than the layer.

Writing that assumption down, rather than assuming it away, is the same discipline as labelling an unmeasured number. A go-to-market thesis is a hypothesis with a falsification condition, or it is a hope.

Five use cases that make "universal" concrete

Tool selection at scalestrongest

With hundreds of MCP servers, no agent holds every tool schema in context. Rank tools by graph proximity to the task instead of stuffing schemas. This is a retrieval problem and the system already solves it — the api-surface domain and the OpenAPI connector exist for exactly this.

Error prevention

Encode preconditions and dependencies as typed edges — this call needs that auth, this step must follow that one — and surface them before the agent acts. The edge types are open (invariant 19), so this needs no schema work.

Institutional memory

Ingest internal documents, runbooks and tickets; answer with citations to the exact source span. The provenance model already supports it; the connectors do not exist yet.

Vertical expertise & compliance

A platform's industry verticals map one-to-one onto the domain registry — isolated ontologies, no cross-contamination. And provenance on every edge plus an audit log of every retrieval produces a defensible record of what the system knew and when.

The three-minute demo, and why it is in that order

1
Ask a question in plain language. Get an answer where every sentence traces to a source passage. Establishes that it works.
2
Show the cost counter beside it. llmCalls: 1, context in characters, against what conventional retrieval would have sent. Establishes the economic claim as a number on screen rather than a slide.
3
Switch to an unrelated domain and ask a question using the same word. Show the two never mix. Establishes isolation as a demonstration rather than an assertion.
4
Attempt access with a credential scoped to the other domain — watch it return nothing. Establishes that isolation is enforced, not merely observed.

The story is: cheaper, citable, and it cannot leak between customers. Steps 3 and 4 are the pair that matters — one shows the data boundary, the other shows the authorization boundary, and a buyer who has been burned before knows those are different things.

The technical demo, depth on demand

1
Run the isolation suite live; show boundary-crossing traversal being refused.
2
Walk the retrieval path; show the zero-model-call evidence response with graph ranks and stats exposed.
3
Show the quality scorecard against baselines — including the family where keyword search beat us before fusion. Volunteering the loss is what makes the wins credible.
4
Kill the process mid-batch and restart it. The job re-queues, is reclaimed, and completes with zero duplicated edges. Durability demonstrated rather than claimed.
5
Open the audit log and reconstruct exactly what an agent asked and what it was shown.
6
Be candid about the known gaps — this document is the same list we would hand them.

The risks, stated as a buyer would

RiskHonest answer
"Extraction quality caps everything and you cannot measure it."Correct. A real run produced genuinely useful entities and run-on phrases and a relationship from the acknowledgments. gold.ts is the instrument; the score is not yet computed. It is item one on the measurement list.
"'Zero LLM calls' is a trick."Retrieval spends one embedding call, accounted separately, skippable when the caller holds the vector. The honest phrasing is "no generative call", and the claim must be stated exactly or it reads as one.
"Graph construction is expensive."Yes — the dominant cost, and the value depends on amortising it across many queries. Break-even is under five queries per document (ch. 45). A corpus ingested and queried twice should use naive RAG.
"We already have this layer."Then the pitch is the retrieval core as a component, not the layer. Worth learning before positioning.
"No conflict model."True. Two disagreeing sources become two edges with confidence scores. agents/repair.ts now detects temporal and mutual contradictions at zero LLM cost; it has no route and no metric yet.
"Can it survive a real batch?"Now, yes — and it is demonstrable rather than asserted (demo step 4). Throughput is still the GPU; what the architecture buys is survivability, not speed. Saying so is the answer that survives diligence.
71
Chapter 71 · Judgement

Recipes

Ten tasks you will actually be asked to do, each as a complete sequence. If a recipe is longer than five steps, that is a signal about the design — noted where it applies.

R1 · Add a domain

1
New file in apps/api/src/domains/ exporting a DomainConfig: id (lowercase kebab), name, description, preferred entity/relationship types, domainContext, examples, hierarchicalEdgeTypes.
2
One line in domains/index.ts's registry array.
3
pnpm --filter api test — the registry self-consistency test runs automatically.
4
Grant it to a principal: POST /api/admin/principals with domains: ["your-domain"].

No migration. No route change. Requires a code deploy — which is the limitation Phase 4 removes (ch. 29).

R2 · Add a connector

1
New file in connectors/ implementing Connector. Decide structured honestly — it is a promise about cost, not a hint.
2
Throw ConnectorInputError (→400) for bad caller input, ConnectorSourceError (→422) for an unreadable source.
3
All outbound fetches through services/http.ts with a timeout and a size cap. Add the SSRF guard (D2) while you are here — do not inherit the defect.
4
One line in connectors/index.ts.
5
If structured: true, write the test asserting every unit carries an extraction and no text (invariant 20).

R3 · Add an endpoint

1
Domain-scoped? Call requireDomain(c, raw, 'thing.action') — never resolveDomain. The grep test enforces it.
2
Mutating? requireScopeOn(c, 'write'|'admin', 'thing.action') before any work.
3
Route params: isUuid() before touching the database. Pagination through a bounded, non-NaN helper.
4
Wrap in try/catch returning routeError(c, error, '…'). Never construct a status from a service.
5
Reading entities by id? Out-of-scope must 404, not 403 (invariant 3).
6
Add the endpoint to the parameterised isolation test list in __tests__/db/isolation.test.ts.

R4 · Add a background job type

1
Add the type to JobType in queue/index.ts.
2
New handler in jobs/: (job: Job) => Promise<void>. Classify failures — plain Error for transient, PermanentJobError for dead ends. Do not decide disposition; the runner does that.
3
Make it idempotent. It will be retried. If it writes, it must recognise its own partial work (as the arXiv handler does with attempts > 1) or clear-then-write (as the process handler does).
4
Register it in jobs/index.ts. Decide the lane: does it contend for the network/CPU, or for the GPU?
5
The route inserts a row with createJob() and returns. It runs nothing. Call admit() first if it accepts bulk input.

R5 · Change a retrieval parameter

1
pnpm --filter api eval before. Record all three families, not the overall number.
2
Make the change. Prefer exposing it in RetrieveOptions so it can be swept.
3
Run again. Compare per family. An overall average hides a capability loss (ch. 48).
4
If several values measure identically, pick the smallest that buys the gain (ch. 49).
5
Record the reasoning at the constant, including what you tried and rejected.

R6 · Add a migration

1
Edit db/schema.ts, then pnpm --filter api db:generate.
2
Read the generated SQL. Confirm it does what you meant — this is the review db:push skips.
3
Destructive? Add a DO $$ … RAISE EXCEPTION $$ guard block that refuses unless the data is safe. Migration 0001 is the model.
4
Backward-compatible with running code (ch. 59 rule 2). Additive now; drop in a later deploy.
5
Touching a vector column? The HNSW index must be rebuilt, and checkVectorStorage() must still pass.
6
Apply with pnpm db:migrate. Never db:push.

R7 · Add a new LLM call site

1
Pick an operation name and map it to a role in OPERATION_ROLES — it doubles as the routing key and the metrics key.
2
Use generateCompletion or generateStructuredCompletion. Never call a provider directly.
3
Decide the failure policy: is this call's failure per-item or a deployment fact? Handle LLMUnavailableError and LLMStructuredOutputError differently.
4
Never return an empty-but-successful result (invariant 5).

R8 · Investigate "the answer is wrong"

1
GET /api/field/retrieve?q=… — zero model calls, so you see retrieval in isolation.
2
Read seeds. Wrong entities ⇒ an embedding/seeding problem.
3
Read rankedNodes. Right seeds, wrong region ⇒ an expansion or PPR problem.
4
Read evidence. Right region, wrong evidence ⇒ a fusion or MMR problem.
5
Right evidence, wrong prose ⇒ a verbalization problem — the only stage where the model is at fault.
6
Evidence missing entirely ⇒ an extraction problem. Check propositions for the paper, and papers.processing_error.

Five distinguishable failure modes with five different fixes. That is the payoff for returning seeds, ranked nodes and stats instead of just an answer.

R9 · Onboard a new corpus for a customer

1
Create the domain (R1) and deploy.
2
Create the tenant and a write-scoped principal granted only that domain.
3
Ingest: structured sources first (free), then documents. Check MAX_PENDING_JOBS against the batch size — a 100-document batch with autoProcess admits as 200 jobs.
4
Poll GET /api/ingest/batches/:id. Complete means the graph is built, not just the PDFs fetched.
5
POST /api/field/backfill scoped to the domain, then train-hyperbolic, then communities/build.
6
Issue the customer a read-only principal granted only that domain. Verify with a query to a different domain — it must 403.

Six steps, three of them requiring an engineer. That count is the argument for Phase 6's exit criterion (ch. 41).

R10 · Roll back a bad deploy

1
Re-apply the previous image_tag. Immutable SHA tags make this exact.
2
Do not roll back the schema. If migrations followed ch. 59 rule 2, the old code runs fine against the new schema — which is the entire reason for the rule.
3
Check the queue: jobs claimed by killed tasks re-queue within 60 s. Verify attempts is not climbing toward MAX_JOB_ATTEMPTS on a systemic failure.
4
If a bad build wrote bad graph data, clearPaperContributions + reprocess the affected papers. Provenance means you can identify exactly which.
72
Chapter 72 · Judgement

The review checklist

What to look for in a pull request against this system, organised by the failure it prevents. Most of these are one grep and would each have caught a real bug in this codebase's history.

IsolationP0
  • Does any new query touch a domain-stamped table without a domain predicate?
  • Does any traversal filter on edges.domain only, and not both endpoint nodes?
  • Does a new route call resolveDomain instead of requireDomain?
  • Does an entity lookup return 404, not 403, across a boundary?
  • Is a domain id persisted resolved, never as the raw request string?
Failure honestyP0
  • Does any catch return an empty-but-successful result?
  • Can a failure end in a completed status?
  • Are transport failures distinguished from content failures — and retried differently?
  • Does a swallowed error have a counter and an alarm, or is it just swallowed?
  • Does a state transition clear stale fields from the previous state?
Bounded work
  • Is any new query in the hot path proportional to corpus size?
  • Does every hot-path predicate have an index behind it?
  • Is there a LIMIT without an ORDER BY?
  • Does a new cap appear in stats so it is observable?
  • Does a fan-out have a per-step bound as well as a total?
External calls
  • Any bare fetch outside services/http.ts?
  • Any unbounded body read (arrayBuffer(), text() on an untrusted response)?
  • Is a destination URL validated against private ranges? (D2 — do not inherit it.)
  • Is a retry policy distinguishing deterministic from transient failures?
Jobs & durability
  • Is queued work held anywhere other than the jobs table?
  • Is the handler idempotent under retry? Does it recognise its own partial work?
  • Are dead ends thrown as PermanentJobError, so a 15-minute retry is not wasted?
  • Does a new job type land in the right lane for the resource it contends for?
Geometry & data
  • Are vector dimensions asserted before any comparison?
  • Is an index-paired API's positional contract checked at the boundary?
  • Is missing data coerced to a value that could win a ranking?
  • Is an edge written in the same transaction as its sources row?
  • Does a migration guard its own destructive step?

The five questions to ask about any change

1
What happens at 100× the data? Not "is it fast" — is any term proportional to corpus size.
2
What happens when this fails? Trace it to the caller. Does anyone find out? Is it distinguishable from success?
3
Can this leak across a domain? Ask it even when the answer is obviously no — it was obviously no every previous time too.
4
Is this reversible? If not, it needs a guard, a dry run, or both.
5
Would the next person understand why? If the reasoning is not in the code, the change is one refactor from being undone.
The strongest review technique in this codebase Write the test that is named after the bug, not after the function.

"leaves a live job owned by ANOTHER instance untouched" survives a rewrite of the queue. "testRecoverOrphanedJobs" does not — it gets deleted along with the function it names, taking the knowledge with it. The first is an assertion about the system; the second is an assertion about an implementation.

And when a property spans the codebase rather than a function, write the meta-test: the grep over src/routes/ protects code that has not been written yet, which no ordinary test can do.
73
Chapter 73 · Judgement

Roadmap & what happens next

Where the product has been, where it is going, and — the part that makes a roadmap a roadmap rather than a wish list — a falsifiable exit criterion for every phase.

Where it has been

PhaseStatusExit criterion, and whether it was met
0 · Foundation repairdoneFixed the provider path producing empty graphs, removed fail-open swallowing, made isolation fail closed. Met — tests green, isolation verified against live Postgres.
1 · Make retrieval realsubstantially donepgvector + HNSW, bounded working set, declared embedding space, versioned migrations. 21 s → 153 ms p50 at 100k. Exit criterion NOT met: <150 ms p95 at 1M is unproven (193 ms at 100k).
2 · Prove qualitypartially metHarness, gold corpus, three families, four strategies, sweeps, a local embedder so it runs anywhere. Still required: real corpus, real embeddings, human labels, groundedness, contradiction rate, a second domain.
2b · Hybrid retrievaldoneRRF over graph + vector + lexical. Best or tied-best on every family; hub nDCG 63.1% → 100%.
3 · Governancecore shippedTenants, principals, scoped keys, per-domain grants, one chokepoint, audit of allows and denies. Met — DB-backed tests prove a credential reads exactly its granted domains. Not done: quotas, metering, RLS.
4b · Model routingdonePer-role models, printed at startup and served from /health. The measurement inverted the intuitive allocation.
4c · Batch ingestiondoneThe jobs table is the queue; two lanes; PDF parsing off the event loop; batches; admission control; retry discipline. Met — kill-9 mid-extraction re-queues, reclaims, completes, zero duplicated edges.

Where it is going

PhaseContentsExit criterion
4 · Universal ingestionConnector SDK, more adapters (DB schema, git, MCP manifests, wikis). Domain registry moves into the database.A new vertical, with its own ontology and sources, goes live without a code deploy.
5 · Agent intelligenceTool-ranking endpoint, precondition/dependency edges, contradiction detection (wire up repair.ts), temporal "as of", incremental hyperbolic + community maintenance.On a benchmark of hundreds of tools, ranked selection beats schema-stuffing on both token cost and task success.
6 · Product surfaceOperator console: domains, ingestion monitoring, evidence inspection, audit browsing, usage and spend. Client SDKs. Streaming.A customer administrator can onboard a corpus and grant an agent scoped access without engineering help.
Why these criteria are the right shape Every one is falsifiable by someone who is not on the team. Not "improve ingestion" but "a vertical goes live without a deploy" — a thing you can attempt and fail at. Not "make it faster" but "<150 ms p95 at one million entities", which is why Phase 1 is honestly marked incomplete rather than quietly declared done.

A roadmap phase whose exit criterion you cannot fail is a phase that will be declared complete.

What this book would do in the next two weeks

1
Backups + a restore drill. Half a day. The only unrecoverable failure in the system, and the corpus is days of GPU time that cannot even be reproduced identically.
2
Fix D1 — the cross-tenant audit read. One line. A live multi-tenancy defect in the governance layer itself.
3
Dockerfile + CI. Two days. 250 tests and a clean typecheck currently run only when someone remembers, and nothing is deployable.
4
Score extraction quality against gold.ts. One day. It caps every other quality number and the instrument now exists — it tells you whether the rest of the roadmap is aimed correctly.
5
Fix D2 (SSRF) and D3 (unscoped processing list). One day. Both are real, both are small, both are the kind of thing a security review finds first.
6
Parallelise the three evidence queries. An hour. Probably the best effort-to-payoff ratio in the codebase.
7
Graceful shutdown. Half a day. Thirty lines, and the last piece of the deploy story.
8
The "Ask" console page. Three days. Converts the differentiated capability into something a non-engineer sees in ten seconds — the highest-value product work available.
9
A concurrency load test. One day. The measurement most likely to produce a surprise rather than a confirmation.

Roughly nine days of work. Note what is absent: no partitioning, no RLS, no Redis, no sharding, no streaming, no new connectors. All correct eventually; none of them the next constraint. The discipline is to fix what is broken before optimising what is not, and to measure before deciding what to optimise.

The last word The most valuable property of this codebase is not the geometry, the isolation model, or the durable queue. It is that it knows what is wrong with itself, in writing, with severities attached.

Chapter 51's unmeasured list, chapter 67's defect register, and the honestly-unmet exit criteria in this chapter are not admissions of weakness. They are the artefact that makes every other claim in this book credible — because a team that publishes its gaps is a team whose stated strengths you can believe without re-deriving them.

Build the thing. Measure it. Say what is still wrong. Then fix the most broken one.
Appendices

Reference

Every environment variable, the vocabulary, the papers this system is built on, and a set of exercises that turn reading into understanding.

A
Appendix A

Every environment variable

Grouped by what they control, with the consequence of getting each wrong — because a variable's default is a design decision and its failure mode is rarely obvious.

Core

VariableDefaultPurpose & consequence
